| Field | Value |
| --- | --- |
| **Status** | `approved` |

## Purpose

Define the Electron desktop app (main process + preload) and React + Vite renderer for AI Sidekicks — the second client delivery track after the CLI (per `container-architecture.md` §Client Delivery Sequence) — whose front end the web client and the phone apps also run. This spec specifies:

- the main-process/renderer boundary and the preload bridge capability surface
- main-process responsibilities (every window, native dialogs, notifications, deep-link handling, starting and supervising the daemon, auto-update)
- renderer composition for each V1 Signature Feature view
- code-signing, notarization, and distribution across macOS (arm64 + x64), Windows 10/11 (x64 + arm64), and Linux (x64 + arm64)
- the explicit renderer-untrusted trust stance required to keep auth material out of renderer reach

## Scope

In scope:

- Electron main-process architecture and lifecycle
- Preload bridge contract (capability surface between renderer and main)
- The one front end: the console runs in the desktop app, the web client and the phone apps through one bridge interface with three implementations, and on Remote Control's phone and browser surfaces every screen folds to one column, with one back control, when the window is too narrow for its list and its detail at once; a desktop window never folds
- Renderer composition of V1 Signature Features (the transcript, approvals, runs, the agents pane)
- The console design language (Meridian), the console surface set, the pane and window model, the budgets, and the fixtures the renderer is built and tested against (§Console Design (Meridian))
- The console's own surfaces and what each one shows, offers and refuses: the session screen — one user, any of their linked devices, one runtime node executing, over a session that lives where the repository is — with its icon rail, its all-sessions list, its session views side by side, its composer, its pane kinds, its detached panes, its settings pages, and its notification surface (§The surface set)
- Remote Control's surfaces, which ship in the first release: the Devices page in Settings, and the web client and the phone apps running this front end, built in Plan-025's frontend phase once the backend phases under them merge
- Daemon supervision from the main process (start, liveness, crash restart, the service's own update, and the accepted version range)
- Auto-update flow and signature verification
- Code-signing and notarization for macOS, Windows and Linux
- Deep-link handling for `sidekicks://` URLs
- Security hardening posture (contextIsolation, sandbox, Electron Fuses, CSP, no debug port on either host)
- Crash reporting for main, renderer, and supervised-daemon process crashes, every report kept on the machine that crashed
- Accessibility baseline (OS-level screen-reader and high-contrast compliance; WCAG 2.2 AA for every console route and dialog per §Console Design (Meridian))

## Non-Goals

- Pixel-exact measurements. `§Console Design (Meridian)` fixes the design language, the surface set, the pane and window model, the budgets, and — through `§Console Libraries` — the component-library and theme decisions, and its surface set fixes what each surface shows, offers and refuses. What it does not fix is a figure in pixels: every width, floor, ceiling and gap in the console is derived from what it holds at the current text size, and a pixel figure quoted for one of them anywhere in this spec is an illustration at the default text size rather than a requirement. The few measurements the design language fixes outright — the icon grid and its stroke, the radius scale, the hairline, and the rail's accent spine — are the drawing's own weight rather than a measure of anything the console holds, so each is a token of its own in `§Console Design (Meridian)` and derives from nothing. A surface's own density budget, its fixture scenarios and the code that draws it are Plan-020's console registration.
- Multi-person surfaces. There is one user. A read-only spectator mode, a side pane of people, a people-only chat, request-to-drive controls, hand-over and take-back, approval routing to a named person, and turn ownership are all out; `Interrupt` is available on every linked device instead.
- The session event log as a feature. It is substrate: it powers crash recovery and the transcript projection, and it is never named on screen and never rendered as a record of everything that happened.
- A replay surface. No scrubber and no speed presets; scrollback is scrollback. The one reading aid beside the scrollbar is the ask rail at the transcript's right edge, hidden until the pointer rests there.
- An overlay the design has not named. A modal or pop-up over the transcript exists only where this spec names one — the plan reader, the command palette, the find box, the quick look over a picture or a document, and the box that searches every session — and each is named there rather than admitted as a pattern.
- A surface that ships a dead control. Absent, not disabled, is the standing rule, with the named exceptions §Rules every console surface obeys lists; each of those is grayed in place with its reason in its own hover title.
- A surface that re-derives daemon truth. The console renders what the daemon reports.
- A second settings search. Settings' pages sit in one fixed order, with `Devices` last, after Runtime; the one field above the page list finds the controls on every page, and the Keyboard page's own box, which filters that page's rows, is the only other search on the screen.
- A model-visibility control, and a provider-wide list of tools held off. The model control lists both providers' whole catalogs under the two headings `Claude Code` and `Codex`, each read per provider and refreshed when it goes stale so a model that has left it is never offered; an agent's own tool allowlist carries the provider's built-in tools beside the tool servers, and a standing per-provider disabled set would be a second precedence layer over the allowlist.
- Theme authoring. The app ships two themes, `Meridian`, the default, and `Graphite`; no other theme is written, imported or loaded from a file, and no control anywhere edits a color.
- A log viewer. No surface reads the event log back as a list — no recent-activity fold, no credential-attempt list and no audit view anywhere; the diagnostics controls say what is written and where it is written, and nothing displays what is in it.
- Network configuration. Runtime carries no local-network switch, no private-network join, no coordination-server field, no approval link and no address to copy; the only thing it says about reachability is whether the background service is running, and the only addresses the console sets are the workflows listener's port and the ports shared on the Devices page.
- Archived sessions as a settings page. They stay in the sessions list as their own group, the way archived projects sit at the foot of the Projects page. Each of these absences is absent rather than disabled — nothing refuses them, because nothing offers them.
- Daemon internals (owned by `daemon.md` and Spec-006)
- Control-plane authentication protocol details (owned by [Spec-027](./027-remote-control.md) and ADR-010)
- A browser client of the local daemon. The web client and the phone apps run this front end and reach a machine only over the relay ([Spec-027](./027-remote-control.md)); no browser connects to a daemon's own socket, and the desktop app and the CLI are its only local clients
- The CLI client (Spec-006 owns the IPC contract the renderer reuses; CLI-specific UX is out of scope here)
- Provider-driver internal protocols (owned by Spec-004)
- Workflow authoring semantics — the engine, the definition model, the human-phase forms — owned by [Spec-015](./015-workflow-authoring-and-execution.md) / Plan-014 under [ADR-014](../decisions/014-v1-feature-scope-definition.md), with the node-graph builder under [ADR-024](../decisions/024-visual-node-graph-workflow-authoring.md); the renderer composes those surfaces as the Workflows destination's tabs and its run address ([§Pane kinds](#pane-kinds)), and this spec restates none of their behavior.

## Required Behavior

### Process Model

The desktop application must run as three cooperating processes:

1. **Main process (Electron main process).** Node.js runtime. Owns every window and its registry of windows, native dialogs, operating-system notifications, the deep-link protocol handler, starting and supervising the local daemon, the auto-updater, and the daemon session token it presents on its own connection to the daemon (§Trust Stance). It keeps no entry in the operating system's keystore and holds no control-plane credential: this machine's DPoP key, the hosted account's tokens and every other secret are the daemon's, never main's.
2. **Renderer (Electron renderer process).** One renderer drives every window of the app. Chromium with `contextIsolation: true` and `sandbox: true`. Loads the React + Vite bundle. Has no direct Node.js access and no direct filesystem, network, or OS access — all such capabilities flow through the preload bridge.
3. **Local Daemon (the person's own background service).** Runs `packages/runtime-daemon/`. It is never a child of the desktop app: main spawns it detached ([§Detached Service Launch](#detached-service-launch)), which on macOS and Linux survives a quit of the app and a SIGTERM or SIGKILL sent to the app (on Linux a logout ends it unless lingering is on), and where a detached process does not outlive the app it runs as the per-user service `sidekicks daemon install` sets up. On a Windows computer whose Claude Code and Codex live in WSL 2, a per-user logon task starts the service's Windows half (`sidekicks-windows-half.exe`), which starts the daemon inside the distribution with `wsl.exe --exec`. Main reaches the daemon through its socket — on Windows the per-user named pipe `\\.\pipe\ai-sidekicks-<user SID>` — handshakes with `daemon.hello`, checks the link with `daemon.ping`, and calls `daemon.flush` before a quit, which leaves the daemon running. The daemon owns all execution authority.

The renderer must never fork, spawn, or exec a process. The renderer must never open a filesystem handle or a network socket directly — every such operation flows through the preload bridge and is enforced in the main process.

### Trust Stance

The renderer is **untrusted** relative to the main process and the daemon, consistent with `container-architecture.md` §Trust Boundaries and `desktop.md` §Trust Boundaries.

The main process holds one secret, and the renderer never holds it:

- the local daemon session token, read from its file at every connect and never once at app start, because the daemon makes a new token at every restart: `$XDG_RUNTIME_DIR/ai-sidekicks/daemon.token` on macOS and Linux and `%LOCALAPPDATA%\ai-sidekicks\run\daemon.token` on Windows, per Security Architecture §Local Daemon Authentication

This machine's DPoP key, the hosted account's access and refresh tokens and every other secret the daemon keeps are the daemon's, and reach neither the main process nor the renderer.

All renderer-originated daemon or control-plane requests flow through the preload bridge, which:

1. validates the request against a narrow capability-typed contract brokered to the renderer at session start
2. attaches the session token in the main process
3. forwards the request to the daemon over the Spec-006 Content-Length JSON-RPC transport; a control-plane request takes the same path, and the daemon sends it to the control plane over the ADR-013 tRPC / WebSocket transport under this machine's access token and DPoP proof
4. returns only the sanitized response payload to the renderer — never the raw auth headers

`security-architecture.md` §Local Daemon Authentication carries the same stance: the renderer is **not a direct daemon client**; all renderer-originated requests flow through the preload bridge to the desktop app's main process, which forwards them to the daemon with attached auth headers. The main process and the CLI both present the 256-bit session token at daemon-connect time — the token is primary and socket permissions 0700 are defense-in-depth — and neither connects without it.

### Security Hardening Baseline

Every window of the app is a `BaseWindow`, and every document it shows — the renderer in each window, a pane's own window included, and every page the `browser` pane hosts — is a `WebContentsView` added to that window's content view. A `BrowserWindow` is not used anywhere, because it owns a never-navigated `webContents` of its own that appears in the debugger's target list as a mute page target: a Playwright client attaching to the app auto-attaches to that target and then blocks on it forever, so `connectOverCDP` against a `BrowserWindow` times out at thirty seconds, while against a `BaseWindow` it completes in 37 to 48 milliseconds and the process count is four rather than five (measured on Electron 44.5.1, Chromium 152, macOS arm64). The agent's browser tools reach the Preview pages through the daemon's relay over Electron's in-process debugger, which main attaches to each Preview view and to no other `webContents`, so the window shape is a hardening and a correctness requirement at once ([ADR-034](../decisions/034-embedded-browser-for-preview.md)). No debug port is opened on either host: the app is never started with `--remote-debugging-port` or `--remote-debugging-pipe`, and the daemon reaches its headless browser over Playwright's own launch pipe.

Every view main creates for a console document must be created with the following `webPreferences`. A further window's content — a window of session views, or a pane's own window — is the renderer's own `window.open` child, which main adopts in a `WebContentsView` and which keeps the renderer's sandbox, isolation and content security policy and carries no preload. Each Preview page's view is created in the `persist:preview` partition with `paintWhenInitiallyHidden: true`, and its background throttling left at the default:

```ts
{
  contextIsolation: true,          // must be true; isolates preload from renderer
  sandbox: true,                   // must be true; renderer runs in OS-level sandbox
  nodeIntegration: false,          // must be false; no Node APIs in renderer
  nodeIntegrationInWorker: false,  // must be false
  webSecurity: true,               // must be true; enforces same-origin
  preload: '<absolute path>',      // preload script registered here
  // no remoteModule (removed in Electron >= 14)
}
```

Every renderer document must be served with a strict `Content-Security-Policy`. At minimum:

```
default-src 'self';
script-src 'self';
style-src 'self' 'unsafe-inline';
connect-src 'self' https://<configured-control-plane-origin> wss://<configured-relay-origin>;
img-src 'self' data: blob:;
font-src 'self';
frame-src 'none';
object-src 'none';
base-uri 'none';
form-action 'none';
```

Electron Fuses — the app binary must be packaged with the following fuses (see [Implementation Notes §Electron Fuses](#electron-fuses) for rationale and primary-source citations):

- `RunAsNode`: **disabled** — prevents the shipped Electron binary from running as a generic Node.js runtime via `ELECTRON_RUN_AS_NODE`
- `EnableCookieEncryption`: **enabled** — encrypts the cookie store at rest using OS-level primitives
- `EnableNodeOptionsEnvironmentVariable`: **disabled** — prevents `NODE_OPTIONS` / `NODE_EXTRA_CA_CERTS` from injecting code at startup
- `EnableNodeCliInspectArguments`: **disabled** — prevents `--inspect` and `--inspect-brk` debugger flags at the Electron command line
- `EnableEmbeddedAsarIntegrityValidation`: **enabled** — verifies asar-bundle integrity at load time (stable since Electron 39, and on Linux through digest mode)
- `OnlyLoadAppFromAsar`: **enabled** — refuses to load the app from anywhere other than the signed asar
- `LoadBrowserProcessSpecificV8Snapshot`: **enabled** — prevents renderer V8 snapshots from leaking into the browser process
- `GrantFileProtocolExtraPrivileges`: **disabled** — refuses to grant privileged APIs to `file://` origins; the renderer is served via a custom protocol, not `file://`
- `WasmTrapHandlers`: **enabled** (default) — required for WASM memory safety without perf cost

In addition to the Fuses above, release builds must embed an **ASAR Integrity Digest** (new in Electron 41 via `@electron/asar` v4.1.0+, invoked as `asar integrity-digest on /path/to/YourApp.app`). Digest generation must run **before** the code-signing step because toggling fuses and embedding the digest both invalidate the signature.

### Preload Bridge Contract

The preload script exposes a single typed object on `window.desktopBridge` via `contextBridge.exposeInMainWorld`. The object surface must be declarative, narrow, and capability-scoped. The one other global the preload exposes is a fixture launch, on a page global of its own and only in a fixture-capable build (§Fixtures); it is never a member of this object.

**One bridge for every window.** One renderer drives every window, so the bridge is exposed once, to that renderer. A further window of session views and a side pane's own window are the renderer's `window.open` children, which carry no preload and no bridge of their own: nothing crosses the bridge for a view or for a detached pane ([§Detached panes](#detached-panes)).

**One interface, three hosts.** The same front end runs in the desktop app, the web client and the phone apps, and the bridge is one typed interface, `PlatformBridge`, the front end's host-agnostic capability boundary, declared in the renderer's `services/platform/` with `PlatformBridgeProvider`, through which the renderer receives it. The provider hands down, beside the bridge, the clock the window runs on — real time for a window that reads the preload, and a fixture composition's own clock for a window that composition builds — so neither the clock nor a fixture's scenario engine is a member of any bridge. No bridge carries an attention member either: the rail's count and the notification surface read the attention projection through `daemon`, as `attention.projectionRead`, the whole projection and then every change. It has three implementations: the desktop's, which alone reads the Electron preload's `window.desktopBridge`; the browser's in the web client; and Capacitor's in the phone apps. The other hosts expose nothing on `window`. The preload's object is the desktop's implementation detail, typed by the desktop's preload API type, `PreloadApi` (`apps/desktop/src/shared/preload-api.ts`), and the daemon method map both types import (`DaemonMethod`, `DaemonParams`, `DaemonResult`, `DaemonEvent`) is in `packages/contracts`. A member a host cannot serve is absent from that host's bridge, so its control is absent from the screen, never drawn and refused; the web client and the phone apps reach a machine over the relay ([Spec-027](./027-remote-control.md)).

```ts
// a Preview pane, named by its session; main maps it to that session's open page
type PaneRef = { sessionId: SessionId };

// where the pane's page sits, in the content coordinates of the window that holds the pane
type PaneRect = {
  x: number;
  y: number;
  width: number;
  height: number;
  clip: { x: number; y: number; width: number; height: number }; // the part left visible by everything that clips the pane
  visible: boolean; // false while an overlay covers the page or a tool holds it frozen
  pointerPassThrough: boolean; // true while a pane edge is under the pointer, so a drag never sticks on the page
};

interface PlatformBridge {
  // daemon RPC over the Spec-006 JSON-RPC contract, and the supervisor main runs beside it
  daemon: {
    // one daemon method, forwarded by main with the session token attached
    call<M extends DaemonMethod>(method: M, params: DaemonParams<M>): Promise<DaemonResult<M>>;
    // one daemon subscription; the `daemon.status` topic carries the connection state and the version range
    subscribe<E extends DaemonEvent>(
      event: E,
      handler: (payload: DaemonEventPayload<E>) => void,
    ): Unsubscribe;
    // start the service: the boot card's and the working line's `Retry`
    requestStart(): Promise<void>;
    // update the service; it waits for running work to finish and never stops it (Spec-006)
    requestUpdate(): Promise<void>;
    // cancel the service update, honored until main asks the old service to stop (Spec-006)
    cancelUpdate(): Promise<void>;
    // the service update's steps, the first delivery the current state (Spec-006)
    subscribeUpdate(handler: (update: ServiceUpdateProgress) => void): Unsubscribe;
    // `Restore…`: main stops the service, puts the backup's data in place and starts it again
    requestRestore(backupId: string): Promise<void>;
    // on a Windows computer with WSL 2, the places Claude Code and Codex can run from (Spec-006)
    listPlaces(): Promise<ServicePlace[]>;
    // each place's reading as it lands, the first delivery the list as it stands (Spec-006)
    subscribePlaces(handler: (places: ServicePlace[]) => void): Unsubscribe;
    // move the service to another place with its move set (Spec-006)
    requestMove(place: ServicePlace["place"]): Promise<void>;
    // cancel the move, honored until the old service is asked to stop (Spec-006)
    cancelMove(): Promise<void>;
    // the move's steps, then `moved` or `failed` (Spec-006)
    subscribeMove(handler: (move: ServiceMoveState) => void): Unsubscribe;
  };

  // control-plane RPC — request/response over tRPC, carried by the background service; the control plane pushes no presence: a device's connected state comes from `device.list`, and a machine's presence is read over its channel (`presence.read`, `presence.subscribe`); relay traffic rides the relay's binary wire frames. The control plane holds no session: another device reaches a session only through its machine over the relay ([Spec-027](./027-remote-control.md)), and this app reaches another machine's sessions the same way, through `daemon` on the machine in view. The `PlatformBridge` type matches this block, the desktop's preload API type carries the same namespaces, and a namespace joins them only where a spec section names its owner
  controlPlane: {
    // one control-plane procedure, forwarded by main to the background service, which sends it under this machine's credentials
    call<P extends CpProcedure>(procedure: P, input: CpInput<P>): Promise<CpOutput<P>>;
  };

  // native capabilities — renderer requests, main performs, sanitized result returned
  native: {
    // the platform's chooser for one of its purposes (attach files, pick a folder, import one file); returns tokens, never a path
    showOpenDialog(options: OpenDialogOptions): Promise<OpenDialogResult>;
    // the platform's save chooser for an export's destination; returns a token
    showSaveDialog(options: SaveDialogOptions): Promise<SaveDialogResult>;
    // a file dropped on the composer: the preload reads its path and hands it to main, which mints the token
    getDroppedFileRef(file: File): Promise<FilePathRef>;
    // a pasted picture: main writes the bytes to a temporary file and mints its token
    savePastedImage(bytes: ArrayBuffer): Promise<FilePathRef>;
    // an outside address in the system browser, checked against main's allowlist
    openExternal(url: string): Promise<void>;
    // the working folder, or a file at a line, in the editor named in Settings
    openInEditor(ref: FilePathRef, line?: number): Promise<void>;
    // the session's own folder in the platform's own terminal app
    openInTerminal(request: { sessionId: SessionId }): Promise<void>;
    // the editors installed on this machine, found through the system's app registry
    listEditors(): Promise<InstalledEditor[]>;
    // the operating system's notification permission for this app
    getNotificationPermission(): Promise<NotificationPermissionState>;
    // text onto the system clipboard
    copyToClipboard(text: string): Promise<void>;
    // a file or folder shown in the platform's file manager
    revealInFileExplorer(path: FilePathRef): Promise<void>;
  };

  // the `browser` pane's native page hosts — the renderer measures, main performs (ADR-034); the renderer owns no page
  browser: {
    // the rectangle the pane's page is positioned to; null hides the page
    publishPaneRect(pane: PaneRef, rect: PaneRect | null): void;
    // an editing or focus act on the page (cut, copy, paste, select all, copy link, focus)
    act(pane: PaneRef, act: BrowserPaneAct): Promise<void>;
    // the page as a picture for marking, its scale read from the image
    capturePage(pane: PaneRef): Promise<CapturedPageImage>;
    // page-host events for the pane, `downloadOffered` and `downloadRefused` among them
    subscribe(pane: PaneRef, handler: (event: BrowserPaneEvent) => void): Unsubscribe;
    // on this machine's own desktop window, the system save dialog for the download the pane's page
    // offered; main writes the file where the person chose, and a canceled dialog writes nothing
    saveDownload(pane: PaneRef): Promise<void>;
    // the bound chords the console keeps while focus is in the page, replacing the last set
    publishPageChords(chords: ChordDescriptor[]): void;
    // a kept chord pressed while the page has focus, handed back by main
    subscribePageChords(handler: (chord: ChordDescriptor) => void): Unsubscribe;
  };

  // the window the renderer draws in — its appearance, its size, fullscreen and the requests main routes to it
  window: {
    // the appearance chosen: main mirrors the scheme into the platform and the View menu, keeps the record and paints first frames from `grounds`
    setAppearance(
      appearance: Pick<AppearanceRecord, "theme" | "scheme" | "textSize" | "transcriptWidth">,
      grounds: { light: string; dark: string },
    ): Promise<void>;
    // the appearance record, to every open console window, the one that made the change included
    subscribeAppearance(handler: (record: AppearanceRecord) => void): Unsubscribe;
    // fullscreen starting or ending, the first delivery the current state
    subscribeFullscreen(handler: (fullscreen: boolean) => void): Unsubscribe;
    // the window's minimum size, summed from token sizes and set again when the text size changes
    setMinimumSize(size: { width: number; height: number }): Promise<void>;
    // a `sidekicks://` address or a notification click, which main hands over for the renderer to route
    subscribeToNavigationRequest(handler: (request: NavigationRequest) => void): Unsubscribe;
  };

  // the machine's settings file, which the background service alone writes
  machineSettings: {
    // the service's live read of the file
    read(): Promise<MachineSettings>;
    // one change, handed to the service's `daemon.machineSettingsUpdate`; answered with the file as written
    write(change: MachineSettingsChange): Promise<MachineSettings>;
    // each written change to every window, the first delivery the current file
    subscribe(handler: (settings: MachineSettings) => void): Unsubscribe;
  };

  // the keyboard map, main's own owner-only file of the overridden rows; no feed, since one renderer holds it
  keyboardMap: {
    // the overridden rows as stored
    read(): Promise<KeyboardMap>;
    // the whole map, written atomically; answered with the map as stored
    write(map: KeyboardMap): Promise<KeyboardMap>;
  };

  // auto-update — renderer observes state; main process drives
  update: {
    // the app update's state, with the found version, its notes, `staged` and whether this install can update itself
    getState(): Promise<UpdateState>;
    // each change to that state
    subscribe(handler: (state: UpdateState) => void): Unsubscribe;
    // check the feed at once
    requestCheck(): Promise<void>;
    // download the update found
    requestDownload(): Promise<void>;
    // apply on relaunch, asking nothing: the service, every run and every shell keep running
    requestRestart(): Promise<void>;
  };

  // app meta — read-only
  app: {
    version: string; // the running app's version
    platform: "darwin" | "linux" | "win32"; // the operating system
    arch: "arm64" | "x64"; // the processor architecture
    locale: string; // the system locale
  };
}
```

**What each region of the console reads.** Every fact on a console surface arrives through `daemon.call` or `daemon.subscribe` and is rendered as received. Each session view reads the session's identity, shape (project or chat), project, worktree and base, status, elapsed, ahead count, snapshot count, whether its notifications are muted, pending worktree move, the unsent draft and its staged files, the files each user turn carried, and the spend rows of the session cost receipt's per-account axis. The transcript reads the user's turns, the agent's prose, its reasoning, and the state-changing rows the console itself appends, and per tool run its verb, target, live elapsed or final duration, result summary, diff hunks, failure mark and held mark. The agents pane reads each child's identity, parent, model, the agent that was asked where it was a peer call, state, activity, tools, tokens, spend, timer and transcript rows. The approvals card reads the one pending request with its title, summary, raising child and its answers. The worktree switcher reads the per-project list with its ahead, dirty and occupancy figures, the root branch and setup progress. The `diff` pane reads the scope tabs, the base list, the files with their hunks, the commits, the pull-request state, its checks and threads, the held notes and the staleness signal. The `terminal` pane reads the session's shell output and each shell's control lease. The working line reads the running commands of the session — identity, command text, folder, start, the tool row each belongs to, output as it prints, and the end with its result and duration — the tokens received this turn, and the turn's task list. The `browser` pane reads the open page's address, title and whether its history has anywhere to go, the discovered development servers with their port and framework where one is known, which machine the page runs on, and the staged marks. The rail's attention count and the notification surface read one attention projection, and the main process reads the same projection for the count on the app icon and the operating-system notifications it posts.

**Where the embedded browser's calls go.** The session's page verbs are daemon methods under `preview.*`, reached through `daemon.call` and `daemon.subscribe` like any other: the open pages, live, each with its address, title, favicon, load state, history depth, order, zoom and whether it is released (`preview.pageList`); open one (`preview.pageOpen`), close one (`preview.pageClose`), make one the open one (`preview.pageActivate`) and move one to a new place in the strip (`preview.pageReorder`); navigate, which also serves back, forward and reload (`preview.navigate`); set a page's zoom factor, which main applies with `setZoomFactor` (`preview.zoom`); subscribe to the discovered development servers (`preview.devServerList`); send the marks attachment (`preview.marksSend`); and subscribe to the screencast, which is another device's path only (`preview.screencastSubscribe`). So are the machine-wide site-data verbs under `browser.*`, which are what the Browser settings page reads and writes: list the sites with saved data, each row saying whether it holds an unexpired cookie (`browser.siteDataList`, `hasCookies`); open one site in a page that belongs to no session, so the person signs in to it (`browser.siteSignIn {origin}`); clear one site's cookies (`browser.siteCookiesClear {origin}`); forget one site (`browser.siteDataForget`); clear all of it (`browser.siteDataClear`); and read which Chromium the daemon uses and fetch it again (`browser.chromiumRead`, `browser.chromiumFetch`). The two browser switches, `Remember site data` and `Browser tools for sidekicks`, are not a verb: they live in the machine's settings file, which the daemon reads each time it launches the headless browser or a provider. The daemon reaches the desktop's page host over main's own connection to it, never through this bridge: main reports each page view (`preview.pageTargetReport {pageId, targetId}`) and carries the debugger's traffic both ways (`preview.pageDebuggerSend`, `preview.pageDebuggerReport`); it reads and writes cookies when one host takes over from the other, and never continuously (`preview.pageCookiesRead`, `preview.pageCookiesWrite`): Playwright's `storageState` cookies are mapped onto Electron's `CookiesSetDetails` (`expires` to `expirationDate`), local storage and IndexedDB need scripted replay, and passkeys do not cross and are done again on each host, which the console says rather than silently dropping a login; it clears one site's cookies (`preview.pageCookiesClear`) and one site's or every site's data (`preview.pageSiteDataClear {origins?}`, no origins meaning all), answering each clear with `ses.clearData` per registrable domain; and it turns a popup a page opens into a page through `preview.pageOpen`, never a window. The `browser` bridge namespace above is a different surface with a different owner: it is main-process work on this machine's own pages, and it carries no session state. The agent's own browser tools are on neither surface — they are the tool server the daemon hosts, `playwright-core`'s `tools.createConnection` with one connection per session, which the provider process reaches over loopback HTTP through the session's one tool-server entry, and the renderer learns of a browser act exactly as it learns of any tool call, as a row in the transcript.

**Addresses handed outward.** Every address the console hands outward goes through `native.openExternal`, which main checks against its allowlist, except a loopback address printed in a tool row or a shell, which opens in the `browser` pane instead, and one in a reply pressed with the platform modifier, which opens there too while a plain press opens the machine's own browser; an address main refuses is refused in words on the surface that asked, never silently dropped.

The bridge must not expose:

- raw `ipcRenderer` or `ipcMain`
- `require`, `process`, `global`, or any Node built-in
- auth material (daemon session token, PASETO tokens — including the relay `connectionToken`, a PASETO v4.public `aud=relay-connect` credential — DPoP key) in any form — every control-plane token and key, the relay `connectionToken` among them, is the background service's and never reaches main (relay negotiation runs in the service — see §Interfaces And Contracts), so none can cross the bridge
- arbitrary file paths as strings — path-as-capability never crosses the bridge as a raw string, in either direction. Every native or file-system operation the renderer requests takes or returns opaque `FilePathRef` tokens: the platform's choosers (`native.showOpenDialog`, `native.showSaveDialog`) return tokens; a file dropped on the composer reaches main as a path the preload reads with `webUtils.getPathForFile` and comes back as a token (`native.getDroppedFileRef`); and a pasted picture is written to a temporary file by main and comes back as a token (`native.savePastedImage`). Main's relay turns a token into the path a daemon verb needs, and mints a token for each path a daemon reply offers to open, which `native.openInEditor` and `native.revealInFileExplorer` then take; tokens live only in main's memory, and dereferencing one is a main-process step. A daemon verb that acts on a picked file takes its token — agent and workflow import and export are daemon verbs that take the picked file — and `session.attachmentAdd` takes only a token: main's relay refuses a raw path string on it. On a Windows computer whose service runs in a WSL 2 distribution, a token dereferences to the Windows form of the path the daemon supplies, and main never builds a Linux path. On such a computer a file dropped on the composer is staged to the daemon as bytes: main reads the dropped file and hands the service its contents, so no path crosses ([Spec-006 §The service on a Windows computer](./006-local-ipc-and-daemon-control.md#the-service-on-a-windows-computer)). Display-only path VALUES carried inside daemon- or control-plane-owned payload data (e.g. Spec-007's `canonicalRoot` / `fsRoot` repo-and-workspace fields, which the repo-attach renderer renders verbatim so a user can verify what was attached) are data, not capabilities: the renderer may render them as text, but no bridge surface accepts a raw path string for any OS or filesystem action. A path a person types into a field (Spec-007's user-entered `RepoAttachRequest.localPath`, the path field of `Convert to project…`) travels as data for the same reason; the daemon's resolution and bind-time checks ([Spec-007 §Required Behavior](./007-repo-attachment-and-workspace-binding.md#required-behavior)) are the enforcement point, never the renderer. On another device, `Open folder…` lists the machine's folders in place through `repo.folderList`, each entry carrying its path, and `repo.attach` takes the path of the folder the device was shown; the platform's own chooser stays on the machine itself.

> **Parameterized daemon subscriptions.** `daemon.subscribe` must carry each subscription's request parameters; the event-and-handler signature above has no place for them. The transcript stream is the concrete case: `session.subscribe`, the session's one stream, takes the session's id and `afterCursor`, the last position the window kept, and the daemon validates that request before it replays and then tails; the run streams (`run.subscribeState`, and `run.subscribeQueue`, which also takes a child's handle for that child's own queue) carry their session's id in the same way. The signature's shape — a positional parameter, an options bag, or an event-to-parameters-and-payload map — is set by [Plan-005: Local IPC And Daemon Control](../plans/005-local-ipc-and-daemon-control.md) when it narrows `DaemonEvent`, `DaemonParams` and `DaemonEventPayload` across every daemon method at once, rather than here from one subscription alone.

### Main Process Responsibilities

- **App lifecycle.** Single-instance lock (`app.requestSingleInstanceLock()`), keyed to the install so a development build and a shipped build never collide; launching the console again brings the window used last to the front rather than opening a second copy. On `before-quit` main calls `daemon.flush`, and the daemon keeps running after it on every platform, whoever started it: a quit ends no session, run or shell, so a server started in a terminal tab is still serving after a relaunch, and only Settings › Runtime's `Stop` and `Restart` end work in flight. Relaunch on update apply, asking nothing, because that relaunch is a quit. Closing the last window — whichever window closes last, a pane's own window included — quits the app on Windows and Linux and leaves it running on macOS, where the menu bar and the menu-bar icon stay behind and a click on the dock icon opens a window on the session used last, against the same warm daemon; `⌘Q` quits there. No banner, tray string or console surface states any of this.
- **Window management.** Every window of the app is one kind — the rail, the sessions list and one or more session views side by side — and no window is the main one. Each is a `BaseWindow` hosting its content as a `WebContentsView` per §Security Hardening Baseline, and main keeps every one in its registry of windows with their focus order, so it always knows the window used last ([ADR-023](../decisions/023-electron-main-process-window-retention.md)). Main builds the window used last at start. One renderer drives every window and opens each further one — a window of session views, or a side pane's own window ([§Detached panes](#detached-panes)) — with `window.open`, naming it by its window id as the frame name. Main's window-open handler, set with `setWindowOpenHandler`, answers `{action: 'allow', createWindow}`: `createWindow` builds main's own `BaseWindow` from main's own options and that window's kept place, never from the handler's `options`, whose `show` and size come from the renderer, adopts the handed `webContents` in a `WebContentsView` and returns that same `webContents`, as Electron requires; every other `window.open` stays denied. Main pairs the two closes both ways — the window's `closed` closes the child `webContents`, and the child's `destroyed` closes the window — because closing either alone leaves the other alive. The child runs in the renderer's own process with the renderer's sandbox, isolation and content security policy, no preload and no bridge of its own, and is never in Preview's relay target list. A renderer crash takes every window with it: main closes them, and the reloaded renderer reopens every window from its kept layout.
- **Window places and restore.** Each window's place — its position, size, display, and maximized or fullscreen state — lives in main's own file, one entry per window keyed by the window id the renderer's kept layout shares, and is put back before the window is built; a rectangle off every attached display is pulled back on screen. The renderer keeps each window's views, their order and widths and which one had focus in its own storage. Every window open at quit comes back where it was, with its views and every pane's own window, and only those, so a restart never multiplies windows ([§Persistence on the renderer scheme](#persistence-on-the-renderer-scheme)).
- **One place per session.** A session shows in one view on this device at a time, and each destination in one window; asking for either again, by any route, brings its place forward. Windows add no project-level limit and neither loosen nor add a daemon execution rule: sessions of one project run and show at once, in one window or several, and the only thing a window refuses is a second view of the same session. The daemon alone holds sessions, agents, turns, worktrees, execution, provider processes, shells and workflow runs; a window, a view and the kept layout are presentation, and closing a view or a window stops nothing. The seam runs one way: the sessions feature asks for a view or a window through the renderer's window client, a service over the `window.*` contract the preload exposes, and main's window owner builds, focuses and places the native windows; the sessions feature never touches a native window and holds no window lifecycle.
- **Window size, menus and title.** A window opens centered at a size taken from the display's own work area and refuses to shrink below a floor summed from the parts that must fit at once — the rail, the sessions track and one session view's floor, which is the conversation's own floor and one pane's floor — so a window holding several views scrolls them sideways rather than growing its floor, beside the vertical floor the header, the transcript and the composer still stack in; a pane's own window opens at that pane's default width and the display's work-area height and refuses to shrink below the pane's own floor. The renderer hands each floor to main through `window.setMinimumSize` and again whenever the text size changes, so neither is a written figure. Platform-appropriate menu bar, one static template with the platform's own roles: an Edit menu whose undo, redo, cut, copy, paste and select-all reach the focused field through the system's own chain, a File menu carrying `New window`, a View menu carrying the color-scheme choices with the one in force ticked, and on macOS a Window menu with the platform's own verbs listing every open window by its title, beside the menu-bar icon, a `Tray`, that stays when the last window closes. On macOS the window draws no system title strip — the console fills it, and the rail opens with an inset above its first destination as deep as the three window buttons and the platform's own offset need, absent in fullscreen, which the renderer follows through `window.subscribeFullscreen`; on Windows and Linux the system strip stays with its own buttons and the rail has no inset. The rail and each session header's empty space are the window's drag regions, with every control inside them cut out of the drag region so pressing one presses it rather than dragging or zooming the window, and a view's title drags the view into another window or onto the desktop; a pane's own window takes its pane header as its drag region, with the macOS window buttons in that header's inset. Fullscreen is the platform's own — its window button, its double-click on the drag region, its menu row — and the console binds no chord to it. A window is titled after its focused view's session, `<session title> — <project>` or `<session title> — Chat`, and a pane's own window `<Pane> — <session title>`: the renderer sets each window document's title and main mirrors it onto its `BaseWindow`, so it is what the Window menu lists and what Mission Control and the Windows taskbar show; on macOS it is set but not drawn.
- **Native dialog surface.** The platform's own file and folder choosers, reached through `native.showOpenDialog` and `native.showSaveDialog`, which return tokens (§Preload Bridge Contract). Main posts every operating-system notification itself from the attention projection, so no renderer request posts one, and the console raises no system message box. On a Windows computer whose service runs in a WSL 2 distribution, the folder chooser opens at `\\wsl.localhost\<distro>\home\<user>` and the pick goes to the daemon, which maps it; a pick in another distribution is refused in place with `The background service cannot reach this folder. Pick one under your home folder or on a drive.` There, reveal takes the Windows form of the path the daemon supplies and calls `showItemInFolder`; a Linux name with `:` or another character Windows cannot hold appears remapped from Windows, so reveal then selects the parent folder, and an editor opened in its WSL form avoids the problem. There `Open in editor` launches the editor's Windows command in its WSL form — VS Code as `code --remote wsl+<distro> <linux path>`, Zed with `--wsl <user>@<distro>`, every other editor at the `\\wsl.localhost\` path — while `native.listEditors()` stays main's.
- **Notifications.** Cross-platform via Electron's `Notification`. Must honor OS Do-Not-Disturb. Main posts the operating-system notification for a `pending` entry of the attention projection, only while no console window is focused, whichever console screen is showing, and a workflow's Notify step posts either way; it says the thing's name and its state and never what was said. Four kinds reach a banner — `Waiting on you`, `Finished`, `Failed` and a workflow's Notify step, which is one informational entry on the same projection with no event of its own. The daemon applies the per-kind switches (`Waiting on you`, `Finished`, `Failed` and `Notify steps` under `Notify me outside the app`, kept per device in the machine's settings file), the master switch and a session's mute once, when it writes an entry: a gated entry is written withheld and posts nothing, so main posts only `pending` entries, and no preference gates a withdrawal. A session's mute (`Mute notifications` / `Unmute notifications`, `session.mute` / `session.unmute`) silences that session's `Finished` and `Failed` on the banner, the web address and the email digest, on every device, never `Waiting on you` or a Notify step, and withdraws its standing `Finished` and `Failed` banners. Each banner carries the stable moment identifier the projection supplies, so a session that moves from waiting to finished while nobody is looking replaces its banner in place instead of standing a second one beside it, and the banner is withdrawn when its moment resolves; main records what became of each banner with `attention.bannerSettle {entryId, state}`. Moments that resolve together are merged by the thing they belong to, so one session is named once and two different sessions still get their own. A click brings the app forward and lands on the session's place, as a `sidekicks://` address does. With the app not running, the service starts the app's own program with no window to post the banner, and that process exits. Main withdraws its own banner with `close()` while it holds that `Notification` from the same run; on Windows every other withdrawal goes through the toast history, which the service's Windows half reaches. The app icon outside the window carries the same count the console's own attention control carries — the dock icon on macOS and Linux, a number drawn onto the taskbar icon on Windows — read from that one projection so the two can never disagree, counting only what waits on the person right now, and absent rather than a zero when nothing does. `Show a count on the app icon` alone hides the count, and `Notify me outside the app` with its kinds governs the banners; each is on by default.
- **Deep-link handling.** Main must register a protocol handler for `sidekicks://` on macOS, Windows and Linux: on macOS through the packager's `protocols` entry, on Windows through the registry entry the installer writes, and on Linux through `MimeType=x-scheme-handler/sidekicks` in the `.desktop` entry, which the packager's `protocols` entry does not write. Until the app is packaged the chain is provable on Windows alone. Every session has exactly one address, `sidekicks://session/<id>`, and every workflow run one, `sidekicks://workflow-run/<runId>`, which opens that run's page; each carries its identifier and nothing else — no device, no account, no workspace, no token — so nothing a person can copy, screenshot or find in a log carries a secret. An address carries no credential: every credential a window's calls ride is attached outside the renderer, by main or by the service behind the bridge, and none is ever put in an address. The address is untrusted input from any application on the machine: main parses it and acts on it only by navigating, handing the request to the renderer through `window.subscribeToNavigationRequest`. The renderer, which sees every window, brings forward the view that shows the session where one does, and otherwise switches the focused view of the window used last to it, performing the whole switch a press on that session's row performs; with no window open, main builds one and the renderer shows the session in it. A notification click and a line in the bell's list land the same way. With no app running the platform starts it and the address is answered once the session list has loaded; an address this install's list does not hold says so in one line, `That session is not here.`, rather than opening anything. `sidekicks open <address>` hands the same address to the running app through the platform's own registered link type, so the command does exactly what following the link does, adds no second path into the app, and carries nothing the address does not. The `sidekicks://` form is one contract — one function composes it and one parses it — shared by the session's `Copy link`, this handler and `sidekicks open`.
- **Daemon supervision.** Main starts the daemon detached when it finds none running, and again on the boot card's and the working line's `Retry` through the bridge's `daemon.requestStart()`; it connects through the daemon's socket, reading the token file at every connect, and handshakes with `daemon.hello`. Main sends `daemon.ping` only after 5 seconds with no frame from the daemon, so a busy link carries none, and after 20 seconds with no frame counts the link as dead; the connection state reaches the renderer as the `daemon.status` topic on `daemon.subscribe`. A crash or a dead link triggers automatic restart with exponential backoff (`100ms, 300ms, 1s, 3s, 10s`) up to five attempts, then surfaces a persistent UI error state. Settings › Runtime's `Stop` and `Restart` are the daemon's own `daemon.stop` and `daemon.restart`, the only acts that end work in flight; after the flush, a daemon that has not exited gets SIGTERM and, 2 seconds later, SIGKILL. Each app accepts its own service version and the previous one; outside that range the console is read-only and the working line names the side that is behind — the app or the service — with a press to its fix, the app's update on General or `Update the background service` on Runtime (per [Spec-006 §Fallback Behavior](./006-local-ipc-and-daemon-control.md#fallback-behavior)). The service's own update, its restore from a backup and, on a Windows computer with WSL 2, its move to another place run through main, because the service cannot replace its own program or its own store, or move itself, while it runs: `daemon.requestUpdate()` waits for running work to finish and never stops it, and `daemon.requestRestore(backupId)` stops the service, replaces its data with the backup's and starts it again ([Spec-006](./006-local-ipc-and-daemon-control.md)). On a Windows computer whose Claude Code and Codex live in WSL 2, supervision goes through the per-user logon task and the service's Windows half, which restarts the daemon with the same backoff; when the pipe is absent, main reads why the service cannot start from the status file the Windows half writes, checking the Windows half's mutex before trusting it, and carries it on the `daemon.status` topic as `cannotStart`, beside `whileSignedOut`, whether the service keeps running while the person is signed out.
- **Auto-update.** Scheduled checks against the project's GitHub Releases through `electron-updater` and its stock GitHub provider. Download on `update.requestDownload()`, check its SHA-512 and the operating system's code signature, stage, and apply on relaunch, asking nothing: the service, every run and every shell keep running through the relaunch. An update staged and never applied is reported as `staged` until an update lands. Surface update state to the renderer via the `update` bridge namespace. A failed check discards the file. The app does not roll itself back; a bad release is fixed by the next one.
- **OS keystore access.** Main keeps no entry in the operating system's keystore and holds no control-plane credential. Every secret is the daemon's, each its own item in the operating system's credential store: this machine's DPoP key and the hosted account's refresh token, a provider account's pasted token, a workflow secret's value, and the notification web address with its signing secret and the mail password ([§Native Keystore](#native-keystore)). A provider account's pasted token is masked as it is entered, never read back to any surface, cleared from the form when it is submitted, and stored by the daemon; it is in neither the machine's settings file nor the renderer's own store. No token, key or header value is written to either of those two.
- **Appearance and the keyboard map.** Main keeps the appearance record and the keyboard map as files of its own, beside the window-place file and the updater record (§State And Data Implications), each written whole and atomically over a schema-checked parse and readable and writable only by the person; a missing or broken file reads as the defaults and main repairs it. The appearance record, `userData/appearance.json`, holds the theme, the color scheme, the text size, the transcript width and the current theme's light and dark grounds, written through `window.setAppearance`; main stamps it on the root element of every console document it serves, so the first paint needs no script and no read, and carries it through `window.subscribeAppearance` to every open console window, the one that made the change included, and to a document when it finishes loading. Before `ready` main sets `nativeTheme.themeSource` from the kept scheme and gives each window it builds, and that window's console view, the ground `shouldUseDarkColors` picks, the view's own set before it loads, so the first frame is never the wrong color; it mirrors the scheme into the platform's appearance and the View menu's tick. The keyboard map, `userData/keyboard-map.json`, holds only the overridden rows, keyed by act, read and written through `keyboardMap.read()` and `keyboardMap.write()`; it has no change feed, because one renderer drives every window and holds the one copy.
- **The machine's settings file.** `<home>/.ai-sidekicks/machine-settings.json` has one writer, the background service: `machineSettings.write(change)` hands the change to the service's `daemon.machineSettingsUpdate {change}` and answers with the file as written, and `machineSettings.read()` and `machineSettings.subscribe()` carry the service's live read, each written change reaching every window. Main alone may read the file directly, and only before the service first answers, for the two values it needs at start, the auto-update preference and `Keep crash reports`; the renderer never reads it ([§State And Data Implications](#state-and-data-implications)).
- **The Preview page host.** Main hosts each open Preview page as a `WebContentsView` in the `persist:preview` partition, positioned from the rectangle the renderer publishes through `browser.publishPaneRect`, and moves a page whole — no reload, the same page, socket and debugger link — between windows when its pane moves into its own window and back. When a console window reloads or crashes, main hides every page view in it until a fresh rectangle arrives. Main attaches Electron's in-process debugger to each Preview view and to no other `webContents`, reports each view to the daemon as `preview.pageTargetReport {pageId, targetId}` and carries the debugger's traffic as `preview.pageDebuggerSend` and `preview.pageDebuggerReport` on its own connection to the daemon, so no debug port is opened. It answers the daemon's `preview.pageCookiesRead`, `preview.pageCookiesWrite`, `preview.pageCookiesClear` and `preview.pageSiteDataClear {origins?}` on the Preview partition, clearing through `ses.clearData` per registrable domain, and turns a popup a page opens into a page through `preview.pageOpen`, never a window. A file a page saves on this machine's own desktop window opens the system save dialog: the Preview feature asks for it through the platform bridge (`browser.saveDownload`), and main owns the dialog and the file write, so the window gains no file access. Where no person at this machine can choose — the page reached from another device — main cancels the download in its `will-download` handler with `event.preventDefault()` and reports the refusal to the service, which sends one line back to the pane, `Downloads are off in Preview`. A right-click on the page raises a native menu main builds from the view's `context-menu` event, never the page engine's own. While a previewed page has focus, main claims the chords the renderer published through `browser.publishPageChords` before the page sees them and hands each back through `browser.subscribePageChords`; a bare key and a shift-only key are never claimed.
- **Windows-only jobs.** On both kinds of Windows computer — the service on Windows or in a WSL 2 distribution — every Windows-only job runs in the service's Windows half, not in main: keeping the computer awake while an agent works, the wake timer, and withdrawing banners through the toast history.
- **Crash reporting.** Electron `crashReporter` for main and renderer process crashes. Supervised daemon crashes surface through the Spec-006 supervision contract. Reports stay on the machine that crashed under `Keep crash reports`, on by default: each is built and stripped of personal data on that machine and kept there, and nothing sends it anywhere (session IDs replaced with stable hashes; file paths cut to their names; no content payloads). Any of the person's linked devices reads a machine's reports through Remote Control (`daemon.crashList`), and `sidekicks crash list` reads them from the command line ([§Crash Reporting](#crash-reporting)).

### Renderer Responsibilities

- Render session, orchestration, repo, diff, approval, settings, and workflow-viewer surfaces
- **Own one region each.** Every region of the console has exactly one owner — the one feature, or the one shared folder, that draws it — and each owner sits at its own place in the renderer's import direction (`shared`, then `lib`, `styles` and `assets`, `routing`, `components` and `hooks`, `store`, `services`, `registries`, `features`, `layout` and `app`, each importing only those before it, and no feature importing another): the rail, its destinations, the color-scheme control and the route into Settings belong to the persistent chrome (`layout/`) and to routing (`routing/`); the Notifications list, opened from the rail's bell, to the persistent chrome (`layout/NotificationsList/`), with its projection, count and notifier in the app-wide store (`store/attention/`); the all-sessions list with its groups, its picker and its search, and the session header with its chips and its menu, to the sessions feature; the transcript flow and every row kind in it to the transcript feature, whose row renderer is registered before the transcript pane reads it, so that pane has no not-built state, and which reads a tool row's kind from the payload member `toolKind` (`TOOL_KIND_PAYLOAD_KEY`), the console's own name for it until the timeline read's contract declares the member; the composer, its command list, its send control, its working line, its context ring and the approvals card it carries to the composer feature; the worktree switcher, its setup card and the ship strip to the repos and sessions features; the inspector to the inspector feature; the review surface and the read-only file view to the repos feature, which draws the `diff` pane kind; the agents pane, its child tree and its child view to the agents feature, which draws the `agents` pane; the terminal pane to the terminal feature; the previewed page, its acts and its marks to the preview feature, which registers the `browser` pane under its own owner name, `preview`, the pane kind staying `browser`, and whose page host (`PageHost`) places the page's native view at the rectangle the pane publishes; tokens, themes and motion to the shared styles and components (`styles/`, `components/`); every read and subscription to the services and the store (`services/`, `store/`); and every fixture scenario to the desktop app's `fixtures/` folder. Every side pane is registered through the pane registry (`registries/`) from its own feature, and the session's pane layout reaches each pane body through that registry, one pane slot (`SessionPaneSlot`) drawing each pane's frame and the body resolved for it. Two owners never draw one region.
- **Derive presentation and nothing else.** The daemon owns every fact: run state, tool runs, child state and spend, approval requests and their resolution, worktree occupancy with its dirty and ahead counts, diff and pull-request state, tree staleness, the per-project running / waiting / done tally, and the running-child count behind the agents badge. The renderer derives only presentation — which pane is open, pane widths, hover and focus state, the ship strip's one next action as a pure function of the daemon's uncommitted / ahead / pushed / pull-request facts, and the elapsed readings it holds in its own model rather than reading back out of the drawn page.
- Merge live projections from daemon subscriptions with control-plane subscriptions into a coherent session experience (per `desktop.md` §Data Flow)
- Route all privileged operations through the preload bridge
- Never cache auth material (the bridge enforces this; renderer code treats every call as authenticated-by-main)
- Handle disconnection states gracefully: daemon disconnect → reconnect or read-only mode per [Spec-006 §Fallback Behavior](./006-local-ipc-and-daemon-control.md#fallback-behavior), the working line becoming the connection line with `Retry`; a service outside the accepted version range → read-only, the working line naming the side that is behind; control-plane disconnect → the session keeps running against the local daemon with cross-device sync degraded
- Render every surface in the Meridian design language, inside the console surface set, and under the console rules of `§Console Design (Meridian)`: a control whose capability the bound driver does not declare is absent rather than disabled, a control the daemon may refuse is offered with its typed refusal rendered, and no read runs on an interval
- Drive every window from one renderer: a further window of session views and a side pane's own window are this renderer's `window.open` children, rendered from the same stores and daemon subscriptions, and the renderer sets each window document's title from its focused view (§Main Process Responsibilities)
- Run in WebKit as well as Chromium, because the web client in Safari and the iPhone app's system web view run this same front end: every page declares `<!doctype html>` and `<meta charset="utf-8">`; the build's `build.cssTarget` names the Chromium the desktop's Electron carries and the Safari and iOS versions the hosts carry, so `-webkit-user-select` is written beside `user-select` and no prefixed property is written by hand; a surface that puts focus back on the control that opened it records that control from the opening press or key, never from `document.activeElement`; pressing the find box's chevrons leaves the focus in its field; a `ResizeObserver` callback never changes, in the same frame, the size of an observed element no deeper in the page than the one it answers, itself included; and Tab reaches buttons and links as the design's focus rule draws it wherever the host allows, the system's own keyboard-navigation setting ruling in Safari and the iPhone app

### Console Design (Meridian)

_This subsection fixes the console's design language, its surface set, its pane and window model, its fixtures, its budgets, and what each surface shows, offers and refuses. What stays with Plan-020's console registration is the code that draws each surface, its fixture scenarios and its own density budget. Every wire call a surface makes names a method its owning spec states and its owning plan builds._

#### The four bars

- **Richness.** The console carries a signature set no chat client has: every agent of a session drawn as one tree, approval moments that leave visible residue, system messages for a provider switch, a compaction and a rollback, an honest park banner, and an all-clear state. The first run and the transcript's states are designed compositions, reviewed on their own captures rather than gated on pixels (§Console Test Tiers).
- **Elegance.** Every surface names its single job and its density budget — what is collapsed by default and what is one click away. No surface carries two visible controls for one act. Attention is steered by luminance and the two-hue rule, never by motion.
- **Zero copy.** Mechanics are adopted from the field; skin, names, and copy are ours.
- **Light on the machine.** Richness is paid for in design, never in RAM or CPU. The console polls nothing: every refresh is event-driven through one scheduler with an absolute deadline. Every cap, window, and timeout is a named constant with a one-line rationale, and every subsystem states its memory floor. Heavy work — highlighting, diff compute, graph layout, markdown for long messages — runs off the main thread or lazily, and streaming paints through a bounded reveal budget so four concurrent lanes cost one frame. A library is admitted only after its bytes, heap, and frame cost are measured against an own build, and the budgets below are measured from the first commit.

#### Meridian, the design language

1. **Transcript rows.** The transcript is one continuous flow — no card, no panel and no framed container around a turn — and role is carried by asymmetry alone ([ADR-032 §Decision](../decisions/032-transcript-is-one-flow.md#decision)). The user's words sit in a tinted, right-aligned bubble with an asymmetric corner and no width cap, sized to its own text, well inside the column when short and out to the column's own edge when long. The agent's words are flat prose wrapping at that same edge, followed by a foot carrying the reply's stamp and a hover-revealed copy control rather than a row of its own. Every other row is a flush-left transcript line: a tool call is a line and never a card, with a verb column as wide as the longest verb it must hold, the target, and a trailing cell; a system message names the act in words, never the actor, and carries no glyph. There is no left-and-right alternation, no avatar, no name label, no eyebrow, and no identity mark of any kind for the one user. No row is marked with where it was typed either — no badge for another device and none for whatever delivered it — and a row names a machine only where the machine changes what the row means, as a loopback address does. Rows sit in a centered column with a gutter either side, one derived gap apart whatever kind they are, and no row kind carries a margin of its own. The screen reads as a work log because it is one.
2. **Agent hues.** Each agent in a session wears one step of a twelve-step OKLCH wheel, the steps thirty degrees apart at one lightness and one chroma. An agent whose definition chose a hue wears that step; an agent with no saved definition, or whose definition chose none, takes the next step no agent in the session has taken, steps handed out in the order agents enter the session event log — the order every client replays identically — so every client draws the same hues; once all twelve are taken, a later agent takes the step the fewest agents in the session wear, the earliest step on the wheel breaking a tie, so the twelve stay as evenly shared as they can. No hue off the wheel is used anywhere, and a hue that falls between steps takes its nearest step. An agent keeps its hue for the life of the session. The hue answers "which agent" and nothing else: the agent's swatch, the robot glyph drawn on its hue, its row in the command list and in the agents pane, and wherever the transcript tints that agent. Hues are never used for attention. The one user carries no hue and no identity mark of any kind, and a system message carries none either.
3. **The two-hue rule.** Amber means you are needed. Red means something failed. Nothing else is colored for attention; the brand accent is one desaturated cyan used only on interactive affordances. A view with nothing amber and nothing red is, by construction, a view that needs nothing from you. **Contrast floors, enforced at the token level on both schemes (WCAG 2.2 AA):** body and UI text ≥ 4.5:1 against its ground and large text (≥ 24 px, or ≥ 18.66 px bold) ≥ 3:1; non-text controls and their boundaries ≥ 3:1 against adjacent colors; the twelve agent hues are generated at a lightness that clears 3:1 as edges, rings, and marks on both grounds and are never used as text; amber and red each carry a text variant that clears 4.5:1 and a mark variant that clears 3:1 on both grounds; the cyan accent clears 3:1 as an affordance and 4.5:1 wherever it colors text. **One focus mark.** The one focus ring is the browser's own keyboard-focus mark (`:focus-visible`), drawn identically on every control and scaling with the text: no control sets a ring width or color of its own, so its color is the browser's rather than a token's, and nothing moves to make room for it. A control flush against an edge that clips it — the transcript pane and its scroll area, the diff's body and file entries, the palette's input and list — draws the same mark just inside its own edge, the inset in token units, so it is never cut off (WCAG 2.2 2.4.7 and 2.4.11). The terminal's mount point draws it on `:focus-within` in the browser's own focus color, `-webkit-focus-ring-color`, so it matches every other mark. Every focus mark, the composer card's border included, reaches 3:1 against the surface it is drawn on, in both themes (WCAG 2.2 1.4.11). Because the browser's mark carries no token, this floor is measured on the rendered screen rather than set by a token. The focused pane carries no mark of its own, and the composer card's strengthened border while the draft has focus is the one exception to the one mark. **Two themes, two schemes, one contract.** The console ships two themes — Meridian, the default, and Graphite — each drawn in a light and a dark scheme. The theme and the scheme, with the text size and the transcript width, are kept in the main process's appearance record, which main stamps onto the root of every console document it serves, so each is right on the first paint with no script and no read; every console window's own background, and its console view's background inside it, is painted from the kept theme's ground for the scheme that window resolves to before the first frame, so no frame comes up the wrong color and nothing shows white before the renderer paints; and the platform is told which appearance each window is in, so its own window buttons and system strip are drawn to match. All of it follows the theme when it changes, in every open console window, and follows the system while the scheme is set to follow it. The scheme is reachable three ways that all set the one value: the rail's control cycles follow-the-system, dark, light; the Appearance settings page carries the three side by side; and the platform's View menu carries the same three with the one in force ticked. A press on a theme swatch previews that theme live, with no restart and no reload, and the Appearance page draws the three scheme choices as cards, each as wide as a theme swatch with its picture taking a swatch's height, corners and edge, so the two rows read at one scale at any page width and wrap as the swatches do; each card paints a small live picture of the console from the tokens the chosen theme is using at that moment — the follow-the-system card painting its picture split, light on one half and dark on the other — so a picture can never disagree with the screen; the chosen card carries the accent on its edge and on its name, and choosing a theme repaints all three at once. Every component reads tokens and no literal color appears anywhere in the console tree; the token contract is the union of both themes' needs and is proven at run time against the running console, never by parsing a theme file. Layout, behavior and copy are identical under every theme and scheme, and the status indicators stay static under both. **The check asserts the floors rather than restating them:** in each of the four renderings — two themes by light and dark — text against the surface it sits on reaches 4.5:1, the accent against the surface it sits on reaches 3:1, and words on the accent reach 4.5:1 against it; a token edit that drops a pair below its floor fails the check, which names the rendering, the pair and the ratio it measured. The accessibility tier asserts every token pair against these ratios and fails on the first miss. **One switch draws the console flat.** The Appearance page carries a screen-reader simplification, off by default; with it on the panes lose their decorative borders and washes, no animation or transition runs anywhere, a tool call reads as one plain line of words rather than as a glyph, a column and a cell, and the transcript reads as headings and paragraphs in order. The switch is one preference of the machine, kept in the machine's settings file; the service sets `CLAUDE_AX_SCREEN_READER=1` in the shells the terminal pane starts, where a person may run a provider themselves, passes nothing to a provider session it starts, and claims no provider behavior for the switch.
4. **Type and figures.** UI text is a humanist grotesque; every wire-true figure — costs, counts, SHAs, durations, token totals, timestamps — renders in mono — verbatim as a string, `Intl`-formatted from the exact value as a quantity (the wire-figures rule under §Rules every console surface obeys). Mono is the signature that a number came from the wire; prose never paraphrases a figure. The faces are IBM Plex Sans and IBM Plex Mono, variable builds self-hosted from the foundry packages with tabular figures through `font-variant-numeric` and no font feature settings, and both ship inside the app, so nothing is fetched to draw the interface, no family is ever chosen, and there is no substitute to announce. Numbers that sit in a column are tabular. A live figure under sixty seconds reads in whole seconds and at or above it as minutes and seconds. Every clock figure is written the way the machine's own clock writes it — on a machine set to a twelve-hour clock, `2:20 PM`, `Mon 9:58 AM`, `Yesterday 5:31 PM`, and never `14:20` — with the day word in front of the time and seconds only where the figure already carried them. Text truncates with an ellipsis and keeps its full value in its own hover title; it never wraps inside a fixed-height row. **One scale, one root.** Every text size and line height in the console's own styles comes from the type scale and nowhere else, and every width, floor and ceiling of the chrome that carries them — the rail, the sessions track, the side panes, the transcript's measure, its gutters, its row gap, and every pane's default and floor — is root-relative and derived from what it holds; only the hairlines, the corner radii and the icon strokes stay in pixels. The Appearance settings page's `Text size` offers four steps — `Small · Default · Large · Larger` — driving the root size through four values, 15, 16, 18 and 20 px, the default a 16 px root carrying 14 px body text, so a step grows the reading matter and the chrome in one proportion and leaves what is drawn as drawn. No chord steps it, and a step re-measures every drawn transcript row and drops the height remembered for every row that is not drawn, so the rows grow in place and the row being read stays under the reader's eye. **One width for every transcript.** The same page's transcript-width slider sets the single measure every session's transcript reads at, kept as a multiple of the root size the way every other measure that carries content is, so it steps with the text size and a line of prose holds its measure at all four steps. The figure beside the slider is what the value gives at the text size in force and is read again at every step; the default is the reading measure the transcript already holds, 57.5 root sizes, about 920 px at the default text size; the floor is the narrowest a transcript row still reads at, where a tool row still fits its verb column, its target and its timing on one line, 35.5 root sizes; the ceiling is the widest a line of the agent's prose still reads comfortably at the largest text size, 74.5 root sizes; and one step of the slider is one root size. The value is published as one root token in root-relative units and every session reads its column's width from that token and from nothing else, so every session follows it at once, no session overrides it, and there is no second value per text size.
5. **Motion.** Settles, never bounces: 120–180 ms ease-out, entrances fade with a 2 px rise, zero overshoot in chrome; attribution threads draw with a 240 ms line-grow. Hover and press take the quick step, the grid and the flyout the settle step, an approval's fade the thread step.
