Motion is platform-native (CSS transitions, `@starting-style`, View Transitions, the Web Animations API) with an own spring sampler emitting `linear()` easings; no animation library sits on the render path. **Live figures move on one beat.** One ticker advances every live figure on the screen, beating once a second, which is the finest figure any of them shows; there is no per-row interval, every clock's value lives in the model and is never read back out of the page, a figure that finishes freezes in place rather than being replaced, and the duration cell holds a width floor so nothing shifts when a call ends. A timer is never tinted — not amber while waiting, not accent while running — and a status indicator never blinks: the live timer is what says an agent is working. **Streaming settles rather than animates.** A reply's first word is on the screen the moment the runtime has it, with no lead-in; the reveal runs word by word at the pace the words arrive, measured from the wire over a short window, and when the wire runs ahead it changes up to a catch-up gear entered past a backlog threshold and left after a hold, all under the frame budget, so text never drifts behind and never lands in a dump; it keeps draining after the turn closes so nothing is left unshown ([Spec-011 §Required Behavior](011-live-timeline-visibility-and-reasoning-surfaces.md#required-behavior)). A word never lands half-drawn, and arriving text changes only its opacity, so a line filling in never reflows the lines above it and settled text never shifts. Returning to a session draws everything that arrived while the reader was away as settled text at full strength; only words arriving from that moment on animate. **The follow is the reader's.** The transcript follows the bottom only while the reader is within a couple of lines of it; a scroll away releases the follow at once, however small, and it re-engages when the reader returns to that band or presses the jump control. While the follow is on, the page glides after arriving text rather than jumping, and a layout correction — a picture that typesets late, a row that remeasures, a reflow at a new width — is placed at once instead. When the connection comes back and everything missed has landed, a reader who was at the bottom is carried to the new end — gliding when a little arrived, placed at once when more than a screenful did or motion is reduced — and a reader who had scrolled away is left exactly where they are. A drag on a pane edge, and a selection being dragged inside the transcript, hold the follow until the pointer is released. **Nothing animates unwatched.** Motion runs only where something changes under the reader's eye, is armed by an act rather than by a scroll, and is placed at once in a region that is not in view. `prefers-reduced-motion` removes every animation and transition on every element without exception, places everything at once, and resolves a stream straight to the full text — which is the one case a switch would have served, so there is no setting that turns streaming off and no second rendering of a reply. The reveal engine reads that setting itself rather than leaving it to the stylesheet: the engine resolves the stream it is drawing to its whole text at once, so what the rule says and what the engine does are the same thing.
6. **Copy.** Calm authority: sentence case, past-tense receipts, no exclamation marks, no celebration copy. Refusals name the actor and the rule; empty states name their kind and their escape hatch. No copy claims a capability the code does not implement, and no copy names a vendor as a product feature. **The word a person reads is sidekick, never agent.** The types, methods and events keep their own names and the rule reaches only what is on the screen; two things are the provider's own spelling and stay as it spells them — the mention syntax a provider offers for addressing one of its own agents, and a command name drawn from that provider's own list. **The screen never shows the wire's own spelling.** An error code, a refusal reason, a status, an event kind, a tool name, a policy action or a node kind reaches the eye as words in sentence case, followed by the daemon's own message in plain words; each tool and each policy action registers the label a person reads, a node shows its kind's display name, and an unlabeled code falls back to its own words in sentence case. Only what a person or an agent wrote or would type stays as written — model identifiers, file names and paths, commands, diffs, terminal output and prose — and the mono face is what marks it as written. An account is named only by the identity its provider reports when it signed in: the email address that provider reports, then the plan in the provider's own word, then the organization where the plan has one — never a label the console or the user invented. **Words live in one place.** Every string a person reads is declared once and read from there; no component writes its words inline, and because every row is sized by what it holds, a longer wording fits wherever it comes from. **Every try-again is one shape and one of two words.** It is a faint clickable word at the right end of the line it belongs to, with no box, no border and no icon, and the word says what is redone: `Retry` asks for the same connection, delivery, drawing or step again, and `Try again` redoes a read, a page load or an act that failed. There is no welcome wizard, tour, checklist or dismissible tip anywhere: a surface with nothing in it says so in its own words and offers exactly one next act, named against what the session is bound to.
7. **Density budgets.** Nothing folds itself: every run group and every tool call with a body is open at rest, live or long settled, except an advisor call's readable advice, which is folded at rest, and a fold is only where the person put it — a run never closes when it ends and a call never closes when its output lands; diff cards expand to a height cap and then offer "show all", and a `⌘`-click anywhere on a diff block in the flow opens Review on that file, scrolled to the top of it, exactly as the footer's link does, while a plain click on the block does nothing and the gesture is listed once on the Keyboard page; the inspector holds the open session's facts alone, re-keying when its view shows another session; settings is two panes, one page at a time; secondary controls live one click away — a row's hover footer or its context menu — never as a second visible button.
8. **Kinds of nothing.** Five kinds of nothing render differently because the person's next move differs for each: _not loaded_ (a skeleton in the row's shape), _empty_ (a quiet line with the escape hatch), _error_ (a red-edged row carrying the reason in words with the daemon's own message under it), _not checked_ (a dotted badge), and _unknown, still computing_ (a badge with a clock glyph). A renderer that collapses two of these into one is wrong. **An empty surface names one next act.** The empty transcript says there are no messages yet and invites the first, naming what the session is bound to; a list with nothing in it says so and names the one control that fills it; a search that matched nothing says what it searched for and keeps its group headings drawn. **A subject that goes takes its surface with it, and says so.** A pane whose subject is gone closes itself, and a one-line note between the header and the surface says which pane closed and why; the note outlives the chip that went with it, and opening any pane or leaving the session answers it. A session whose remembered pane has lost its subject opens no pane and says nothing, because nothing closed under the reader. **A region that fails says so where it stands.** One line naming that region with `Retry` beside it, in the slot a pane uses to say why it closed, while the transcript and every other region keep updating; a press draws the region again. A read the daemon does not answer keeps the rows a list already had rather than emptying it, says in amber on the list's own header that it could not be refreshed, and counts no figure it could not read until the next answer lands. **Failure is the only red.** A failed call is marked; a call that succeeded carries no tick, no "done" and no green, because success is shown by absence. Failure is read from the run's own durable record, so a restart replays the same answer and a failed run still reads failed in the morning, and a refusal a person made is not drawn in the waiting color — it is a row whose reason says the person declined.
9. **The refusal grammar.** Controls are offered; refusals are rendered, in one of three shapes — **inline** on the control that was pressed (the reason in words, the daemon's own message under it, the next move when one exists), as a **card** in the transcript when the refusal changes history, or as a **banner** across the session screen when it changes what the whole session can do. A refusal never hides the control that produced it and never re-derives the daemon's rule. An inline refusal is visible words on the surface that asked, at that surface's own width, on one line under the control that was pressed; it stays until the next act and is never carried in a hover title alone. A whole dimmed list carries its reason once in its header, in amber, and again on every row, and a control grayed by one of the named exceptions §Rules every console surface obeys lists carries its reason in its own hover title. A banner sits directly under the session header, above the pane note and the surface, carries its own dismissal, blocks nothing and refuses nothing, and enters no attention count; a dismissal is for that occurrence alone, so the same condition arising again draws it again. One such banner is the provider that ignored part of its own configuration file: it names the provider, the file and the line or key it ignored, quoted from the provider's own report ([Spec-004 §Required Behavior](./004-provider-driver-contract-and-capabilities.md#required-behavior)), with `Open the file` and the dismiss as its two presses; the dismiss holds for that start of the provider, coming back to the session does not bring it back, and a later start still on defaults draws it again. **One Escape order covers the session screen.** Escape dismisses in priority order — the rewind menu or an open overlay first, the plan reader, the quick look, the command palette or the find box (which also clears its highlights), then typed text in Preview's address field when it has focus (discarded, the page address returns), then a pressed Preview tool, then full width, then the More menu, the fork popover, the worktree popover (stepping its sub-mode back to its list), the draft's command list, then an in-progress edit of a pending row (its text restored), the Review base menu, and Review itself when focus is inside it. Escape pressed while the terminal body has focus is the shell's and this ladder does not run. When nothing on this ladder is open, the message box has focus and a turn is running, Escape does what Interrupt does; pressed again it interrupts again and reaches no child; with the draft empty and nothing running, a second Escape opens the rewind menu; in a child's view with focus it acts on that child. A destructive act is confirmed in place, inline where it was started, saying in plain words what it removes and whether it can be undone before it offers the act; it is never a dialog and never switchable off. Replacing unsent words in the draft is one such act: an act that writes into the draft while it holds unsent text, starting a workflow from the palette for one, asks directly above the draft that it will replace them.

**Layout grammar.** The app frame is three columns: the icon rail at its own derived width, the sessions track, and the screen view taking the rest, which on the session screen holds one or more session views side by side (§The session screen). The middle track is nothing wide — a zero-width track and not a stub — while it is closed, and while it holds the all-sessions list or the notification list it takes the width the person has dragged it to, and until they have, the width a session row's own furniture needs at the current text size. The grid itself animates, so the screen view slides over rather than being covered, and the track pins its children to its full width while it animates so nothing reflows mid-motion. A desktop window opens centered at a size taken from the display's own work area and never shrinks below its floor: across, the rail, the sessions track and one session view's floor — the transcript's own floor and one pane's floor — summed; down, the shortest window in which the header, the transcript and the composer still stack; both recomputed from those parts whenever the text size changes. The one width at which the grid changes shape is the fold to one column, and the content sets that point — the transcript's own floor against what the track and an open pane need at the current text size — never a device class, a build or a list of devices. Because a desktop window never shrinks below its floor, a desktop window never folds; the fold is reached where the same front end runs with no window floor, on Remote Control's phone and browser surfaces. Folded, every screen shows one level at a time: Sessions the list, then the session, then a pane; Sidekicks the list, then the editor; Skills the list, then the skill; Workflows the tab lists, then the canvas or the run page, then the step panel; Settings the page list, then the page. A detail level's header begins with one back control naming the level above it — `‹ Sessions`, `‹ Sidekicks`, `‹ Skills`, `‹ Workflows`, `‹ Settings`, and `‹ <session title>` over a pane. Both levels stay built and only one is shown, the other inert, so moving between them shows and hides rather than rebuilding: a streaming turn keeps streaming, the panes keep what they had open and nothing reloads. A window that folds lands on its deepest open level, a pane when one is open, and widening it again draws the levels side by side with nothing closed; on the session level a pane's header chip, while its pane is open but hidden, shows that pane's level and never closes it. The rail stays a rail: it is drawn beside each screen's list level and is absent on a detail level, where the back control leads to it, and it never becomes a bottom bar. The platform's own Back — a phone's back gesture or a browser's back — dismisses the topmost surface in the order Escape does and stops short of the work: it never interrupts a turn, clears a draft or opens the rewind menu. Where the pointer is coarse every press target grows to a fingertip, what hover reveals stands at rest, and every act a drag performs has a press that does the same. Every menu, list and card a folded screen opens keeps clear of the device's own top and bottom insets and caps its height at a share of the window rather than at a figure, so nothing opens under a home bar or past the top of the screen and every row of it can be reached; where the window has no inset the padding is nothing and the layout is unchanged.

A session view's pane layout is seven tracks — the session column, the inspector, the review surface, the agents pane, the terminal, the Preview pane and the file view — and exactly one of the six beside the session column stands open at a time, or none of them, the terminal able to stack beneath whichever one is up; a pane moved into its own window (§Detached panes) leaves its track shut and is drawn in that window instead. The column is two rows: the session column spans both, a main pane takes the upper row of its own track, and a stacked terminal opens the lower row and spans the pane tracks, so it is exactly as wide as the pane above it. Inside the session track the column is the session header, then the surface, then the docked composer. Every pane's header names what the pane shows and carries its own close; the focused pane carries no mark of its own, and where the keyboard focus sits is drawn only by the one focus mark on the focused control, never by a hue. The inspector is a pane kind, not a fixed third column.

The command palette opens over the session column on a light scrim, with its search field focused and empty and the transcript and the composer still running under it; Enter runs the selected row and closes it — except `Copy link` and `Copy conversation`, whose row reads `Copied`, or `Could not copy`, for the one transient duration before the palette closes — 1,200 ms, the one fixed duration on which `Opening…`, `Copied` and the copy flash all clear — and Escape, a press on the scrim and the chord that opened it all close it without running anything. It has categories — each unbroken run of rows under a small heading reading that run's own category, the best-placed row's category leading and the categories not reshuffling between keystrokes, the headings labels the arrows walk straight past so the list stays one flat set of rows — recents, where an act run lately carries a recent mark and an empty field lists those acts first, newest first and up to eight; a scoped-context row naming what the command acts on; a footer naming the keys it answers with the result count beside it; a ceiling on how many rows one query renders; and one matcher shared with settings search, whose matched letters are marked inside a row's name by weight and brightness on the hue the name already wears rather than by a color of their own. That one matcher is the Settings screen's search as well: the field above the page list searches three strings per page — the page's own name, its entry's label, and the words that page declares itself findable by — the best of the three winning and ties falling in the list's own order, and it searches the controls inside every page, ranking a hit on the control's own label above the heading it sits under and that above the control's one-line hint, with ties again in page order. Each hit reads the control's label over the page and heading it lives on, the arrows move the highlight, Enter opens the page and lands on the control, and a term nothing matches draws one line naming what was searched for. The list holds every act the open session has, each under the name the screen already gives it — the pane chips, the session's own menu rows, and the composer's level, mode, model, effort and speed controls, a control's row opening that control's own list rather than standing in for it. Five acts live only here: `Copy conversation`, which puts the whole open session on the clipboard in the form the screen reads; `Clear the terminal`, which empties the active shell's own view and its scrollback on screen without sending anything to the shell, so it is safe while a command is running, reaches the active tab alone, is absent from the list while the session has no shell, and adds no control to the pane header or the tab strip; `Fold every run` and `Unfold every run`, which close and open every run group in the session at once; and `Search all sessions`, which opens the box that searches every session (§The session screen). `Copy conversation` and a message's own copy are the only ways a transcript leaves this screen: nothing anywhere exports one to a file, prints one, or reads one aloud. An act this session cannot take is absent from the list rather than grayed; an act that exists but is closed right now stays listed with the one sentence its owner wrote for why, and refuses in place when it is pressed: `Open in new window` with no side pane open reads `Open a side pane first.`

Iconography is a single-stroke set — Tabler glyphs, and faces of our own drawn to the same geometry where Tabler has none that fits, in one collection compiled at build time — drawn on the 14 px glyph grid the icon token fixes, at the stroke weight the icon-stroke token fixes, with round caps and joins; the rail's glyphs are the one place they run larger. The robot is the one generic mark for an agent — drawn on the agent's hue it is the agent's swatch, the only avatar on the console — and an agent definition may carry its own glyph from the same set as that agent's picture; the mode control draws a hammer or a checklist. The one user carries no mark, and a system message carries no glyph. Borders are the hairline token and there are no heavy rules; the radii are the radius scale's three steps, 3, 6 and 10 px, and nothing is drawn at another radius; elevation is the composer card's glass surface rather than a stack of shadows; each surface carries one filled control and no more; and hover is one derived color that reveals only affordances whose space is already reserved.

#### Rules every console surface obeys

- **Absent, not disabled.** A control whose capability the bound driver does not declare is not rendered — a disabled control would assert the capability exists and is momentarily unavailable, which would be false. A control the daemon may refuse is rendered and its refusal is shown; eligibility is never projected by the renderer — the `I-002-19` fail-closed-projection discipline, generalized. The rule reaches the menus, the composer's command list and its controls, the pickers, the session strip and the rail, and it has three named sets of exceptions, each grayed in place with its reason in its own hover title and each answering no press. **Three single controls** whose subject is a choice the person can change beside them: the review surface's approve verdict on a pull request the user authored, the composer's output-speed control on a session whose chosen model has no fast output, and the `browser` pane's back and forward, whose subject the person changes by following a link. **The session header's three pane chips**, which stand at every moment on either session shape and gray when they cannot apply, because the header is the one place a person's eye returns to and a chip that comes and goes cannot be learned. **And two rows that stand inside a list their neighbors explain**: the shell tab menu's close-others and close-to-the-right where there is no such tab, and the worktree create form's base list on a branch another worktree already has checked out.
- **Offer, then render the refusal.** The renderer never pre-denies; it calls, and renders the typed refusal code with the daemon's message text and the person's next move.
- **One accountant.** Every cost figure comes from the committed-spend read surface ([Spec-014 §Cost Figure Display Consistency](014-multi-agent-orchestration.md#cost-figure-display-consistency)); the renderer never sums visible rows, never labels a figure a lower bound, never totals across sessions. A figure the daemon has not supplied is not counted in the renderer at all, and a gate whose input is missing refuses rather than guessing.
- **Write-only credentials.** A credential-bearing input appears on no reply and is never echoed ([ADR-026 §Decision](../decisions/026-provider-credential-custody-posture.md#decision); the Provider Accounts and MCP Servers sketches below).
- **Fail-closed projection.** An unknown enum member renders as the explicit unrecognized row or badge, never as a guess.
- **Wire figures are verbatim; wire quantities are formatted from the exact value.** Two classes, decided by the value's kind and never by the surface. **Byte-for-byte strings** — roots and paths, SHAs and digests, identifiers, versions, model names, commands, diffs, terminal output, provider-supplied labels and message text, and any cost or count the wire supplies as a string — render exactly as received, in mono, never re-parsed. A wire **name** is not a figure and is not in this class: an error code, a refusal reason, a status, an event kind, a tool name, a policy action or a node kind reaches the eye as words, under item 6 of §Meridian, the design language. **Formatted quantities** — a numeric duration, byte count, token count, percentage, rate, or timestamp, and a cost the wire supplies as a number beside its currency — render through `Intl` (`DurationFormat`, `NumberFormat` in unit, currency, and compact styles, `RelativeTimeFormat`) in the renderer locale, in mono, from the exact wire value with no client-side arithmetic beyond the format itself — the one exception being byte quantities, which the console's single shared formatter scales by powers of 1024 and labels from `B / KiB / MiB / GiB / TiB` (`Intl` has no binary unit), the scaled number still passing through `NumberFormat`, `apps/desktop/AGENTS.md` §Chokepoints holding that formatter as the only site that scales, with the `wire-figure-formatting` runtime tripwire firing on a figure rendered outside the two classes: a dollar figure as `$` and the amount in US dollars, two decimals above $0.50 and four at $0.50 or below, rounded half up from the stored amount and carrying no mark of an estimate, bytes in binary units, durations digital at one minute and above and `1.2 s`-style below, relative times always paired with the absolute timestamp on hover, and compact numbers only where the exact figure is one hover away — the exact wire value is exposed in the element's `title` and in the inspector, so no formatted figure hides the number the daemon sent. A model's context window is the figure its provider reports — Claude Code's at the session's creation and after every model change, Codex's from its model catalog corrected by the first turn's own report — and never an assumed one: it reads beside the model's name and as `of <window>`, and a row whose window has not arrived shows the model with no window, never a placeholder figure.
- **No interval polling.** Reads happen on subscribe, on window focus, on reconnect, and on the terminal events the owning spec names — through one refresh scheduler firing at `min(lastEvent + delay, firstEvent + maxWait)`, serialized, so a trailing debounce cannot starve under a stream.
- **Reserved, not stubbed.** A capability-gated feature the corpus reserves gets no surface, no placeholder, and no setting until the capability exists.
- **No control ships inert.** Every control the console draws is real and must work, and a fix lands on every surface, session and state that shares the part it repaired, confirmed on each rather than assumed.
- **One way in, one way out.** A menu or pop-over anchors to the control that opened it, flips to the other side when it would leave the window and clamps to the window's edge so every row of it stays reachable by the pointer, and closes on an outside press and on Escape and on nothing else; closing it returns focus to the control that opened it, however it closed. Every menu and list answers the same keys — the arrows move one cursor, home and end jump to the first and last row, Enter presses the row the cursor is on, Escape closes, rows that cannot act are skipped, the first row takes the cursor when the list opens, and no letter key moves the cursor, because the field keeps the caret. The cursor's row is scrolled into view on every move, and a list that shrinks as a query is typed keeps its cursor on a row that still exists. Transient statuses — an opening line, a copied line, the copy flash — all clear on one fixed duration. A surface waiting on a read says it is loading only after one short fixed delay and only while nothing of it is drawn, so a surface that was already open never flashes a loading line, and a session whose read fails reads `Could not load this session` with `Try again`.
- **Nothing forces a state by hand.** No switch, flag or hidden control anywhere in the console stages an attachment, opens a picker, slows a list or forces a state. Fixture code and data are compiled only into a fixture-capable build and the developer tools only into a development build, never into a release build (§Fixtures), so nothing in a shipped console can make scripted data look like real work.

#### The surface set

Before any surface draws, opening the app draws one quiet cover over the window until the background service has answered, then fades it away and never draws it again while the app runs. The cover carries the working indicator and one line saying what is happening — `Starting the background service…` when the app started the service itself, `Connecting to the background service…` when it found one already running — and nothing behind it is drawn half-painted. A service that cannot be reached at app start draws one quiet card in the middle of the window in place of an empty console, `The background service is not answering.` with `Retry`, and nothing else; once the console has drawn, the same fact belongs to the working line, so the card never appears over a session a person was reading.

##### The icon rail

Five destinations and two controls. The destinations are Sessions, Sidekicks, Skills, Workflows and Settings, in that order ([ADR-029 §Decision](../decisions/029-five-rail-destinations.md#decision)), each a glyph with a text label on hover and in its accessible name, the Sidekicks glyph being the robot the composer's agents badge draws, the one generic mark that ever stands for an agent, and the Workflows glyph the `workflow` pane glyph; the rail runs the first four, then a spacer, then the attention control and the color-scheme control, then Settings. It is a bare sunken column of square hit areas, each as wide as its glyph plus the press target a pointer or a fingertip needs around it and growing with the root text size, with no cards, no chips and no panels. The destination the person is on draws an accent spine at the spine token's own weight, in the rail's own padding and outside the button. Rail glyphs are the one place a glyph runs larger than the console's own size, and they come from the console's own collection. Every rail button carries a hover title naming its destination; an icon-only control carries one string that is both its hover title and its spoken name, with no second tooltip surface and no delay before it shows. The Sessions button's hint ends in the chord that opens the list, read from that act's own row on the Keyboard page each time it is drawn, so a rebinding changes the hint and the suffix is absent while the act holds no chord. The Sessions button toggles the sessions track and reads as expanded to assistive technology while that track holds the all-sessions list, the same way the attention control reads while the notification list is open.

**Each destination owns its own addresses**, so a deep link and a press on the rail are one act and the back control works. Sessions addresses the all-sessions list and one session's screen. Sidekicks addresses the definition library, one definition, and a new definition; that screen is [Spec-026](./026-agent-definitions-and-peer-invocation.md)'s and this spec restates none of it. Skills addresses the list, the new-skill form, a folder opened at its entry file, and that folder with one other file open; that screen is [Spec-029 §The destination, the rail and the addresses](./029-skills.md#the-destination-the-rail-and-the-addresses)'s. Workflows addresses its list, the authoring canvas and one run being watched — `#/workflows`, `#/workflows/runs` and `#/workflows/runs/<runId>`, the grammar Settings uses — as in-page tabs of the destination [Spec-015](./015-workflow-authoring-and-execution.md) owns; an unknown run id opens the list with one line saying the run is not here. Settings addresses the screen, one page, and a page with a selection; `Devices` is the last of its pages, after Runtime, and never a destination of its own. The command palette lists all five in one category of its own, so every destination is reachable from the keyboard as well as from the rail.

The attention control toggles the notification list and reads as expanded while that list is open. It carries the console's one badge: an amber pip, hidden from assistive technology with the count carried in the button's accessible name, absent rather than a zero when nothing waits, taken from the daemon's attention projection and never counted in the renderer. It counts the sessions and workflow runs waiting on a person right now. The color-scheme control cycles follow-the-system, dark, light. Settings carries one small quiet dot while an update is staged and waiting for the restart — no count, because there is only ever one update, and not the attention color, because nothing is needed from the person — hidden from assistive technology with the fact in the button's accessible name and gone when the update is applied; what the update is and the press that applies it live on the General settings page. That dot is the one exception to the one-badge rule.

Nothing else is in the rail: no session list inside it, no status text, no branding, no avatar, no second settings entry, and no mark on any other button. The Sessions button carries no mark of its own — a session row's status dot is that row's state, not a notification. What belongs to one session — its worktree and repository acts, Review, the approvals card and the agents pane — is reached inside that session, from its header, its pane layout and its composer; the rail names the top-level destinations and leaves no surface out. Assistive technology reads the rail as the console's sections.

##### The all-sessions list

One row per session in the sessions track, grouped by shape and by nothing else: chats above every project group, each project one group under a foldable header carrying the daemon's running / waiting / done roll-up, and an archived group at the foot of the scroll under its own header with its count, folded by default and opening the way a project header opens its list. Grouping is the only discriminator — no badge and no second row layout — and a row names neither the provider that runs it nor the machine it runs on; what a session is bound to is read in the inspector. A session the person typed in a terminal has no row: the agents reach it and the person talks to it in that terminal ([Spec-014 §Sessions Talking To Each Other](./014-multi-agent-orchestration.md#sessions-talking-to-each-other)). Each attached project is one group and its rows are that project's own sessions: no group stands for two checkouts and no tally is merged across them. The per-project tally, and the chats header's own count, are the daemon's and are never counted in the renderer. With two or more machines registered to the person, the list's head carries the machine in view, its name and a chevron, opening a list of every machine that marks `This machine` and `Not reachable`; picking one changes every screen to that machine, and the session this device last had in view there opens, or the list's first row on a machine it has never shown. A machine that is not reachable can still be picked: its list shows as it was last read, its head saying it could not be refreshed, and a row pressed there opens its session as last read. With one machine the switch is absent.

A row is a button of two stacked lines, the second padded to align under the title rather than under the dot: the status dot and the title on the first — with one bell-off mark after the title on a muted session, sized from the row's own text as the dot is, titled `Notifications muted`, the title truncating before the mark does and the row's spoken name ending `, notifications muted` — then the branch in mono on a project session or the document count on a chat, with the age right-aligned. Neither of those second-line facts sits behind a switch and neither costs a repository read at draw time. A third reading takes that same slot while the session is trading messages with another session and nobody is typing in either one: `talking to builder · 14` — the other session's name and how many messages the two have traded since the person last wrote in either — on **both** sessions' rows, with the age still at its right. It carries no cost figure, because money on a session's surface lives only in the inspector's cost section; it is never counted as something waiting on the person, so it never reaches the attention control or the bell; and it goes when the exchange ends, the row returning to its branch or its document count. An age reads `now` under a minute and then one count in one unit with no "ago", advancing on the console's one shared beat. A status dot has five states the daemon derives: running in the accent, waiting on the person in amber, done a hollow ring, failed a red mark, idle a faint gray circle. Its size derives from the row's own text and is a step larger on a project roll-up; each carries a hover title naming its state in one word — working, waiting on you, done, failed, idle — which is also how the row's spoken name ends; and the running and waiting dots step through an opacity ramp rather than blinking. A session that finished while the person was away draws its done dot filled until they open it and hollow from the moment they do, read from the one seen-or-unseen fact the daemon keeps for the notification list, so the row and that list can never disagree. A running or waiting claim the daemon has stopped renewing stops reading as live once it is older than a bound derived from how often the daemon reports: the row falls back to idle and its timer stands still where it froze. A failed reading is never aged out, because it is a fact about the run rather than a claim about right now.

An untitled session's row reads `New chat` or `New session`, faint and italic, never an invented title and never a goal: a goal is a command sent inside a session, and nothing names, labels or groups a session by one. A session names itself from its first completed exchange — a short throwaway pass reads that exchange and writes a few words, falling back to the first words of the first message where the pass returns nothing — and keeps that name until it is renamed. The name is written only while the session is still unnamed, and a rename made while the pass is running is re-read before anything is written, so a name the person typed always wins; naming a session renames neither its branch nor its worktree. Renaming is an edit in place from the row or the header, saving on Enter and canceling on Escape, an empty name restoring the first-message title, and the row and the header updating together. A row the person minted keeps its place until they archive or close it: an emptied draft, an unnamed session or a session that never sent anything removes nothing, because the list loses a row only on an act of theirs.

There is one new-session control, dashed at rest and solid on hover or while open, and its picker is the whole of the choice a session is started from: `Just chat`, then the recent repositories, then `Open folder…`, then `Clone repository…`. It offers no machine, because a new session runs on the machine in view, which the machine switch already chose. On the machine itself `Open folder…` opens the platform's own folder chooser and attaches the folder picked; on another device it lists the machine's own folders in place, starting at the home folder of the account the service runs as, so the device acts only on what the service listed, as the desktop acts only on what the platform's chooser returned. `Clone repository…` passes the address or path typed to git, started with an argument list and never through a shell, so git's own transport rules decide, and clones into the folder `Clone new repositories into` names, the clone running inside the composer card where a worktree's setup runs; a destination that exists and is not empty is refused in place before anything is fetched. It offers no agent, no provider and no model either: a session has one agent, no step anywhere attaches a second one to it, and a saved definition is named in the composer's command list or on a workflow node — the only two places one is chosen — rather than picked when the session is started. Minting a session prepends its row with a faint italic placeholder title and the right second line, an age of now, bumps its group's own count or forces that project's list open, closes the picker, switches the screen to the session and puts the caret in the draft. Opening the sessions track focuses the search field without scrolling the rows, and closing the track closes the picker with it and returns focus to the rail's Sessions button. The repository a session works in is chosen there and only there; a chat that later needs one gains it through the inspector's attach action. Search filters the list in place across every group and never hides a group header, matching a row on its title, its second line and its folder path, ranked so titles that start with the query come first, then titles that contain it, then second lines and paths, with a clear control at the field's right end that restores the whole list and returns focus to the field. A folded project holding a match unfolds while the field has text and folds again when it is cleared, and only the projects the search itself unfolded fold back. A group whose own name matches keeps every session under it. Searching the text inside sessions is a different surface — the box the palette opens — and the track's own field stays a search over the rows in the list.

A press on a row switches the focused view for real — title, chips, counts, transcript, composer state and panes — or, where the session is already on screen in a view, brings that view's window forward and focuses the view. The focused view's session is marked by one selected wash on its row and by that row reading as the current one to assistive technology; every other session on screen, in this window or another, carries a thin mark at its row's leading edge in the same accent, read as `On screen in another view`; nothing else marks either, and a hovered row takes the one derived hover color and no second mark. A right-click — or, where the pointer is coarse, a touch held still — raises that row's own actions at the pointer: the same list the session header's menu holds, built from one list so the two can never disagree, acting on the row it was raised on whichever session is on screen, and switching nothing, because switching stays the row's own press. Where the pointer is coarse the row draws its menu control at rest rather than on hover; editable text is left to the platform; and no gesture over a row archives, closes or deletes anything — a swipe across a row scrolls the list. While a row is hovered or holds focus its age is replaced in place by one word — archive, or unarchive on an archived row — in the slot the age holds, so no row changes height or width, and that is the row's only hover action. An archived row is the ordinary row at a dimmed ink; unarchiving returns it to its own project's list and drops the group's count, and the group and its count go when the last row leaves it. A closed session's row sits in the same group at the same dimmed ink and reads `Closed` in the age's slot, a state and not an action, with no action of its own; it stays readable and searchable until `Delete old data` removes it. A session can be pinned to the top of its own group from its row's actions and unpinned the same way: one pin and no second tier, pinned rows sitting above the daemon's order inside their group in the order they were pinned.

The list draws only the rows in view, plus six either side, once it holds 200 sessions or more, and stays flat below that; the threshold is read off the row count and never off a screen size. A group draws its first rows and puts the rest behind one row saying how many more there are, carrying the roll-up dot of the rows it hides; no session on screen in any view is behind that cut or hidden by a fold — its row is still drawn under a folded project's header at that project's own indent. What a project header remembers is the folds and not the opens, so a project attached tomorrow arrives open and a fold survives a relaunch. When the order actually changes every surviving row glides from where it was to where it now is, a new row fades in and a removed row simply goes; a change of height alone starts no motion and the first fill never animates. Holding the platform's own modifier stamps a small key-cap chip on the first nine rows in the slot the age holds and those chords open them as a row press does, and a press on a row with that modifier held opens it beside the focused view; the chips are there only while the key is held, never at rest, and never while a menu or an overlay owns the keyboard.

Long names truncate and carry the full value in a hover title, and nothing in the list overflows the track's own width. The list fades at whichever end is hiding rows, each end gated on its own, over a ramp derived from a row's own height. Where two attached projects share a name each header grows a dim parent-folder prefix, enough of it to tell them apart, with the full path in its hover title; a prefix never truncates and wraps above the name where the pair will not fit. A newly attached project's header appears at once; a project the daemon cannot attach takes its header away again and says why in one line in the picker that asked — a folder that is not a git repository reads `Could not attach: not a git repository`, because a project is a repository ([Spec-007 §Required Behavior](./007-repo-attachment-and-workspace-binding.md#required-behavior)) — with nothing else in the list redrawn. A refresh the daemon does not answer keeps every row the list already had, says in amber on the list's own header that it could not be refreshed, and counts no figure it could not read until the next answer lands. With no sessions at all the list reads `No sessions yet. Press New session to start with a chat or a project.` and names nothing else; with a query that matches nothing it reads `No sessions match "<query>".` and keeps its group headers drawn. Before the list's first read has answered, the top row and `New session` draw at once and the list reads `Loading…` after the one short delay. The list carries a foot line naming the keys it answers, in words.

##### The session screen

**Session views.** Every window is one kind and none is the main one: the rail, the sessions list and one or more session views side by side, each view one session's header, transcript, composer and its own right column under the pane-layout rule below. The views share the window's width, each at least the transcript's floor plus its open pane's floor, and when the window cannot hold every view at its floor the row of views scrolls sideways and keeps the focused view in sight; a new window opens with one view. A session shows in one view on this device at a time, and each destination in one window; asking for either again, by any route, brings its place forward and focuses it, and nothing draws a second copy. A plain row press acts on the focused view (§The all-sessions list); a press on a row with the platform modifier held, and `Open beside` in the row's menu, add a view with that session beside the focused one and focus it; `Open in new window`, in the row's menu and the session's menu, puts the session in a window of its own, taken out of the view it was in. `Open beside` is absent from the menu of a session already on screen, and `Open in new window` from the menu of a session that is its window's only view. A view's title drags the view: dropped among another window's views it joins them at the drop place, and dropped on the desktop it becomes a new window there; a window a drag leaves with no view closes. One view per window holds focus: a press anywhere inside a view focuses it, the Keyboard page's `Focus session on the left` and `Focus session on the right` move it, and every session chord, pane chord, the palette and find act on the focused view; moving focus between views changes nothing in either. While a window holds more than one view, each view's header carries a close control whose hover title and spoken name read `Close session view`, and the platform modifier with `W` closes the focused view, or the window when the view is its only one; closing a view or a window stops nothing, and its sessions keep running. `New window` — the platform modifier with shift and `N`, the File menu's row and the palette's — opens a window whose one view is empty, with the sessions flyout open and the new-session picker under it, and Escape there leaves the window on the sessions list. Wherever this section speaks of the session on screen, the open session or the session in view, it means the session of the view the rule acts in. The renderer places every view and opens every window, main's window-open handler building each one (§Main Process Responsibilities), so no wire call and no bridge member carries a view.

**The session header** names the session and carries, in order: the title, the pull-request word where a request is open on the branch, the review chip with its added and deleted counts, the preview chip, the terminal control, the session's own menu, and, while the window holds more than one view, the view's close. It is as tall as its own controls and the type beside them, identical on every platform, and nothing about the title bar moves it. All three pane chips are drawn at every moment on either session shape, each carrying one fact and never growing a second; one that cannot act reads as unavailable to assistive technology, takes the faint ink, carries its reason in its own hover title, answers no press, and leaves its chord doing nothing. Each pane has exactly one chord, a row on the Keyboard page like every other: the inspector opens on the platform modifier with `I`, the review pane on that modifier and shift with `G`, and the agents pane, the terminal and the Preview pane on the same pair with `S`, `T` and `B`; while the review pane's diff holds focus two bare keys step its files, the next on `J` and the previous on `K`, answering neither inside a text field nor with a modifier held. Every one of them is rebindable, and what a chord reads anywhere in the console is the binding in force rather than a written string. On a project session the review chip grays with its counts at zero while the tree is clean, reading `Review · +0 −0` with `Nothing changed in this worktree yet.` in its hover title, and goes live from the first change, the preview chip grays with `No page or dev server yet.` while the session has neither a page nor a discovered server, and the terminal chip grays with `This session has no shell.` once every shell has ended; each goes live again the moment its subject comes back. On a chat session all three gray at every moment whatever the session has written, each naming in its own words what converting the session to a project would light, because a chat keeps files rather than changes and has no repository for a shell: Review's reads `Chats keep files, not changes. Convert to a project to review changes.`, Preview's `Chats have no page or dev server. Convert to a project to preview one.` and Terminal's `Chats have no repository for a shell. Convert to a project to open one.` The command palette lists each of those acts either way, carrying the chip's own sentence while it is grayed.

The pull-request word is a clickable word in the header's own color and font — no box, no border, no glyph, underlined on hover, answering focus and Enter as it answers a press — drawn only while a request is open on the branch and absent the moment none is, never grayed, because absence is the rule everywhere outside the pane chips. The word follows a change made on the site within a minute: while the header is on screen the daemon sends the site a conditional request every 60 s, which costs no quota when nothing changed, and it reads again at once after the person's own git and pull-request acts and the events that change the request. A press opens the review pane on its pull-request tab; where the branch carries more than one request the word reads the newest. Diff counts render with the minus sign and never a hyphen, and an untitled session's title reads `New chat` or `New session`, faint and italic.

The header carries no location chip, attach chip, status dot, goal item, model chip, run-status text, held-note count, port, agent chip or agents badge: the held notes live in the composer's attachment row and the agents badge in the composer's footer strip. It carries no sessions control either, except the one back control the one-column layout puts at the head of the line, which shows the sessions list and is absent side by side. The session's menu is the session's own actions, built from one list with the row's right-click menu and, where the pointer is coarse, the menu a touch held still on the row raises: `Rename session…`; `Open inspector` with its chord; `Open beside` and `Open in new window` where they apply; `Pin to top`, or `Unpin` on a pinned session; `Mute notifications`, or `Unmute notifications` on a muted one; `Convert to project…` on a chat session alone; `Send to the cloud…` on a project session whose provider carries cloud tasks; `Copy link`; a rule; `Archive session` and `Close session…`. `Mute notifications` silences the session's `Finished` and `Failed` notifications on every device and every way they leave the machine, never its `Waiting on you` or a workflow's Notify step; the bell keeps every line and counts every waiting entry, and muting takes down the session's `Finished` and `Failed` banners still standing. Mute and unmute act in place through `session.mute` and `session.unmute` and switch nothing. `Copy link` puts the session's address, `sidekicks://session/<id>`, on the clipboard as one plain line — the one form the copy, the main process's link handler and `sidekicks open` share — acting on the session it was raised for and switching nothing, and its row reads `Copied`, or `Could not copy`, for the one transient duration before the menu closes. `Copy link` is the one control that copies a session's address: the inspector has no fact for it, because its `Address` fact is the session's inbox. The inspector item reads as opening the inspector and never as worktree details. `Send to the cloud…` is absent on a chat and a terminal session and on a provider or pinned build without cloud tasks; where the provider has them but this session cannot use them, it stays listed with its reason and refuses in place: `Cloud tasks need a ChatGPT sign-in on this account.` on Codex, `Cloud sessions need a claude.ai Pro, Max or Team sign-in on this account.` on Claude Code, and `Cloud tasks need a GitHub remote.` on both. Pressing it arms the composer for one message: a line at the composer's top, above any attached files and the draft, reads `Next message runs in <provider>'s cloud`, with `Cancel` at its end, and on Codex `Cloud environment` (the labels this account's tasks already name, or one typed, kept per project) and `Attempts` (1 to 4, Codex's own bound); Send then goes to the cloud and not to the agent, the message landing in the transcript as the person's own, the agent never receiving it, and the composer disarming, with nothing drawn over the transcript. Under that message sits the task's row, `Cloud task · <state>`, the state only what the provider reported and in sentence case on both providers: on Codex `Pending`, `Ready`, `Applied` or `Failed` (Codex's error, with its own message under it); on Claude Code `Submitted`, for the task's whole life, with `Open on claude.ai`; no row reads `Running`, `Finished` or a percentage the provider did not report. A ready Codex task's row carries `Show diff`, which opens the attempt's diff in the review pane as a read-only source, `Attempt <n> of <m>` where it ran more than one, and `Bring back`, which takes a file checkpoint through the daemon's checkpointer and then applies that attempt to the session's folder; the row then reads `Cloud task · Applied`, or on a partial apply `Brought back in part · <n> skipped · <m> in conflict` with every path listed, and `Undo bring back` on it puts the files back from that checkpoint through the same dry run and question as `Undo to here`.

**The pane layout** holds the session column and the pane tracks. The right column holds exactly one main pane — the inspector, the review surface, the agents pane, the Preview pane or the file view — and, under it, optionally the terminal. Opening a second main pane replaces the first. The terminal replaces nothing and nothing replaces it: opened while a main pane is up it stacks beneath that pane, opened with nothing up it fills the column, and a main pane opened while it is alone goes above it and leaves it where it is. Closing the main pane leaves the terminal alone in the column; closing the terminal leaves the pane. There are never two main panes, and the column carries no tab strip. A pane moved into its own window (§Detached panes) leaves the column, so another pane can open there; while it is away its chip, its toggle and its chord bring its window forward, the chip's hover title reading `<Pane> · in its own window`, and a pane returned from its window lands in the column under this same rule. That window is the pane's whole width, so it carries no full-width control. One entity opens one pane, structurally: a single mount point re-focuses the pane an address already has rather than opening a second. A pane opens by expanding its grid track in flow, never by overlaying, and panes are separated by a one-pixel inset line rather than a border. Opening the review pane, the terminal or the Preview pane collapses the sessions track, and nothing restores it.

Review, the terminal and the Preview pane can each take the full width, collapsing every other track; while one holds it its control reads as pressed and its hover title flips to the wording that gives the width back, and a second press or Escape returns it without also closing the pane. Changing which pane the slot holds gives up full width first. Every pane carries a drag edge of its own, and the sessions track carries the same seam: the pointer drags it, one arrow grows and the other shrinks it by one nudge of the current text size and by five with the shift key, and home or a double-click resets it; a separator reports its current size and its two limits on every set. The terminal stacked under a pane carries a second, horizontal edge with the same shape and the same keys turned on their side, and it opens on the lower third of the column, each half clamping where it stops being readable. The grab zone is wider than the line it draws — inside it the pointer becomes the resize cursor and a hairline brightens under it — while the drawn line itself never moves or changes width, so nothing on screen shifts. A drag suspends transitions and holds the transcript's follow until the pointer is released, and the width persists only on release. Every pane clamps at the narrowest its own content still reads at, and the session column keeps the floor its header must hold; a pane narrows itself to keep that floor when the sessions track or the notification list opens, when a pane opens and when full width comes off, not only on a drag. The column has one width and the stack shares it: the pane on top sets it, and the terminal beneath takes it, its own minimum applying only while it is alone in the column.

Every pane clips its own overflow, scrolls in one named inner scroller and never overflows horizontally. Every scroller but the transcript draws its scrollbar over its content and takes no layout width for it in any state: the bar appears while the region is scrolled or hovered, fades when it stops, and its thumb drags. The transcript draws none, its right edge being the ask rail's. A wheel or a drag inside a box that scrolls scrolls that box, and only once the box is at its own end does the gesture reach the transcript behind it; the transcript keeps its own overscroll, never bouncing the window and never passing the scroll outward, with the platform's own scroll anchoring off so a picture typesetting three screens up cannot rock the column under the reader. Each pane loads its body the first time it is opened and stays loaded, so reopening rebuilds nothing, and a body that fails to load reads as one line naming that pane with a retry beside it. Every one-shot read a pane makes takes a cancel signal, and a pane cancels its own reads when it closes or when a newer read replaces them, so a diff nobody is looking at stops being decoded and drawn.

Each half of the stack reads every standing pane rule on its own. A shell that ends while the terminal is stacked closes the terminal half with its one line and leaves the pane above it where it was; a main pane whose subject goes closes itself and leaves the terminal alone in the column. The terminal control is the one control that opens and closes that half, each pane's own close closes its own, and nothing joins the header for the stack.

**The session's other work is read where it is acted on.** A goal shows only as the system messages that set, update, complete or clear it; a run is paused and interrupted from the working line; a dispatched agent is read in the agents pane, opened from the agents badge on the composer's strip; the worktree the session works in is the strip's worktree switcher and the inspector's facts; a waiting request is answered on the composer's card; and a finished plan or a file a chat wrote is a row in the inspector's artifacts section, a plan opening in its reader and a file in the file view.

**Banners sit under the session header**, above the pane note and the surface, in the shape §Meridian, the design language item 9 fixes: each carries its own dismissal, blocks nothing, and enters no attention count. Six conditions draw one. A session whose provider started without part of its settings names the file and the line or the key the provider itself reported — never a guess — and offers to open that file in the editor the General settings page names; it goes on its dismissal for this start of the provider, or the next time the file loads cleanly, and a later start still on defaults draws it again. A provider process that ended on its own under a running session names the provider and the exit code the daemon read, and offers a restart, which starts the provider again on the same session and lets the waiting messages go as the next turn. A provider build this console cannot work with names both versions and offers the Providers settings page. A provider build that changed under a running session names the old and the new version and offers a restart, leaving the running turn untouched until the session is restarted. A session whose folder is lost reads `<folder> can't be reached · new runs wait until it's back`, or `<folder> is a different repository now · new runs are stopped`, and that banner also goes on its own when the folder is back. When the window cannot save how its panes are arranged, the banner reads `Pane layout not saved · it will save again on your next change`; the arrangement stays on screen and is saved again on the next change, and the failure's code goes to the window's diagnostic capture, never onto the screen. One more line sits where the banners sit and is not one of them: while the window repairs a hole in what it received, or a read it depends on has failed, it reads `Catching up…`, appearing only once the repair has run 0.4 s and then staying at least 0.4 s, and it goes when the missing entries have arrived; nothing else on the screen repeats it. A repair read that fails turns it to `Couldn't catch up · Try again`, and `Try again`, the window's refresh on focus and a reconnect each read again. It shows no technical cause; the cause goes to the window's diagnostic capture.

**A message between two sessions is two rows, one in each transcript**, drawn in the console's own tool-row shape — a verb column, the target, and a trailing cell — and faint: the sending session's row stands open at rest, as every call with a body does, and the receiving session's row is collapsed. The sending session's row reads `Message  to @builder · s-8f21`: the other session as `@name` with its id after it, and in the trailing cell the state, which says no more than happened — `sent` when the call returned, `delivered` once the message reached the other session, `queued, builder is paused`, `held by builder`, `refused: <reason>` in the provider's own words, and `not delivered` where the other session went idle without it. A send to a Codex session the person typed in a terminal, once the service it lives in has been away longer than that provider's own reconnect allows, reads `not delivered` with the reason on its second line, `Reopen it in your terminal: codex resume <thread id>`, and no `Retry`, since sending again cannot reach it. The receiving session's row reads `Message  from @planner · s-4a10` with `replied` in the trailing cell once it has, and it sits where the message was folded into that turn rather than at the turn's end. Opened, each row carries the message with the reply under it, and the receiving one additionally carries `14th since you last wrote` as a fact and not a limit. A message that carried files draws on **both** rows the file chips a sent turn's own attachments draw, one per file. This is the **one** row on the screen whose verb is the console's own word rather than the provider's name for the tool, because the tool's own name is code-shaped, and it takes the generic glyph at the muted hue rather than a mark of its own; nothing on it is drawn as code and no name on it runs words together. The row is the same line on either provider, drawn from one daemon-side tool event, so nothing about it changes with which provider sits at either end ([Spec-014 §Sessions Talking To Each Other](./014-multi-agent-orchestration.md#sessions-talking-to-each-other)). A send is an ordinary tool call under the sending session's own permission level: at a level that asks the ordinary approval card comes first, at `Sandboxed` and `YOLO` nothing asks, and no gate of the app's own applies; what the message causes in the receiving session follows that session's own level. Two system messages belong to the same surface: an interrupt taken while an exchange is live reads `Turn interrupted · exchange with builder ended · 2 messages not delivered` in both transcripts, and a paused session carries one faint collapsed line, `3 messages waiting · paused`, which opens to the list of them and goes as the continue delivers them in the order they arrived.

**Finding inside the open session** is one chord and one box, drawn on whichever surface has focus — the transcript, or the shell where the shell holds it — on that surface's top-right corner over the content, with no scrim and nothing under it moving, while the transcript and the composer stay live. The box holds its field, focused on open with its text selected and kept for as long as the session stays; the count of matches, reading `N of M` or `No matches`; the previous and next controls; and its close control. There is no scope note, because the count speaks for the whole session. What is searched is the visible text of every row — the person's messages, the agent's replies, the tool rows and the system messages. Each match is marked where it sits, the one being read is marked as current and scrolled into view, and Enter or the step chord moves forward, shift-Enter back, all three wrapping at the ends. The count counts the whole session rather than the rows on screen, so stepping past the last loaded hit loads the history that hit sits in and moves the transcript to it, on the person's own press and never by itself; the count and the reach are the daemon's search over the session's own rows, never a renderer-side walk of what it does not hold. Stepping to a match opens the run group holding it first, so the marked line is never inside a fold. The chord pressed again while the box is already open re-focuses the field and selects what is in it rather than opening a second box. Escape closes the box, removes every mark and returns focus where it was.

**Searching every session** is a second surface, opened by the palette's `Search all sessions` and drawn the way the palette is: one field over the session column, and under it the hits grouped by session — the session's name, then its own matching messages, each hit carrying enough of its words to be recognized, with the matched letters marked the way the palette marks them. It reaches every session the list holds, the archived ones included, in the daemon's own ranked order and with no ceiling on how many come back. It answers its own chord as well as the palette row. Choosing a hit switches the screen to that session and lands on that message, loading the stretch of history it sits in on the way. This is the only surface that searches the text inside sessions; the sessions track's own field searches the rows in the list (§The all-sessions list). Both reads are the daemon's ([Spec-001 §Interfaces And Contracts](./001-session-core.md#interfaces-and-contracts)), and neither box walks rows the console does not already hold.

A region of the session screen that has nothing to show, has lost its subject or failed while drawing says so in the shapes §Meridian, the design language item 8 fixes.

##### The composer

The composer is the chrome every session view carries, sketched under §Signature Feature Composition Sketches. It is a floating card at the transcript's own content width, docked one gutter's gap off the bottom of the session column, and it holds — top to bottom — the hairline, the setup card while a clone or a worktree's setup runs, the working line, the approvals card while one waits, the composer proper with its command list, its attachment row, its draft field, its send control and its toolbar, and the session strip. The jump control sits above the card inside the dock while the follow is released. It addresses the session's own agent and carries no machine picker; the one other thing it can address is another session, which an `@` in the draft names (§Session Composer). Files reach the attachment row three ways, and the page code never holds a path in any of them: the attach control's picker, whose picked files come back from `native.showOpenDialog` as tokens; a file dropped on the card, which the page hands to `native.getDroppedFileRef`, the preload reading its path and handing it straight to main, which mints the token; and a picture pasted into the draft, whose bytes the page hands to `native.savePastedImage`, main writing them to a temporary file and minting its token. `session.attachmentAdd` takes the token and refuses a raw path string, and main's relay turns the token into the path the daemon needs. What its parts show, offer and refuse is in that sketch.

##### Pane kinds

Six kinds. The session's own flow is the `transcript` kind, scoped to its session. Beside it the right column holds exactly one main pane at a time or none — `inspector`; `diff`, which is both the review surface and the read-only file view; `agents`, the agents pane; and `browser`, the previewed page — with `terminal` able to stack under whichever one is up, and filling the column when none is. Opening a second main pane replaces the first, and which main pane a session had open, and whether the terminal sat under it, is remembered per session. A pane moved into a window of its own leaves the column, so another pane can open there (§Detached panes). Nothing else is a pane. Each pane's heading reads its title — `Transcript` for the `transcript` kind, `Review` for `diff`, `Preview` for `browser` and `Sidekicks` for `agents` — and every pane announcement, the transcript feed's accessible name among them, speaks that title exactly as the heading shows it, never the kind id and never a second lowercase spelling. The Workflows destination is a page of its own with tabs, and one workflow run opens at its own address inside that page, so neither the builder nor a run is a pane kind ([Spec-015](./015-workflow-authoring-and-execution.md)). There is no approvals pane, no runs pane and no artifact pane: the one waiting approval, an agent's question and a plan waiting for a verdict are each the single card in the composer, one at a time; pausing and interrupting a run are the working line's own pressable words and a message sent mid-turn is the transcript's last row until the agent takes it; and a plan opens in its reader over the session column, a picture or a document in the quick look, and a file a session wrote in the `diff` kind — scoped to that one file in the review surface on a project session, and as its own lines in the file view on a chat. The inspector is the session's own, and a repository's, workspace's or worktree's changes open the `diff` pane — no pane kind exists for those entities and the set is not widened for them. A pane-layout snapshot of an unknown version is discarded whole, an unknown pane kind is dropped and reported, and a pane reference that fails validation is rejected.

- **`transcript`** — the transcript surface, scoped to its session. One flow per session, all but the one on screen hidden, capped at the single width every row of it reads with a gutter either side, its rows one derived gap apart whatever they are. It is the one scrolling surface in the conversation: no run group, tool body, diff or output scrolls inside itself, nothing in it is cut at a height of its own beyond the cuts a long diff and a long output take, and nothing scrolls sideways — code wraps, a table wraps its cells and a diff line wraps. The flow fades into the header at its top edge over one line height and nowhere else. Older history comes in as the reader nears the top, one stretch per gesture so a fling cannot pull in a whole day, with a line at the top of what is loaded for the case the automatic one cannot see; newer rows page in as the reader nears the bottom without the reading position moving. Only the rows near the window are drawn, with a band above and below kept ready so a fast scroll never shows blank space, and old rows leave memory in cuts that never take a row on the screen or the row under the reader. A session long enough to reflow is held blank while its row heights settle and shown in one piece, with a failsafe so it can never stay blank; a session opened before mounts at the heights its rows really had last time. It has two position aids: the ask rail at its right edge, and the jump control above the composer. A run group is drawn open, as the transcript draws it, and stays open until a person closes it — by its own header, or by the palette's fold-every-run act; nothing chooses the state a run arrives in, and no setting bounds how many stand open at once. Every way of being sent to one row takes the same path — the row is loaded if it is not on the page, the run group holding it is opened first, the flow lands on it, and it washes faintly in the accent for one duration and then fades; a row that has gone says so in one line instead of moving the page. The row kinds themselves, and their states, are [Spec-011](./011-live-timeline-visibility-and-reasoning-surfaces.md)'s; §Transcript View composes them.
- **`inspector`** — the session's own details, as wide as its widest label-and-value pair reads at the current text size, with a header carrying a back control, the kind and the name, `Open in new window` and a close control, over its own scrolling body. The chord that toggles it gives the slot back to whichever pane was open before when it closes it, while the pane's own back and close controls empty the slot. Its kind word is `Worktree` on a project session and `Workspace` on a chat, and wherever it is the open pane it re-keys to the session on screen. On a chat the repository fact reads none beside `Attach a repo`, which promotes the session in place, keeping its history and its managed workspace, and that session's inspector carries no branch, no base and no snapshots. Its sections, in order:
  - **Identity** — repository, path, branch, base, mount, created, provider, account, notifications and address. The account fact reads which account the session runs on and offers no switch, because which account a provider's work runs on is set on the Providers settings page and moves every session on that provider that is not pinned to an account ([Spec-025 §Moving a session to another account](./025-provider-accounts-and-credential-homes.md#moving-a-session-to-another-account)). The mount fact reads `Healthy`, `Can't be reached` or `A different repository now`, from the same `health` the lost-folder banner reads, so the two never disagree ([Spec-007 §Fallback Behavior](./007-repo-attachment-and-workspace-binding.md#fallback-behavior)). The notifications fact reads `Muted` or `Not muted` and has no press of its own; the mute is changed from the session's actions list. The address fact carries `Copy address`, which puts the session's inbox on the clipboard as one plain line — the address a Claude Code session in the person's own terminal writes to when it messages this session ([Spec-014 §Sessions Talking To Each Other](./014-multi-agent-orchestration.md#sessions-talking-to-each-other)) — and is not the session's `sidekicks://` link.
  - **Memory** — the `Auto memory` switch, reading `On` or `Off`, over one row per memory file or folder the provider reported for this session: on Claude Code the memory paths its first frame names and the project's `CLAUDE.md` and `CLAUDE.local.md` where they exist, on Codex the memory store under the account's home. Each row is the path in mono with `Open`. The section says which account home it reads, because an account's store is its own and never the person's `~/.claude` or `~/.codex`. The rows come from the daemon's `session.memoryRead` and the switch is `session.autoMemoryUpdate`: Codex applies it at once, and Claude Code when the session's process next starts, so a press mid-run reads `off when this run ends` or `on when this run ends`. `/memory` opens the inspector on this section and `/pause-memory` presses the switch.
  - **Hooks** — read-only, from the daemon's `session.hookList`: on Codex one row per hook the provider reports for the session's folder, naming the event, the handler in one word and the file it came from, with one dimmed row per error or warning in that report, in Codex's own words, so a hook skipped for want of trust or a file that failed to load is named rather than silently missing; on Claude Code, which reports no hooks, one row per file hooks live in — the project's settings files, the account home's settings file and each plugin's hook file — each with `Open`, under one line: `Claude Code does not report which hooks loaded; these are the files it reads.` The daemon's own hooks are not listed. `/hooks` opens the inspector on this section.
  - **Rules** — the approval rules in force for the session and its project, read from where the providers keep them, one row per rule reading what it allows or blocks and its scope — `Allow pnpm test · this session`, `Block api.example.com · this project` — each with a `Revoke` that confirms in place, and a session holding none reads `No rules yet`; it reads and revokes through the rule list and the rule revocation of [Spec-010 §Interfaces And Contracts](./010-approvals-permissions-and-trust-boundaries.md#interfaces-and-contracts). A session rule's revoke applies from the next call on both providers; a project rule's revoke removes it from the provider's file. Every standing rule on the machine is the Providers settings page's list, read from the same files.
