  - **Cost** — `Copy` on its header, which puts the section's rows and its total on the clipboard as plain lines; then one faint uppercase header per provider the session spent on, the session's own provider first, each over one row per account that provider spent on — the account named as the account fact names it, then tokens, then dollars — and, under a provider whose voice calls ran, one `Voice` row reading their minutes and dollars; a `Subtotal` row only under a header holding two or more rows; then `Total`. The figures are the daemon's cost receipt by account, each in the one money format and none marked as an estimate, and a request on a model the price list does not carry yet is held with its exact tokens and joins the rows and the totals when the next fetch prices it. The section ends in three rows the person can set, standing after `Total` and, on a session that has spent nothing, at the foot of the section under a `Total` reading `$0.00`: `Max steps · Unlimited`, that session's own bound on a turn; `Spend limit · Unlimited`, dollars for the whole session across every provider and account it uses; and `Tokens per run · Unlimited`, input and output together for one run. Typing a number sets it for that session and empty is `Unlimited`; they are the only figures in the section that are not a reading of what was spent, and the values every session starts from are the Runtime settings page's. When a turn reaches the step number it ends there and one flow row reads `Step limit reached · 40 · Continue`, and the run is interrupted; the press is a send into that run, returning the same run to `running` — as a message arriving from another session does too ([Spec-003 §The Step Bound On A Turn](./003-queue-steer-pause-resume.md#the-step-bound-on-a-turn)). The two other limits are held by the service's own spend count, which feeds this section, and the service stops both providers itself at the first usage report past a limit, so one request can overshoot slightly. Nothing appears while a limit is unlimited or not reached; a reached limit lands as one system-message row, like `Goal set`, never a banner, a modal or a composer refusal. `Spend limit reached · $20.00 · Raise limit` stops every running turn in the session, the lead's and the helpers', as an interrupt does, with one row in the lead's transcript; a message sent while the limit stands still lands in the transcript, no turn starts, the same row lands under it, and the draft is never lost. `Token limit reached · 500K · Raise limit` ends that run, and the next message starts a new run with a fresh count; a helper's run is its own, so a helper that reaches it stops alone, its child row reading `stopped · token limit reached`, and the lead goes on. `Raise limit` opens this section's field, and saving a higher number carries the stopped turn or run on, as `Continue` does for the step limit. The app writes these rows itself, like `Step limit reached`.
  - **Context** — the tokens used against the model's window with the ring's own percentage, then one row per bucket, a deferred bucket drawn dimmed, and on a Codex session the line `Codex reports totals, not what is in them.`
  - **Changes** — the session's own `+A −D` on its header, one row per changed file with that file's own `+A −D`, and `Open in Review` under the rows, which scopes the review surface to that file, or `Nothing changed here yet.` where there are none.
  - **Artifacts** — each finished plan a `Plan` row with its first heading, its time and its state word, and each file a chat session wrote a `File` row that opens the file view on it, or `Nothing made here yet.`
  - **Runs here**; **Snapshots**, on a project session only — a snapshot row carries `Restore`, whose press rolls the folder back and lands one flow row, `Restored to <snapshot name>`, and a rewind from a message lands `Restored to before <the message's first words>` instead; and **Actions** — `Attach a repo` on a chat, and `Open in editor`, `Open in terminal` and `Reveal in Finder` on a project, the first opening the session's whole working folder in the editor Settings names.

  Every `Open` on a path the daemon served — a memory path, a hook file — goes through the same open-in-the-editor member every other surface uses, `native.openInEditor`, with the file reference main's relay minted for that path, so the page never holds a raw path. The only controls in the inspector that change anything are the memory switch, a rule's revoke, a snapshot restore and the cost section's three limit rows, each one press or one number rather than a form; everything else it offers opens something elsewhere — a path in the editor, a file in the review pane, a plan in its reader — or, in the case of `Copy address` and `Copy`, puts lines on the clipboard. It offers no settings form, no model switcher, no token graph and no raw event dump, and nothing in it overflows its own width: every long value truncates and carries a hover title. **The console's own caches are bounded from this machine's memory rather than by a count.** The files it has cached, the color spans the daemon computed and the pictures it has loaded each carry a share of the machine's physical memory, handed to the renderer once at start — pictures at their decoded size, each decoded straight to the size it is drawn at (`createImageBitmap` with its resize options) and at the screen's size when opened full size, 1/64 of it and at most 256 MiB, cached files 1/512 (16 to 64 MiB), color spans held packed 1/2048 (4 to 16 MiB) — the least recently used clean entry going first when one is reached; the trim runs only while nothing is happening on screen and no turn is live, it never takes the rows a child's view or its parent is showing, and nothing about any of it is shown — no figure, no control, no notice. That run-time budget is a different thing from the fixed targets in §Budgets, which are the bars every console change is measured against.

- **`diff`** — the one pane kind both the review surface and the read-only file view use: a repository's, workspace's or worktree's changes open it from the inspector or from a row in the transcript, and a file a chat session wrote opens it read-only on that file's own lines with its version stepper and `Compare`, which redraws the body as the changes between the version in view and the one before it. What it shows is [Spec-009](./009-gitflow-pr-and-diff-attribution.md)'s and [Spec-012](./012-artifacts-files-and-attachments.md)'s; this spec fixes that both are the one pane kind, that the review surface is that pane with the ship strip and the pull-request tab composed on top rather than a modal, a route or a pane kind of its own, and that the review surface opens on no chat session, because a chat keeps files rather than changes. The ship strip is one state machine: a state line read off the daemon's facts, one next act — commit, push or open the pull request — and one alternate, a pull while the branch is behind standing in place of whatever the state would otherwise offer. The review surface's diff is one list at line granularity — every file header, every notice, every gap and every code line is a row of it — and only the rows on screen are built, a bounded number of them each frame however large the diff is; a folded file contributes no rows at all, so folding every file empties the list at once, and a line's coloring happens off the path that builds its row.
- **`browser`** — the session's own previewed page. Two page hosts, one path to them, and no debug port on either. On the machine that executes the session the page is real — a native `WebContentsView` in its own persistent partition inside the window, or the daemon's headless Chromium where no desktop is open there — and on another device it is a live picture of that page and nothing else, with the address line reading `Runs on <machine>`. One resolver in the daemon returns the session's browser context, and nothing above it branches on which host is live ([ADR-034](../decisions/034-embedded-browser-for-preview.md)). On the desktop, main attaches Electron's in-process debugger to each Preview view and to no other page, and carries its messages on the connection the app dials to the service (`preview.pageTargetReport`, `preview.pageDebuggerSend`, `preview.pageDebuggerReport`); the daemon's relay joins them into one browser whose target list is exactly the Preview pages, never a console window. The relay's page-session filter is a hard requirement: on a page's session only the few target commands that page needs for its own children pass, and every other target and browser command is refused, so no page reaches the console's own interface. The headless host is launched with Playwright's `chromium.launchPersistentContext` over Playwright's own pipe with no listening port, from an installed Chrome or Edge where there is one and otherwise from Playwright's Chromium, fetched once on first need, which the transcript shows as one flow row before the call goes on. A page's title, address, history and load state are read from whichever host is live, from the debug protocol's `Target.*` and `Page.*` events, and a page's reported title or address changes only when it really changed. The live picture on another device is a screencast the service produces on whichever host runs the page, through a debugger session of its own with focus emulation on, so a page covered by another window or shown behind a sleeping display still gives frames and takes clicks; it runs only while that device has the pane open.

  The pane's header carries `Open in new window`, the full-width control and close; under it an address line runs back, forward, reload, the address control, the size the agent holds as a chip — `390 × 844` — with `Fill pane` beside it, or `Offline` while the agent holds a network condition that stops the page's traffic, both together reading `390 × 844 · Offline`, the live width while the pane's edge is under the pointer, the machine line on another device, the marking tools with the stroke-hue slider under the pen, and last the hand-off that opens the same address in the system browser, drawn only while there is a page to hand over. A size the agent set is not locked: pressing `Fill pane` returns the page to the pane's size and the chip goes, and the agent learns its size was dropped the way it learns of any other page change. The person has no control that drops the agent's network condition; it lasts until the agent changes it or its session ends. Back and forward are always drawn and grayed in place from the page's real history rather than from a count the pane keeps. The address control edits in place, navigates on Enter, keeps typed text while the page updates under it, and takes an http or https address, a bare host, a loopback host and port, a bare port, an address literal or a path against the current page; anything else is refused in the pane's own one-line refusal naming the exact cause, and it never searches. Where the service had to carry a dev server's port to the next free one because its own number was taken, the address reads the port it loads with a faint note naming the one it replaced, `127.0.0.1:5174 (5173 was in use)`. Its page tab strip appears only at two or more pages, one tab each with the page's icon, its title falling back to the host, its port on a loopback page and its own close control, drawn like the terminal's strip and activating the same way, reordering by drag with a thin accent bar marking the slot it will land in — the slot at the end of the strip included — and a canceled drag leaving the order as it was, and closing on a middle-click as on the control. While a page is loading a small turning mark takes the place of its icon on its tab, and with one page open, where no strip is drawn, the reload control turns instead; the mark goes when the page answers. A typed address navigates in place rather than minting a tab, and a page's own pop-up request opens as another tab rather than an operating-system window; several discovered servers are several pages, one tab each, and the pane opens on the page that was used last. A loopback address printed in a tool row's output or in a terminal shell opens this pane at that address on a plain press, while the same address written in the agent's own prose opens the machine's own browser on a plain press and opens here on the platform modifier; every other address opens in the system browser. The body holds exactly one of four states: the empty rectangle the desktop positions the real page over, the live picture on another device, the discovered development servers as rows naming each server, its framework where the owning process's command line names one, its loopback address, and `started here` on a server this session started — or the one line saying none is running in this project — and the load failure, naming the host that did not answer, with `Try again`. With a tool pressed the body shows the page frozen into a picture under the marks layer: the capture is taken first and the view hidden second, never the other way round, the page is reported not visible for the whole frozen state so no rectangle report can bring the live page back over the picture, and the picture's scale is read from the returned image rather than from the platform's reported scale factors. Comments drop a numbered marker with a note, boxes are dragged with a note, the pen draws freehand in the color on the slider and keeps it; comments and boxes take the accent and never the waiting color; a mark is removed from its note and a stroke from its own close control; clearing and sending the marks exist only while a mark does; and releasing the tool returns the live page with the marks kept. A tool pressed with no page open does nothing, because there is nothing to freeze; a Box or Pen gesture that never moves leaves no mark, only Comment acting on a bare click; and switching pages or closing one while a tool is pressed deselects the tool. Sending them stages one attachment chip in the composer's attachment row and focuses the draft, the attachment carrying the picture with the marks drawn in, and per mark its number, note, element reference and box — a stroke its points and its color — with the address and the width; the sent picture carries no removal affordances and no unresolved color token. A mark's element reference is taken at the moment it is committed and is valid only for the snapshot that minted it. The pane reports its own screen rectangle, and the part of it still visible against everything that clips it, on every frame the pane moves or resizes, so the page never lags the frame it sits in; an overlay that covers the visible part hides the page, because that is the one place an embedded page is visibly not a document element, and while a pane edge is under the pointer the page lets pointer events through so the drag never sticks on it. When a console window reloads or crashes, the app hides every page view in it until a fresh rectangle arrives, so a dead window never leaves a page painted. A Preview moved into a window of its own takes its page with it whole (§Detached panes). Zoom lives on the keys alone, per page, stepping the platform's own presets while focus is inside the pane and resetting to the default for a new page; every chord the console has bound is still the console's while the page holds the keyboard, and everything else the person types reaches the page. The console's own page never zooms and never navigates: those keys and the platform's back-and-forward gestures are swallowed everywhere outside this pane, and the text size stays the Appearance page's, which scales the type and nothing else. A right-click on the page raises a small native menu that main builds from the page's own context-menu event — never the page engine's own, and never one drawn in the renderer, which would hide the page — whose rows are `Back`, `Forward` and `Reload`; `Cut`, `Copy`, `Paste` and `Select all`; `Copy link` where the press landed on a link; and `Open in your browser`; a row whose act is not available where the press landed is absent rather than drawn dead. One saved site-data set per machine serves the pane, the agent's tools and a workflow's browser steps alike, so a login in the pane persists across restarts; the Browser settings page manages it — whether it is remembered, each site's `Sign in`, `Clear cookies` and `Forget`, and clearing all of it — and holds `Browser tools for sidekicks` and the browser in use. When one host takes over from the other, the daemon carries the saved cookies across and replays a site's local storage and IndexedDB by script; a login a site keeps in the platform's own authenticator does not cross, and the pane says so rather than dropping the login silently. A file a page saves opens the system save dialog on the machine's own desktop window, the Preview feature asking for it through the platform bridge and main owning the dialog and the file write, so the window gains no file access; on another device, on a headless machine and anywhere no person at that machine can choose, the download is refused with one line in the pane, `Downloads are off in Preview`: on the desktop main cancels it in `will-download` and reports the refusal to the service, the headless host is launched with downloads off, and on both hosts the service sends the line back. **Caps.** One browser per machine, one tool server per daemon, one renderer process per open page, and as many pages per session as the machine's own memory allows — no count stands between the person and another page. When available memory in main falls below 20 % of physical memory, and at least 1 GiB, the oldest page nobody is looking at is released, one every 1.5 s until the reading is back above it and never the page on screen, and the headless host does the same in the daemon; a page unseen for ten minutes is released and reloads on demand, the headless browser closes after ten idle minutes, the screencast runs only while another device has the pane open, and discovery ticks only while a project session is live. The service keeps each session's pages — address, title, icon, order, which is active and zoom — and `preview.pageList` marks a page `released`, on which main destroys its view: a released page reloads when shown, which is how headless pages, and every page after an app restart, come back, while after a service restart main's page views reconnect with nothing reloaded. The pane is never a web browser: no bookmarks, no download list, no developer-tools control, no search, no picture standing in for a real page on the machine that executes the session, no second pane open beside it, and no dialog, overlay, backdrop or scrim anywhere in it. Preview is no source of attention either: a page loading, a mark drawn or an agent's browser call is none of the things that wait on a person. **The agent's browser tools** act on that same page through one tool server — Playwright's own, `tools.createConnection` from the pinned `playwright-core`, hosted inside the daemon, one connection per session handed that session's browser context — identically on both providers: the session's one tool-server entry reaches it on a loopback route guarded by a secret of that session's own, passed to Claude Code through `--mcp-config` with `--strict-mcp-config` and to Codex in the conversation's own configuration at `thread/start`. `Browser tools for sidekicks` decides whether the browser tools are in that entry's tool list, the entry staying for the daemon's other tools, and the entry never carries `browser_run_code_unsafe`, which would run any code in the daemon's own browser tool process outside the provider's sandbox and the command approvals. Each call is a row in the transcript in words with its screenshot inline, gated by the session's permission level like any other tool; a call driven from another device names the machine in its row.

- **`terminal`** — the session's own shells, lease-gated over [Spec-002](./002-machine-registration.md)'s terminal write lease. It holds the person's own shells and nothing else: it never mirrors an agent's own command-line screen, because what an agent is doing is read in the conversation, as its working line, its tool rows, its diffs and its approvals. The pane opens wide enough for a command line and its output to wrap rarely and never narrows past the point one still reads at, with its own drag edge and a full-width control, and it is drawn on no chat session. Under the pane header sits a tab strip: one tab per shell the session has open, each carrying the shell's own title — its basename until the shell sets one — and its own close control, with a control at the end of the strip that opens another shell and makes it active. A tab's shell is the person's own login shell started in the session's own tree, so their profile, aliases, prompt and version managers are all there; where their shell names nothing that can be started the daemon falls back to the platform's default and says so in one line above the prompt. While `Simplify for a screen reader` is on, every shell this pane starts carries `CLAUDE_AX_SCREEN_READER=1`, so a Claude Code the person runs there reads the same way. A tab's close control ends that tab's shell with it — refused in place while an agent's running command holds that shell, and asked once in place first when another of the person's devices holds it — and a middle-click closes a tab as its control does; tabs reorder by drag, and a right-click raises a small menu with close, close-others and close-to-the-right, the last two grayed with their reason where there is no such tab — there is no close-everything row, because one press would end every shell in the session, a running server among them. The sub-line reads the active tab's project and tree, and the body — a fixed monospace grid one step of the type scale in size, with glyph shaping off and a line height that tiles so box-drawing characters meet with no seam — shows the active tab's shell. Each shell keeps its own control lease, so this device can hold one shell while another of the person's devices, or an agent's running command, holds another. The lease is per device, not per window, so a terminal moved into a window of its own keeps the lease this device holds and takes no second one. The lease line sits between the strip and the body and speaks for the active tab's shell alone: where another of the person's devices holds it, the line names that device and offers to take the shell, asking once in place, and the body is read-only and says so to assistive technology; where an agent's running command holds it, the line names the agent and offers to stop that run, and there is nothing to take. Switching tabs re-reads the line for the shell that tab shows. Closing the pane hides its shells and ends none of them — a build started in a tab keeps running and the pane comes back to it — and quitting the console ends none either; a shell ends when its own tab closes, and a shell that ended keeps its tab, its last line saying it exited and with which code, until that tab is cleared. The code that draws the character grid loads the first time the terminal is opened, not at launch: while it loads, and until the active shell's first frame has arrived, the pane reads `Loading the terminal…`, and if that code cannot load it reads `Could not load the terminal` with `Retry`. A shell that does not start reads `The shell did not start · <cause>`, the cause the daemon's own, with `Try again` at its right in the one try-again shape; the session's working folder going is the one thing that closes the whole pane, with its one line, because every shell in it has lost its subject. Output a shell prints while its tab is not drawn is held for it under a bound and shown when the tab is drawn again; a tab whose command ended off screen carries a small quiet mark until the person looks at it, and nothing rings for it. A session opens as many shells as the machine allows, and the only refusal is the daemon's own, written in place under the strip in plain words with the open control left where it is. A selection in the body raises a control that lands those lines in the draft as a fenced code block and moves focus there without sending, which touches neither the scrollback nor the lease, so a shell someone else holds can still be read and quoted. While the body has focus every platform-modifier chord is still the console's and every other key is written to the shell, Escape and Enter included; a key pressed while the view is scrolled back snaps it to the live bottom first. An address the shell prints is a live link in the body's own text: a loopback address opens the `browser` pane at that address and any other opens the system browser through `native.openExternal`, and the press moves neither the shell's focus nor its lease. The pane carries no repaint control — a program whose frame has gone wrong is redrawn by moving an edge a step, which reaches it as a real resize — and no export: what a person wants out of it leaves as a selection. The pane is the same on either provider — the same tabs, the same keys, the same lease and the same controls — and nothing in it belongs to one provider and not the other. Opening it draws a tab for each shell the session really has, drops the tab of a shell that had already gone, and opens a fresh shell only where the session has none; switching session swaps the whole strip for the one that session owns, each shell keeping its screen and its scrollback rather than being redrawn. Focus follows the act: opening the pane puts the cursor in the active tab's shell, the open control puts it in the new tab's body, closing the active tab activates its neighbor, closing the last shell closes the pane and lands focus on the header's terminal control, closing the pane returns focus where it was before it opened, and a shell opened from another of the person's devices lands as a tab without taking focus. A paste of any size reaches the shell whole and in order, the daemon writing it in pieces under its own write bound, and where the program in the shell asked for paste marks the whole paste is wrapped in them before it is split, so the program reads one paste; the bound is the daemon's and no figure lives in the pane. A right-click inside the body does nothing, because copying and pasting there are the keys' own acts; shift with Enter writes a line feed rather than submitting, which is how the provider command-line tools take a newline; and the cursor is a filled block while the shell has focus and an outline of that block when it does not, and never blinks.
- **`agents`** — the agents pane, its tree of every agent in the order the work happened and the child view inside it, composed in §Agents Pane View.

##### Detached panes

Any side pane — the inspector, Review, the agents pane, the terminal, Preview and the file view — can leave its session's view for a window of its own and come back. One act moves it: `⌘⇧A` on the open side pane (with the terminal stacked under a main pane, the one holding focus, and the main pane when focus is in neither), and `Open in new window`, a pop-out control in every side pane's header beside full width and close, drawn the same way on each and carrying no text; pressed in the pane's own window, `⌘⇧A` returns it. There is one window per pane per session: the act on a pane already in its own window brings that window forward. The pane leaves its session's right column, so another pane can open there; while it is away, its chip, its toggle and its chord in the session's view bring its window forward instead of opening it a second time, and its chip's hover title reads `<Pane> · in its own window`. The window carries the pane's own header with `Return to session` where `Open in new window` stood, reading `Returning to session…` while the return is under way, and its close, which is the same return. In its own window the pane answers exactly as it does in the column, less full width, since the window is the pane's whole width already. The window is titled `<Pane> — <session title>`; the pane header is its drag region, with the macOS window controls in the header's inset; it opens at the pane's default width and the display's work-area height, and refuses to shrink below the pane's own floor.

A pane's own window belongs to its session and to no window: it stays when the window it came from closes or that view shows another session, and it ends only by its return, its close or a quit. A return puts the pane in its session's view under the one-main-pane rule — a main pane replacing the main pane there, the terminal stacking beneath it — and where the session is on screen nowhere, the return opens the session in the focused view of the window used last, with the pane in its column. Its place and size are kept per pane kind, pulled back on screen when the display it sat on is gone, and a pane that was in its own window when the app quit comes back in its own window at its kept place, from the detached state the pane-layout snapshot keeps.

One renderer drives every window. The renderer opens the pane's window with `window.open`, and main's window-open handler (`setWindowOpenHandler`) hosts the child in its own `BaseWindow`, built from main's own options; the renderer copies its stylesheets into the child and renders the pane there, sharing its stores and daemon subscriptions. The child keeps the renderer's sandbox, isolation and content security policy, has no preload and no bridge of its own, and nothing crosses the bridge for a detached pane; main pairs the two closes, so closing the window closes the child and the child's end closes the window, and no detached window is ever in Preview's relay target list. A crash of the renderer takes every window with it: main closes them, and the reloaded renderer reopens every window, its views and each detached pane from the kept layout, as after a restart. Chords are when-scoped, so each window answers the chords of what it holds; an app-wide chord pressed in a pane's own window acts on its session's view where that view is on screen and brings its window forward, and otherwise acts in the window used last. The desktop's banner shows while no console window is focused, and appearance reaches every window. The terminal's lease is per device, so a detached terminal holds no second lease.

Preview's page moves with its pane, whole: main moves the page's view out of one window and into the other (`removeChildView` on the window it leaves, `addChildView` on the one it joins), with no reload — the same page, socket and debugger link, the agent's session and the screencast carrying on, a size the agent holds refitted to the new pane — and the pane reports its rectangle through `browser.publishPaneRect` against the window that holds it. The page's owner window becomes the new one, so focus aims there. Closing a window never closes the pages in it, so on every return main puts the page back in its session's view, and on a quit it closes it; a pane detached when the app quit comes back with its page `released`, its stored address, order and zoom kept.

A pane's own window is an arrangement of this desktop: another device and Remote Control see no change, and the session, what each pane shows and the terminal's lease are the same.

##### Settings

Settings is where the machine is configured — the accounts that pay for the work, the tool servers, the projects, the browser, the keys, the look, the notifications, the background service and the devices linked to reach the sessions — and nothing on it makes or runs work; a rail destination does that. Every page is one projection of what the daemon and the main process already hold: the screen recomputes nothing they report and keeps no second copy of a fact, so a page whose write is refused renders that refusal and keeps the value it had rather than pretending to save, and a reading the daemon cannot settle is drawn as unsettled — an account it cannot decide about reads as undecided and never as signed in. The console carries no identity of its own on this screen or any other — no sign-in gate in front of the first session, no organization or workspace step, no avatar, no security section, and nothing that offers to carry an earlier workspace across — and the only sign-in any page offers is a provider account's, on the Providers page. A passkey, the credential a browser or a phone uses to link itself, is listed and removed on the Devices page alone. Nothing on the screen authors an agent, sets how a diff reads, or holds a preference for an editor inside the console: an agent's instructions are written on the Sidekicks screen, the review surface keeps its own reading controls, and the editor preference on General names an outside editor.

Two panes, one page at a time. The left pane is the page list with its search field above it and the entries below in a fixed order; the right pane draws exactly one page. The search field finds controls on every page over the same matcher the palette uses: every word typed must appear in a control's own label, the heading it sits under or its one-line hint, hits rank on the label first, then the heading, then the hint, with ties in page order, each hit reads its page and heading — a control under no heading reads its page alone — and choosing one opens the page and lands on the control. A page that splits its own pane in two — the server list and the selected server's detail on MCP servers — is that page's own business and not a third pane of the screen. The pages are rendered always and in order with the open one marked as the current page to assistive technology, so a page whose service is unreachable keeps its entry and says on the page what is missing — a list that shrinks teaches a person the setting does not exist. The list is a roving-cursor list whose cursor opens the page it lands on: an arrow, home or end moves the cursor and draws that page at once with no second press, and the cursor wraps from the last entry to the first and back, so the keyboard and the eye walk the same order. Which page is open lives in the route — the screen, a page, and a page with a selection — so a deep link and a click on the list are the same act and the back control works; the selection is handed to the page untouched and narrowed fail-closed against that page's own vocabulary, so an unrecognized selection opens the page for nothing rather than refusing to open. Three surfaces deep-link in: the worktree setup card's `Edit setup steps` link opens Projects on that project, the composer's tool-servers control opens that session's own server list, under which the single link `Manage in Settings` opens MCP servers, and a run refused for its account opens Providers on the account the refusal names. Arriving from any of them finishes the journey rather than landing at the top: the page opens, a folded section holding the named control is opened first, the control is scrolled into the middle of the view and lit briefly and then quiet again, and a control the page is not drawing leaves the person on the page rather than refusing the link. Where the window is too narrow to hold both panes at once — the break being the width the two panes' own content needs at the current text size, never a device class — they become one screen at a time, the list alone and a page with a back control above its title that returns to the list. Reopening Settings opens the page that was last open. The rail and its attention control are the same here as on any other screen, and the sessions track sits collapsed.

Every page opens with one line saying what it is and where its values are kept; on another device that line names where — a device's own pages say they are kept on that device, and the machine's pages name the machine. A control commits as it changes: the screen's only save-and-revert pair belongs to the Projects page's worktree draft, which is a real draft, so closing Settings commits the field being edited and never that draft, and the one other exception is a secret typed into a masked field — the web address and the mail password on Notifications — each submitted by its own `Save`. A control whose value differs from its default is visibly marked. A control kept in the machine's settings file draws only once the file has been read, so it never shows a value it does not hold and no changed mark shows before then: until the read answers its row reads `Reading this machine's settings…`, and a read that fails reads `This machine's settings could not be read.` with `Try again`; every such reading line appears only after the console's one short delay and only while nothing it stands for is drawn, so a read that answers at once draws no flash of words. A refused write rolls the control back to the value it had and renders the daemon's own refusal in place, in the refusal grammar's inline shape, and an act that settles without changing what is drawn says so in one line on the page, in flow; there is no toast anywhere on this screen. Every switch carries its role, its accessible name and its state, answers Enter and Space, shows the one focus mark, and sits in a press target derived from the text size; a switch grayed by one of the named exceptions keeps all three and says why in its own hover title. A sign-in that does not finish — a provider account's or a tool server's — says so where it was started, leaves what it was signing in to exactly as it was, and keeps the control that starts it again. Credential material reaches this screen only as a write-only field. What this install looks like stays on this install: the theme, the scheme, the text size, the transcript width, the page last open and the keyboard map are kept for this machine and no page offers to carry a preference to another one, nothing exports or imports the screen's own values, and nothing resets the screen in one press — a reset is a page's own, and the Keyboard page's is the only one.

The ten pages, in that order:

- **General.** What this install is and how it updates itself, and what a new session starts from. The page opens with the running version and, beside it, the platform, the architecture and the language, each as the app reports it. Then the update block, which draws the whole path as five numbered steps — `Checked`, `Downloading`, `Checking the signature…`, `Ready to install`, `Restart to finish` — with the step it is on marked and the ones behind it ticked: idle names the newest version this machine has seen and offers `Check for updates`; found names the version and the date it was released and offers `Download`; downloading draws its percentage over a bar; ready says so and offers `Restart to finish`, which restarts the app at once with no confirm and no line about work in flight, because a restart stops no session, run or shell: the background service keeps them running through it. Under the line naming the version found sits one fold, `What changed`, carrying that version's notes in plain sentences, shut until it is pressed and drawn in every arm that names a version and in none that does not. A verification failure reads `The update could not be verified and was not installed.`, with `Try again` as a faint clickable word at the right end of that line. An update a previous run staged and never applied is reported in the same block — the update status's `staged` member, set beside whichever arm the block is in and filled at launch from the updater's cached pending update — naming the staged version beside the version this install is on, and it stays there until an update lands; while an update is staged or ready to install, the rail's Settings button carries one small dot. An install that cannot update itself draws no update button: it says so in place and names what gets the newest version — on an install a package manager put there, that package manager's own update command, and on a macOS copy running outside a writable folder, moving the app to Applications. Below the block: `Check for updates automatically`, on; `Keep crash reports`, on, under `Reports stay on this machine, with personal data stripped.`, the one control for it anywhere in the console — a report is built, stripped of personal data and kept on the machine that crashed and is sent nowhere, any of the person's linked devices reads it through Remote Control (`daemon.crashList`) and `sidekicks crash list` reads it from the command line, and with the switch off no report is kept; `Start a new session on the last model and effort used`, on, carrying those two values, under `The permission level is never carried over. A new session always starts at YOLO.` and one line naming the model and effort that would be carried — with the switch off, or on the first session ever, a new session starts on the first usable signed-in account's provider and that provider's default model, and the automatic-compaction bound is not remembered here either, staying the provider's own on Providers and the session's own behind its context ring; `Keep this Mac awake while a sidekick is working` on macOS and `Keep this computer awake while a sidekick is working` on Windows and Linux, off, under `Closing the lid still puts it to sleep.`, with nothing added to the rail for it — with it on, the background service holds the machine and its screen awake while any agent works and lets go when none does, so the hold lasts exactly as long as the work, with the app open or quit, and it stops only idle sleep, never a sleep the person asks for. The hold is the service's own, tied to its process, through each platform's own tool: `caffeinate -d -i -w <daemon pid>` on macOS; on Linux `systemd-inhibit --what=sleep:idle --mode=block` over a process that ends with the service, for sleep, and `org.freedesktop.ScreenSaver.Inhibit` held on the service's own session D-Bus connection, for the screen; on Windows, the service on Windows or in WSL 2 alike, the service's Windows half calling `SetThreadExecutionState(ES_CONTINUOUS | ES_SYSTEM_REQUIRED | ES_DISPLAY_REQUIRED)` on its own thread and clearing it with `ES_CONTINUOUS` alone; `Editor that opens files`, one preference every open-in-the-editor act in the console honors, listing every editor the console looks for — Visual Studio Code and its Insiders build, Cursor, Devin Desktop, VSCodium, Sublime Text and Zed, then Xcode, the JetBrains IDEs, Nova, BBEdit, Emacs and Notepad++ — each found through the operating system's own register of installed apps and never through the command path, because an app started from the Dock or the Start menu has no command path of the person's to search, on Linux the desktop entries in the system's application folders, backed by each editor's own launcher paths for an install that adds no entry — an entry matches an editor by its desktop id, a JetBrains entry by its `StartupWMClass`, or its `Exec=` program, the id match wins, and a `NoDisplay` or `Terminal` entry is skipped while another matches; detection on Windows is measured there before it ships; and Devin Desktop is one entry, found under its own identifiers and under the Windsurf ones an install that has not updated still carries, and reads `Devin Desktop` whichever it finds; main reads that list through `native.listEditors()`. `System default` leads the list and holds the value until someone changes it; an editor this machine does not have keeps its place dimmed and unpickable and reads `(not installed)`; and an editor chosen here that later goes missing reads `That editor could not be found. Pick another, or files open in the system default.` on its row, keeps its place, and lets the open land in the system default rather than failing. Last, `Default checkout for a new project session`, the two places the worktree switcher offers — `A new worktree`, the default, or `The checkout the project already has` — under `A choice made when a session is created always wins.`
- **Providers.** Signing in to Claude Code and Codex, which account new work runs on, and what each provider does on its own; the page's opening line says every sign-in and pasted token stays on this machine. On a Windows computer with at least one WSL 2 distribution, one row above both provider sections, `Where Claude Code and Codex are installed`, reads the place the background service runs in, `Windows` or `Ubuntu (WSL)`, with each provider's version there, `Claude Code 2.1.271 · Codex 0.156.0`, under one line, `Your sidekicks use the Claude Code and Codex installed here.`, and on every other computer the row is absent; Providers is the only page that names a side or a distribution, and only where the place is its subject. Where the row is drawn, every provider line that says this machine names the place instead, reading with Ubuntu in use: `Not installed in Ubuntu.` with `Install in Ubuntu`, which runs the provider's Linux installer inside the distribution and reads `Installing Codex in Ubuntu…`, and, where the other place has the provider, `Codex 0.156.0 is installed on Windows. Install it in Ubuntu, or pick Windows above.` beside the same button; Codex's shared-service line, `Runs one Codex service in Ubuntu while it is on, so your sidekicks can message a Codex session you typed in an Ubuntu terminal.`, grayed `Codex is not installed in Ubuntu.`; the terminal plugin's line opening `Adds /sidekicks to Claude Code in an Ubuntu terminal.`, grayed `Claude Code is not installed in Ubuntu.`; `Command`'s `Where this provider's command lives in Ubuntu. It is checked again when the path changes.`; `Standing rules`' `one row per rule this provider holds in Ubuntu`; `Import sessions from Claude Code`'s `Reads the conversations Claude Code already keeps in Ubuntu and adds them to the sessions list. Sessions imported here are copies; import again to bring in what the tool added since.`; `Import memories from your own Claude Code home in Ubuntu`; and `Remove`'s confirm, `Remove <email> from this list? Its folder in Ubuntu is left untouched. Sign-in history and spend go with it.` With Windows picked the same lines read `on Windows` and `a Windows terminal`. `Change…` lists every place with what was found in it, read when the list opens — `Windows · Claude Code 2.1.271 · Codex not installed`, `Ubuntu (WSL) · Claude Code 2.1.271 · Codex 0.156.0`, `Debian (WSL) · Checking…` — a WSL 1 distribution dimmed with `Needs WSL 2`; picking another place confirms in place and moves the service and what it keeps — sessions, agents and what they remember, workflows, settings and signed-in accounts, each account's sign-in file moved and never copied — while project folders stay where they are, and the computer stays one machine to every linked device. A move that fails puts everything back and offers `Try again`; where the place in use has gone from the computer, the row reads `Ubuntu (WSL) · No longer on this computer` under `Choose where Claude Code and Codex are installed.`, and a pick confirms under `Cancel` and `Start there` with `Use the Claude Code and Codex on Windows? The background service starts there with nothing in it; Restore… on Runtime brings back your latest backup.`, the last clause only where a backup exists. The move's progress, settled and failure lines are [Spec-006 §The service on a Windows computer](./006-local-ipc-and-daemon-control.md#the-service-on-a-windows-computer)'s. Nothing is asked at first launch: the service starts where both providers are found, then where one is, then on Windows, and where that place has neither provider, the flow row a session that cannot start draws reads `Choose where Claude Code and Codex are installed` and opens this row. Then one section per provider, each in two halves: the settings that provider exposes at the provider level above the accounts registered against it. The provider's half reads whether its command is installed and signed in and which version, or `Cannot tell right now.`; a provider not installed reads `Not installed on this machine.` with `Install`, on every platform, which runs that provider's own documented installer once, as the person, where the service runs, with no administrator step, no terminal and no input, and `Cancel`, the installer's own timeouts bounding it, and a failure shows the installer's own reason with its command under it as text to run by hand. Under that: `Command`, one field that reads `Auto-detect` while empty — looking on the service's own path, then the login shell's, then where each provider's installer puts its command — and is checked again when it changes; `Available for new sessions`, which every provider can turn off; `Helpers at once`, empty by default, which is no limit; `Standing rules`, one row per rule that provider holds on this machine, read from its own files, each reading what it allows or refuses with its scope (an account home or a project) and the file it came from, and `Revoke` confirming in place and removing the rule from the provider's own file, which the provider follows as it does on its own (`provider.standingRuleList`, `provider.standingRuleRevoke`) — the inspector's `Rules` reads the same files for one session, and the line under the section's rows reads `Applies from the next tool call.` under Claude Code and `Applies to conversations started after this.` under Codex; `Protected paths`, read-only, each entry naming where it came from; `Compact automatically at <N>% full` in both providers' sections, moving in steps of five between the lowest bottom stop and the highest top stop that provider's models report and mapped onto the provider's own knob; under Claude Code, `Output style`, whose choices and their one-line descriptions come from the installed build's own style listing and which the daemon applies to sessions started later as a flag setting that writes no file, `Advisor`, `Off` and then the advisor models Claude Code offers, under the line `Applies to sessions started later. A session already running changes its own advisor from its composer.`, kept in the app's own settings file and never in Claude Code's, which a session takes when it is created and keeps from then on, and `Use Sidekicks in terminal Claude Code`, off, which installs the app's plugin and one setting in the person's own Claude Code settings and, turned off, removes only those; under Codex, `Reach Codex sessions started in a terminal`, on, under `Runs one Codex service on this machine while it is on, so your sidekicks can message a Codex session you typed in a terminal.`; and `Import sessions from <provider>`, under `Reads the conversations <provider> already keeps on this machine and adds them to the sessions list. Sessions imported here are copies; import again to bring in what the tool added since.`, reporting into one progress row on the page. The registry itself — what an account is, how it is signed in, how its billing mode and its per-limit quota are kept, which account a run starts on, and what pressing an account does to the sessions already running on that provider — is [Spec-025](./025-provider-accounts-and-credential-homes.md)'s; §Provider Accounts And Cost View composes both halves and states what the page shows and refuses. The current-account mark also moves from any screen by the `Cycle accounts` chord the Keyboard page holds, as [Spec-025 §Moving a session to another account](./025-provider-accounts-and-credential-homes.md#moving-a-session-to-another-account) describes.
- **MCP servers.** The settings-level governance page for this machine's tool servers, and the only place in the console where one is added, removed or governed: a server list on the left of the page pane and the selected server's detail on the right. The governance rules it renders — the inventory, per-tool overrides, the authorization it never re-derives, and the events it reads — are [Spec-024](./024-mcp-server-configuration-and-governance.md)'s; §MCP Servers View composes the page and states what it shows, collects and refuses.
- **Projects.** Where cloned repositories go, every project attached to this machine, what runs after a worktree is made, and the environment rows every process starts with. `Clone new repositories into` sits first: one folder with `Change…`, which opens the platform's own folder chooser, reading until it is set the daemon's own answer (`repo.cloneFolderRead`) — the folder holding the most recently attached project, or the home folder when there is none — the same answer the session picker's clone line reads. An `Every project` section follows, holding the environment rows every process the console starts is given and `Branch names`, the pattern a new worktree's branch is named by, `sidekicks/{session}/{title}` until the person changes it, checked by git's own rule and against every branch in the repository and refused in place with nothing written. Then every project attached to this machine, one row each, opening in place; on a Windows computer with WSL, a project whose folder is on the other side's disk carries `This folder is on another disk, so sidekicks read it more slowly.` An opened row carries, first, `After creating a worktree`, the steps that run after a worktree is created — the files to copy, the commands to run in order, and the time limit they are given — behind the screen's one save-and-revert pair; they run as soon as the tree exists, at every level, with the repository's own git config, its hooks included, and no approval card, and the setup card is their record: each step in order with its status and the elapsed time, then `Worktree ready`. That project's own environment rows sit directly beneath those steps, a name set there winning over the row of the same name above it and saying so on itself, then `Branch names for this project`, empty unless set and overriding the `Every project` pattern when it is. Either environment list refuses at save, in place and writing nothing, a name that is not an environment variable name, a credential-shaped name — a provider key, or any name ending in `_TOKEN`, `_SECRET`, `_KEY` or `_PASSWORD` — and a name the console sets itself on the processes it starts; the rows are ordinary configuration and read back plain. Beside them sit four actions on the attachment: rename, one field on the row that Enter applies and Escape leaves, which edits the display name and moves nothing — not the folder on disk and not the project segment of any worktree path, so a renamed project's worktrees and the sessions standing in them are untouched; open in the editor General names; archive, which takes the row out of the sessions list's grouping and moves it to an archived group at the foot of the page, its sessions kept and one unarchive action on it; and delete, grayed while an agent is running anywhere in the project with its hover title naming the running session — the pattern a worktree's own removal control uses — and otherwise behind a confirm in place that says its sessions stay, the folder on disk stays, and the act cannot be undone. What the steps do, what an environment row reaches, and what attaching and detaching a project mean are [Spec-007 §Required Behavior](./007-repo-attachment-and-workspace-binding.md#required-behavior)'s and [Spec-008 §Required Behavior](./008-worktree-lifecycle-and-execution-modes.md#required-behavior)'s; this page is where they are written.
- **Browser.** What the browser inside the console remembers, and whether agents may drive it. One saved site-data set serves this machine — the cookies and local storage, and the console's own visit list — shared by the `browser` pane, an agent's browser tools and a workflow's browser steps alike, with no per-session partition, so a login survives a restart. The page holds: `Remember site data`, on; the sites with saved data, one row per registrable domain reading the host, `Saved cookies` where it holds at least one unexpired cookie or `Saved site data` where it holds other data and no cookie, and when it was last used — never whether the person is signed in, because a saved cookie proves no login and a login kept in the site's own storage holds none — with the one act that row's state allows beside `Forget`, and nothing offered in a state where it could only refuse: `Sign in` on a `Saved site data` row, which opens that site in a page of its own that belongs to no session, over the machine's one site-data set and showing the address line alone, so what the site saves is kept for every browser surface, and reads the list again when that page closes; and `Clear cookies` on a `Saved cookies` row, which clears the site's cookies across its registrable domain, parent-domain cookies included, and leaves its other data and claims nothing about a sign-out; `Forget`, per site and never per session, the site's full erase of its cookies, storage, cache and service workers; `Clear all site data`, confirming in place and naming how many sites go with it; `Browser tools for sidekicks`, on, which decides whether the browser tools are in the tool list of the one tool-server entry the daemon hands each session, the entry staying for the daemon's other tools, rather than whether one call is allowed, under one line saying every call an agent makes still goes through that session's own permission level and nothing here loosens it; the browser in use — the app's own, an installed Chrome or Edge, or the one fetched on first need — with its version and, where it was fetched, when, and `Fetch it again`; and the self-hosted git hosts, one validated host per row with its own removal beside a field that adds another, where adding one asks the installed, signed-in `gh` and `glab` which of them answers for it, so a self-hosted GitHub or GitLab lights the pull-request half of the review surface, and a host that does not parse, or that neither answers for, is refused in place with nothing written. With no site data saved the page says so rather than drawing an empty table. The page reads and acts through the daemon's `browser.siteDataList` (each site carrying `hasCookies`), `browser.siteSignIn {origin}`, `browser.siteCookiesClear {origin}`, `browser.siteDataForget`, `browser.siteDataClear`, `browser.chromiumRead` and `browser.chromiumFetch`. On the desktop the daemon clears through main, which owns the Preview partition — `preview.pageCookiesClear` for a site's cookies and `preview.pageSiteDataClear {origins?}` for `Forget` and, with no origins, for clearing everything — answered with `ses.clearData` per registrable domain, the one call that removes a login cookie set on the parent domain; on the headless host the daemon clears in its own browser context. The two switches are kept in the machine's settings file, which the daemon reads each time it launches the headless browser or a provider, and have no verb of their own.
- **Keyboard.** Every chord the console answers is a row on this page, and nothing on any other screen changes for it. The page carries its own search box above the rows, filtering them as it is typed and saying so where nothing matches, and the rows sit in four groups in one order — Panes, Sessions, Find, App — one row to an act, forty-two rows over forty-three keys. Panes holds `Review` on `⌘⇧G`, `Preview` on `⌘⇧B`, `Terminal` on `⌘⇧T`, `Sidekicks` on `⌘⇧S`, `Inspector` on `⌘I`, `Open in new window` on `⌘⇧A`, `Next file` on `J` and `Previous file` on `K`; Sessions holds `Sessions list` on `⌘B`, `Search sessions` on `⌘K`, `Search all sessions` on `⌘⇧F`, `New session` on `⌘N`, `Next session` on `⌥⌘↓`, `Previous session` on `⌥⌘↑`, `Focus session on the left` on `⌥⌘←`, `Focus session on the right` on `⌥⌘→`, `Open session 1` to `Open session 9` on `⌘1` to `⌘9`, `Previous ask` on `⌥⌘[`, `Next ask` on `⌥⌘]`, `Send message` on Enter, `Search sent messages` on `⌃R`, `Interrupt` on Escape and `Interrupt everything` on `⌃C`; Find holds `Find in this session` on `⌘F`, `Next match` on Enter or `⌘G`, `Previous match` on Shift-Enter and `Close find` on Escape; and App holds `Command palette` on `⌘⇧P`, `New window` on `⌘⇧N`, `Open Settings` on `⌘,`, `Open Workflows` on `⌘⇧W`, `Next waiting` on `⌥⌘N`, `Answer this run` on `⌥⌘↩` and `Cycle accounts` on `⌥⌘A`. Each of those is the default, and every row is rebindable. `Open in new window` moves the open side pane into a window of its own and, pressed in that window, returns it to its session (§Detached panes). **No chord carries two acts**, with one shape of exception: two rows may share one key where they are disjoint by state — Escape, where closing the find box answers only while that box is open and interrupting only when nothing is open to close, and Enter, where stepping to the next match answers only while that box is open and sending the message in the message box. A row carries the act's on-screen name, a one-line description of what it does — never a command identifier and never a raw when-clause — and the chord it holds now drawn as key caps; the rebind and reset controls are drawn only while that row is under the pointer or holds focus, and the reset only while the row is overridden, so a page of forty-two rows reads as names and chords rather than as a hundred controls. The page carries one whole-page reset naming how many chords are changed, in the singular where exactly one is. Rebind records the next press on the control itself while the rest of the keyboard is suspended, so a chord the console already uses can still be pressed; during a recording Escape leaves the chord alone and Backspace or Delete leaves that act with no chord at all, and one line under the search box says both. A rebind that settles says so on the row in one line, naming the act and the chord it now answers to, or that the act now answers to no chord at all. A key another act already answers to is refused on the row, naming the act that holds it. **A chord is modifiers plus exactly one key**, held in one spelling so two chords written differently are known to be the same and the refusal on a taken one can be trusted, and it answers only while the modifiers held are exactly its own — a finger too many defeats it rather than firing the shorter chord. `Cycle accounts`, `⌥⌘A` by default, is the one act read as a held chord: its modifiers stay down while its key repeats, it settles when they are released, and it is still one chord, recorded and refused exactly like the others; its row's one line says it moves a provider's current-account mark through that provider's saved accounts while the key is held and settles on the one under the mark when it is released. Where the platform reports a different character than the key that was pressed, the letter under the option modifier and the punctuation under the platform-and-shift pair are recovered from the physical key, so those chords still answer. Sending the message is a row like any other, holding Enter by default: while it holds Enter, Enter sends and shift-Enter starts a new line; while it holds the platform modifier with Enter, Enter starts a new line and that pair sends. The message box gains no control for it either way. **The grammar is when-scoped.** Each act declares once whether its chord reaches into a text field: inside a field only the chords so marked fire and every other key falls through to the text editing, and while a terminal shell or a previewed page holds the keyboard every platform-modifier chord is still the console's while everything else reaches the shell or the page. A chord answers only where its own act can act: a chord whose chip is grayed does nothing, and a chord whose surface is not open does nothing. The condition an act is live under is a typed condition tree built in code over a closed set of named contexts, never a parsed expression; conflict detection runs against those conditions, and the saved map holds only key combinations. Each window answers the chords of what it holds, and a chord's session view is the focused one. One line above the rows says that in the terminal the chords carrying the platform modifier act in the console and every other key goes to the program running there, and one line under them says that Claude Code and Codex keep their own keys and names the command each of them answers to for changing them — the console neither reads nor writes either program's key file, because a session runs over the wire where no key exists and a command a person runs in the terminal pane reads its own file itself. The page closes by naming the one key it does not hold, under `Keys this page does not hold`: `⌘W closes the focused session view; when it is the only view, it closes the window.` There are no two-stroke sequences, no keymap presets and no import or export of the map: every chord is one press, every row is changed on its own row, and the map arrives from nowhere but the person's own rebinds. Every chord drawn anywhere in the console reads the live binding rather than a fixed string. The map is local to this install and is never written to a wire. It is its own file, `userData/keyboard-map.json`, which main alone reads and writes through the bridge's `keyboardMap.read()` and `keyboardMap.write()`, the write returning the map as stored so the page redraws from what landed; it holds only the rows that differ from the chord they ship with, keyed by the act rather than by a position, so a row put back to its default drops out of it and a default improved in a later release reaches anyone who never touched that row, and an override for an act the console does not have is ignored when the file is read, draws nothing, and is left out the next time the file is written. It is written whole and atomically and readable and writable by this person alone, so a crash mid-save cannot leave the keyboard half-written; a missing or broken file reads as the chords the console ships with, main repairs it, and the page says both happened. It has no change feed: one renderer drives every window, so one reader holds the map. A development build's reload chords are compiled out of the shipped app and are no rows here, because in a shipped console there is nothing there to rebind.
- **Appearance.** How the console looks on this machine, kept for this install. Its first control is the theme, two swatches — `Meridian`, marked `Default`, and `Graphite` — each previewing live on press. Under it the color scheme's three cards, `System`, `Light` and `Dark`, `System` the default, each as wide as a theme swatch and painting a live picture of the console at a swatch's height, corners and edge, so the two rows read at one scale and wrap as the swatches do; then `Text size`'s four steps, `Small · Default · Large · Larger`, with `Simplify for a screen reader`, off, beside it; then `Transcript width`, a slider directly under `Text size` with the width it gives at the current text size read beside it, moving one root font size a step between the narrowest a transcript row still reads at and the widest a line of the agent's prose still reads comfortably, and returning to its default on a double-click of its knob. The theme, the scheme, the text size and the transcript width are main's appearance record: a change goes to main through `window.setAppearance`, which writes the record, and main stamps them on every console document it serves, so the first paint is right with no script and no read. The screen-reader switch draws the console flat — the panes lose their decorative borders and washes, no animation or transition runs, a tool call reads as one plain line of words, and the transcript reads as headings and paragraphs in order — and, because it reaches past the console, it is kept in the machine's settings file, where the background service reads it when it starts a terminal-pane shell and sets `CLAUDE_AX_SCREEN_READER=1` in those shells and in no other process. What each of them changes, and what each of them must not change, is described in §Meridian, the design language; this page is where they are chosen.
- **Notifications.** When the console tells the person something is waiting, and how a moment reaches them away from the screen. `Show a count on the app icon`, on. `Notify me outside the app`, on, which governs every operating-system notification on this machine, under one line saying the attention kinds are posted while the app is not in front and a workflow's Notify step is posted either way; beneath it four switches, each on and each with its one line — `Waiting on you`, `Finished`, `Failed` and `Notify steps`. A kind switched off stops that kind's banner on this machine and nothing else: the bell's list still holds every entry, the icon count still counts every waiting entry, and a banner already posted is still replaced and withdrawn by its moment. With `Notify me outside the app` off, the four keep their state and draw grayed, each reading `Turned off above.` Under the four, one line says a session muted from its menu stays quiet about finishing and failing and still says when it is waiting on the person. Where the operating system refuses the app's notifications the switches still draw, and the page says the system is refusing while the bell and the count inside the app keep working; on macOS main reads the permission through `native.getNotificationPermission()`, off its main thread (`node-mac-permissions`), where `authorized` and `provisional` are allowed, `denied` is the refusal this line draws, and `not determined` means not asked yet. Then the two ways a moment leaves this machine, both off until the person sets them up. `Send to a web address` posts one signed message for each moment of the kinds picked under it to an address the person owns, naming the session and its state and never what was said, only while no app window is in front on any of the person's devices, and a Notify step either way; the address is masked and write-only behind its own `Save`, sealed in the keychain and shown back only as its scheme and host, with its signing secret shown once, a test send and one status line. `Email me what I have not seen` sends at most one email per chosen period from the background service through the person's own outgoing mail, listing what waits on them and what finished or failed that they have not opened, never what was said, over an encrypted connection only; its password is masked, write-only and sealed in the keychain, with a test send and one status line. Neither sends a muted session's `Finished` or `Failed`. Nothing on the page makes a sound, there is no per-session list, and the daemon's own stored records are never rendered as further switches: the daemon keeps no notification preference of its own. The switches are kept in the machine's settings file — the kinds as `notifications.kinds.waitingOnYou`, `.finished`, `.failed` and `.notifyStep`, a missing key reading on — and describe this machine's screen, while another device keeps its own; the background service reads them once, when it writes each entry, and marks an entry whose banner a switch or a mute withholds ([Spec-017 §Default Behavior](./017-notifications-and-attention-model.md#default-behavior)). The mute itself is the session's: `Mute notifications` and `Unmute notifications` in the session's actions list (`session.mute`, `session.unmute`) silence that session's `Finished` and `Failed` on every device and every way out, never its `Waiting on you` or a Notify step.
- **Runtime.** The background service that runs the agents, the folders it can reach, what it keeps, and the port it listens on. The page shows what the supervisor reports about the service and edits none of it — whether it is running and since when, its version, and how much of the machine's processor and memory it is using, each reading stamped with the time it was taken, and, on Linux where no Secret Service answers, one line under the service's own, `Secrets are kept in <path>, readable only by you, because no Secret Service is running.`, `<path>` read from the status read's `secretsFile`, absent on every other machine — read through `daemon.status.read` when the page opens and again on `Check again`, and nothing samples it on a timer. A service that is not answering reads `The background service is not answering.`, and the page keeps its controls drawn rather than emptying. On Windows, where the service has written down why it cannot start — WSL not responding, its restarts used up with the error it gave, the place it ran in gone from the computer, or its connection's name held by another program — the page reads that reason instead, in words naming what failed and what to do and never the side the service runs on, each line quoted in [Spec-006 §The service on a Windows computer](./006-local-ipc-and-daemon-control.md#the-service-on-a-windows-computer), from the `cannotStart {reason}` member of the supervisor's `daemon.status` topic; and on Windows one line under the service says when it will not keep running while the person is signed out — their Windows password changed since `sidekicks daemon install --while-signed-out` ran, or the computer allows no service with nobody signed in, each line quoted in the same Spec-006 section — from the same topic's `whileSignedOut`. Beside the facts sit `Restart` and `Stop`, each confirming in place that work in flight stops — and where Codex sessions typed in a terminal are inside the shared Codex service, `Stop`'s confirm counts them (`session.terminalProviderSessionList`), because stopping the service strands them until the person runs that provider's own resume ([Spec-006 §Required Behavior](./006-local-ipc-and-daemon-control.md#required-behavior)) — and `Update the background service`, which the app's own update on General does not touch: it draws `Checking`, `Downloading`, `Checking the checksum…`, `Waiting for running work to finish` and `Restarting the service` over a bar, with `Cancel` until the app asks the old service to stop, and it never stops work to update — while any runs it waits, naming on one line, its parts joined by `·`, up to three running sessions and runs by title, each opening its work, then `and <N> more`, then the count of terminal Codex sessions — and it settles naming the old and the new version, or both versions and the reason where it rolled back. Below them: the folders this machine can reach, each with what is using it and its own removal — a project's folder removed by the same act and under the same rule as Projects' delete, each worktree the app made listed beneath its project, marked `worktree`, and removed by the worktree switcher's rules, and each kept worktree a row with its name, when it was removed and its size, carrying `Put back` and `Delete now`; how long diagnostic logs (7 days by default) and finished sessions (90 days) are kept; `Package cache`, the packages workflow steps download, with `Cache limit` (`Unlimited` by default), what each cache holds, and its clears, which never break a step already installed; `Delete old data`, the one act that deletes finished sessions past their bound; `Backups` — `Back up automatically`, off; `Back up to`, with `Choose…`; the last backup's line; `Back up now`; and `Restore…`, which lists the backups in the folder and, confirmed in place naming the work that stops, has main stop the service, replace its data with the backup's and start it again (`daemon.requestRestore(backupId)`), `sidekicks db restore <backup>` doing the same on a machine with no app — a backup being a plain copy that holds no credential, so another computer reads it directly and the person then signs in to providers and links devices again; `Export all data`, which writes everything this machine keeps for the person to a folder they pick, leaving out settings, credentials and keys; `Erase all data`, which confirms in place and removes everything the app keeps on this machine, its credential-store items included, project folders staying; the port a workflow started by a web request listens on; `Ask me after one start leads to`, at `100 runs` by default; `Stop a run after`, `No limit` by default; the memory cap for tool processes; `Max steps per turn`, `Spend limit` and `Tokens per run`, each reading `Unlimited`, what every new session starts from — no step limit being what both providers do on their own — under one line saying what each number bounds and that a session can set its own in its inspector; and the two diagnostic switches, recording traces and recording an event-replay log, the second naming the file it writes on the side the service runs. What each of those controls does, what each refuses, and that each value is set in one place and no second one are [Spec-006 §Required Behavior](./006-local-ipc-and-daemon-control.md#required-behavior)'s, and this page is the surface that shows them. No control on it turns a deletion on: the diagnostic logs are dropped past their bound, the package caches past a limit the person sets, and a finished session past its bound becomes eligible for deletion — eligible is not deleted — so `Delete old data` is the only thing on the page that removes a session. Diagnostics is not a page of its own — its three controls, the two switches and how long diagnostic logs are kept, are the whole of it — and nothing on this page reads the event log back: there is no recent-activity fold, no credential-attempt list and no audit view anywhere in the console. Nothing in the console inspects a stalled run, reads a failure's classification, requests a recovery action or reads the redaction policy, and no surface here claims those reads; [Spec-018](./018-observability-and-failure-recovery.md) owns the operations themselves.
- **Devices.** The person's computers and the devices linked to reach their sessions, the last page, at `#/settings/devices` and `#/settings/devices/<id>`, under `Computers and devices linked to reach your sessions remotely.` Its body is Remote Control's, contributed to the page list through the settings page registry, and it is present on a machine with nothing linked yet, because it is where the first device is linked. Four sections in one column, each opening with its line and, while empty, showing its line and its act alone. `Machines` holds a card per computer that runs the person's sessions: its name, `This machine` on this one, its operating system and service version — a Windows computer whose service runs in WSL reads as Windows, one card whichever side runs the service — whether it is reachable or when it was last seen, `Rename` in place, `Keep this Mac awake for your other devices` (`Keep this computer awake for your other devices` on Windows and Linux), off, under a line saying it holds only while the machine is plugged in and closing the lid still puts it to sleep, and `Remove`, confirmed in place. `Devices` holds a card per linked phone or browser: its name, its platform and app version, `Connected now` or when it was last seen, when it was linked, `This device` on the one in hand, `Rename` in place, `Linked from <device>, which you revoked.
