Revoke it if that device was lost.` on a device that a since-revoked device linked, and `Revoke`, confirmed in place, after which the card moves to a `Revoked` group carrying `Forget` and that device never links again. `Link a device` draws in place a code to scan and the same link as text with `Copy link`, good once for 5 minutes, then six digits both screens must show before `It matches` links the device. `Passkeys` lists each passkey with the platform that holds it and when it was added, with `Remove` confirmed in place; a passkey lets a new phone or browser link itself with no other device at hand, and `Add a passkey` is present in the web client and the phone apps and absent in the desktop app. `Shared ports on <machine>` lists the ports that machine shares with the person's other devices, the one address besides the workflow port that the console sets. A name set by `Rename` is the one every device shows. Nothing here reads a log back: the statement chain's statements are kept for the machines to verify and never shown as an activity list, and no other page lists a device or a passkey. How linking, revoking and the machines' trust work is [Spec-027 §Device registration and revocation](./027-remote-control.md#device-registration-and-revocation)'s, and the methods the page calls are [Spec-027 §Interfaces And Contracts](./027-remote-control.md#interfaces-and-contracts)'s.

##### Notifications

The notification surface composes the daemon's attention projection — items grouped by session, actionable split from informational — under [Spec-017](./017-notifications-and-attention-model.md). The list shares the sessions track at the same derived width and is shown in that track's place: the two lists are never open together, opening one closes the other, and only the open one's rail button reads as expanded. Its top row is its heading and its close control; its scroll holds a `Waiting on you` group under its own header with its count, then an `Earlier` group. A group with no entries is not drawn, and with no entries at all the list shows its heading and nothing under it, with no sentence. An entry is one line — a dot in the row's own state, the title, the state word and the relative time, newest first inside each group — and the whole line is the control: a session entry switches the screen for real, a workflow-run entry opens that run in Workflows, and neither closes the list. With two or more machines each line ends with its machine's name, and pressing a line on another machine switches to that machine and opens the session. Opening the list focuses its first entry and closing it returns focus to the rail's attention control; the chords that open the sessions track, the review pane and the terminal treat this list exactly as they treat that one. Only waiting entries are counted, and they are pinned above the rest: the count is the sum of what waits on every machine, an unreachable machine's as last read, so it always equals the lines the list holds, and the app icon's count is the same figure. Finished and failed entries are read and never counted, and an entry leaves the waiting group the moment its moment resolves — the daemon's projection, never the renderer's arithmetic. There is no dismiss (an item clears when the daemon resolves it), no re-filtering by preference in the renderer, no count on a finished or failed entry, no mark on an entry, no second badge, no overlay, and nothing said inside a session; a muted session keeps every line in the list and every waiting entry in the count. Outside the window, on this machine, the operating system's notification carries the moment. Whether an entry may raise a banner is decided once, by the background service, when it writes the entry: an entry of a kind switched off, one written while `Notify me outside the app` is off, and a muted session's `Finished` or `Failed` are withheld, and a withheld entry starts nothing. Main posts only the entries left pending, and only while no console window is in front, a workflow's Notify step either way; with the app not running, the service starts the app's own program with no window to post the banner, and that process exits. Each banner carries a stable id for its moment, so a later state replaces it in place rather than standing a second one beside it, and it is withdrawn when its moment resolves, a Notify step's never; no preference gates a withdrawal, and main honors the system's do-not-disturb (§Main Process Responsibilities). The web address and the email digest set on the Notifications settings page are the two ways a moment leaves the machine.

#### Fixtures

- **One app, two compositions.** At startup the app composes either its real boundaries or their fixtures, once, and nothing past startup asks which: no surface, service or store branches on a fixture flag, and a surface cannot tell which composition it is running on.
- **Where fixtures live.** The desktop app's `fixtures/` folder holds scenario definitions and scripted data only: one `scenarios/<scenario>.ts` per scenario, shared `data/`, and `index.ts`, the catalog. A fixture implementation sits beside the real boundary it substitutes — a service's fixture beside that service, a feature's fixtures inside that feature, a store's beside the store's owner — and answers the operations that boundary has and no others. There is no fake backend, no stand-in bridge answering by method name and no generic catalog of fixture methods, and no backend method is invented for a fixture. Fixture-only types stay in the desktop app and never enter `packages/contracts`.
- **The launch.** `--fixture <scenario>` starts the app on that scenario's scripted data with no daemon behind it and nothing running, and `--fixture <scenario> --session <session-id>` also opens that session; no argument is the normal app. Main reads the arguments and checks the name against the catalog before the first window is built, then hands the checked launch to the window as renderer switches (`webPreferences.additionalArguments`). The preload reads the switches and, in a fixture-capable build only, exposes the launch as the page global `__fixtureLaunch__` beside `desktopBridge`; `services/platform/live-bridge.ts`'s `readFixtureLaunch` reads that global, and `app/fixture-composition.ts`'s `composeFixtureLaunch` turns it into the composition `App.tsx` hands to `PlatformBridgeProvider` as its `composition`, so the provider holds no fixture branch. A launch that names a session writes that session's address before the first render, so the window opens on it as on any other address. An unknown scenario, a missing value, or a session the scenario does not hold stops startup with an error that names it; nothing falls back to another scenario. The arguments are the same through the development command and straight against Electron (`electron . --fixture transcript-states`), and the test tiers launch with them too. The pane-harness address, `#/pane-harness/<paneKind>/<sessionId>`, parses in every window: a fixture launch's composition registers the harness screen, through which the test tiers mount one registered pane body for that session, and a window whose composition registered none renders the address as not found, as it renders any other unknown address.
- **The catalog is these scenarios**, each named for the behavior it exercises and never for an incidental count: `multi-session-streaming`, which holds several sessions across more than one project, each with its transcript, and one reply that streams word by word; and the focused scenarios `first-run`, `concurrent-streaming`, `transcript-states`, `empty-session`, `waiting-for-input`, `approval-request` and `terminal-lease`, which serve development and the test tiers. A lost connection to the background service is not a scenario: its states are exercised against the real boundary that reports the service's status.
- **Which builds carry fixtures.** Fixture code and data are compiled only into a fixture-capable build, marked by the compile-time `define`-substituted identifier `__FIXTURE_BUILD__` — true in the development and fixture builds, false in a release build. A release build carries no fixture code, no catalog and no loader, so `--fixture` there is invalid input and a startup error like any other, never a silent normal launch and never a hidden demo mode, and scripted data can never be mistaken for real work. A runtime `process.env` gate would leave the code in the bundle and is not used (§Pitfalls To Avoid). The fixture flag means only that fixtures are compiled in: code meant for ordinary development alone reads Vite's own `import.meta.env.DEV`, and the scenario itself is chosen only by the launch argument — three separate things, never one flag. Main's hidden-window controls, through which the Electron test tiers launch a window that stays hidden or takes no focus, read a main-process define of their own, `__TEST_TIER_BUILD__`, true only in the `smoke` and `fixtures` build modes and never the fixture flag, so a development build's window is never hidden.
- **Developer tools exist in a development build only** and are compiled out of the shipped app, the same discipline the `browser` pane keeps in offering none of its own; they are no row on the Keyboard page, because in a shipped console there is nothing there to rebind.

#### Budgets

Named targets, measured from the first console commit, recorded in a dated endurance ledger, and gating every console PR. **Where they are measured.** The deterministic budgets — bundle size, heap at rest, steady heap, and first-transcript-row latency against the fixture's frozen clock — gate on any runner. The hardware-dependent budgets — frame time and CPU — are measured under one protocol: the built bundle in the desktop app playing the `concurrent-streaming` fixture scenario with the frozen clock, ten warm-up seconds discarded, the metric taken as the median of three runs, on the **pinned CI runner class** the desktop workflow names by label (the reference machine for gating purposes), with thresholds calibrated on that class at first landing with 25 % headroom, re-baselined only when the label changes, and one automatic re-run before a failure counts. The figures in the table are the product targets on a laptop-class reference profile — eight cores, integrated GPU, one 60 Hz display — which the endurance ledger records beside the runner-calibrated thresholds, so a runner regression and a product regression stay distinguishable. A budget that cannot be measured on the pinned class at all is never silently skipped. **Every figure in the table is a gate on the harness and none of them bounds the running console.** What the running console keeps — the files it has cached, the color spans the daemon computed and the pictures it has loaded — is bounded instead by the budget it works out from the machine's own memory, which the `inspector` bullet of §Pane kinds describes and which is never a figure written down here.

| Budget | Target |
| --- | --- |
| Renderer initial bundle | ≤ 450 kB gzip, excluding lazy chunks (terminal, node graph, math, diagrams, browser tools) |
| Frame time, four lanes streaming | p95 ≤ 16.7 ms on the reference machine |
| Renderer heap, one session open at rest | ≤ 120 MB |
| Steady heap, `concurrent-streaming` live stream | flat over thirty minutes; ≤ 250 MB renderer heap |
| Idle CPU, session open, nothing streaming | ≤ 0.5 % of one core averaged over sixty seconds; no timer fires except the refresh scheduler's deadline and the presence heartbeat |
| Streaming CPU, one lane | ≤ 15 % of one core on the reference machine; the reveal engine yields when the frame budget is spent |
| Terminal instance | ≤ 20 MiB at the default scrollback |
| Time to first transcript row on launch | ≤ 800 ms from window show, fixture mode |

#### Persistence on the renderer scheme

Layout, scroll position, selection, pins, and expansion sets persist per install; **drafts do not** — composer text, form values, paths, and code a user has typed and not sent are user-authored content, and the only durable homes the corpus gives such content are the daemon's PII-mapped stores ([Spec-020](./020-data-retention-and-gdpr.md)). A draft therefore lives in exactly one of those and never in window storage: it is daemon-held and session-scoped, saved a moment after typing pauses, restored on a session switch, on a restart and on the person's other devices, and cleared by sending it. The files staged beside it are copied to the daemon at staging time and held with the session outside the checkout, and a review's held notes live in the same store, so a half-written message or review reaches the person's other devices and sending needs no upload step.

What the renderer's own store holds is device-local convenience and nothing else: for every window, which session views it holds, their order and widths and which one had focus, and each pane that sits in a window of its own, one record per window keyed by the window id main's own place file shares; pane widths, the sessions track's own width, the stacked terminal's height, which pane each session had open and which child the agents pane was on, the review pane's scope and base branch per session, the reader's place in a comparison and in a session, the projects the person folded, which files in the review pane they folded, the expansion sets, and which settings page was last open. Two things a window needs before the renderer runs live instead in main's own files under `userData`, each written by main alone: where each window was — its place, size, display and maximized-or-fullscreen state, one entry per window, and a pane's own window per pane kind — and the appearance record, `userData/appearance.json`, which holds the theme, the color scheme, the text size, the transcript width and the current theme's light and dark grounds. Main paints each window and its console view from the kept ground before the first frame, and stamps the record on the root element of every console document it serves — the theme, the scheme, the scheme resolved to light or dark, and an inline style carrying the root font size and the transcript-width token, `--meridian-transcript-width` — so the first paint needs no script and no read. The renderer reads its starting values from that element, sends a change through `window.setAppearance`, which writes the record, and hears every change, the View menu's scheme pick included, through `window.subscribeAppearance`; the Appearance page writes through that member, the width as the one root token every session's transcript reads its own column width from (§Meridian, the design language item 4), and the renderer's store keeps no appearance value. Every read and write is wrapped defensively and the screen renders correctly when a store is empty or throws. Everything open at quit comes back after a restart and nothing more: every window where it was, with its views, their order and widths and which had focus, and every pane's own window at its kept place, so a restart never multiplies windows; a session deleted or archived since drops its view, and a window left with none shows the sessions list. A crash of the renderer takes every window with it: main closes them, and the reloaded renderer reopens every window, its views and each pane's own window from the same kept layout, as after a restart. A remembered pane is put back only where its subject is still there — a shell to draw, changes to read, a page or a discovered server to open, the agents list and the inspector always having one — and the memory is held until the daemon has answered about that subject: an answer that has not arrived, or one that fails, reopens nothing and erases nothing and is tried again when the daemon reports itself reachable, and an answer landing after the person has opened a pane themselves is dropped rather than replacing what they opened. Nothing is announced when a pane does not come back; the session simply opens with an empty slot. A remembered base branch that the daemon's own branch list does not hold falls back to the default base, and a store that is empty or unreadable opens the review pane on its changes scope against that default with nothing said. The remembered reading place in a session is the bottom where the reader was following it and otherwise the row they were on and how far into it, with the history around that row loaded to reach it; a place that cannot be found opens the transcript at the bottom. A saved window rectangle that is off every attached display is pulled back on screen, and the window is restored without first appearing at another size. Projections never persist and are re-derived on reconnect (§Pitfalls To Avoid). The durable store therefore holds **UI state only** — a value-class enumeration the persistence layer's schema encodes, and a write outside it (message text, form values, paths, code, names, anything user- or machine-authored) is a tripwire failure at the store's write chokepoint. That persistence lives in IndexedDB under an own per-session partition with LRU trim and a quota gauge, which exists on the renderer's custom scheme because the scheme is registered privileged (`standard: true`) before `app.ready` (§Renderer Bundle; Plan-020 I-020-6).

### Auto-Update Flow

1. Main checks for an update every four hours and at app start while `Check for updates automatically` is on, and whenever the person asks. It checks through `electron-updater` 6.8.9 with its stock GitHub provider, which reads the update files the release publishes, and refuses a release whose version is not above the last one this install has seen ([§Auto-Updater](#auto-updater))
2. On an available update, General reads `Update available — <version>, released <date>.` with `Download`. The download starts on the person's press and never on its own, and it is a block-map delta where the platform has one — NSIS, the macOS zip once a previous update is cached, and AppImage — and the whole file otherwise
3. When the download lands, `electron-updater` checks its SHA-512 against the release's update file, and on Windows the installer's publisher (`Checking the signature…`)
4. The update is ready, `Ready to install — <version>. Restart to finish.`, carried to the renderer on `update.subscribe`. On macOS the platform's updater is handed the file only when the person presses `Restart to finish`, and never before the check has passed. An update a previous run staged and never applied stays reported in the update block, naming the staged version beside the running one, until an update lands
5. `Restart to finish` (bridge `update.requestRestart()`) restarts the app at once, with no confirm: a restart is a quit, and the background service keeps every session, run and terminal shell running through it
6. After relaunch main runs the handshake with the service (`daemon.hello`), as at every start. The app does not roll itself back; a bad release is fixed by the next one. A service outside the accepted version range is not a failed handshake: the console is read-only and the working line names the side that is behind (§Daemon Supervision Lifecycle)
7. Any failed check discards the file, and General reads `The update could not be verified and was not installed.` with `Try again`; the next check fetches it again

**The background service updates on its own path**, apart from the app: Settings › Runtime's `Update the background service` and `sidekicks self-update` take the same release feed and check the downloaded program against the SHA-256 the release's manifest lists for it, and nothing more. A service update never stops work: while any is running it waits on `Waiting for running work to finish`, naming that work on one line, its parts joined by `·` — the running sessions and runs by their titles, at most three, then `and N more`, then the terminal Codex sessions inside the service as a count — with `Cancel` offered until the app asks the old service to stop. What it does and refuses is [Spec-006 §Required Behavior](./006-local-ipc-and-daemon-control.md#required-behavior)'s.

**On a Windows computer whose Claude Code and Codex live in WSL 2**, the Windows installers carry the Linux runtime for their architecture, for glibc and for musl, and the service's Windows half installs it into the distribution, keeping the current runtime folder and the one before it. The service's own update downloads the Linux release inside the distribution, checks it against the checksum the release publishes, installs it the same way and asks the Windows half to restart on the new folder. **The runtime has its own rollback**: if the new folder's daemon sends no ready line within 10 seconds, or its `daemon.hello` fails, the Windows half starts the previous folder again, and Runtime's settled line names both versions and the reason. The Windows half itself updates with the app: it runs from a per-version folder main fills at install and update, so an app update never replaces a running file, and the new copy takes over at the next service restart.

### Daemon Supervision Lifecycle

1. App start: main looks for the background service where it listens — its socket, or on Windows the per-user named pipe `\\.\pipe\ai-sidekicks-<user SID>`, on both kinds of Windows computer — and connects; the cover reads `Connecting to the background service…` while it does
2. If no service answers, main starts one and the cover reads `Starting the background service…`. On macOS and Linux main spawns the service detached (`child_process.spawn` with `detached`), never as a child of the desktop app, because a detached child survives a quit, SIGTERM and SIGKILL (measured on Electron 44.5.1 on macOS and Electron 44.1.0 on Linux); on Linux a logout ends it unless lingering is on. Where a detached child does not survive, the service runs through the per-user service `sidekicks daemon install` sets up. On a Windows computer whose Claude Code and Codex live in WSL 2, main runs the per-user logon task, which starts the service's Windows half (`sidekicks-windows-half.exe`), which starts the daemon in the distribution with `wsl.exe --exec`. In every case main reaches the daemon through its socket (see [Implementation Notes §Detached Service Launch](#detached-service-launch)), and waits up to 10 seconds for `daemon.hello`
3. Versions: each app accepts its own service version and the one before it. Outside that range the console is read-only — nothing mutating is sent and subscriptions still read — and the working line names in words the side that is behind, the app or the background service, with a press that opens its fix: the app's update on Settings › General, or `Update the background service` on Settings › Runtime
4. Live: main sends `daemon.ping` only after 5 seconds with no frame from the service, so a busy link carries none, and counts the link as dead after 20 seconds with no frame; a service that exits is noticed at once, when its socket closes. The link's state reaches the renderer as the `daemon.status` topic on `daemon.subscribe`, and while the link is down the composer's working line is the connection line
5. Service lost: main starts it again with backoff (100 ms, 300 ms, 1 s, 3 s, 10 s); after five failed attempts it stops trying and the console shows the persistent state of §Fallback Behavior. On a WSL computer the Windows half restarts the daemon with the same backoff and, after the fifth failure, writes down why the service cannot start; main checks the Windows half's per-user mutex, and when no one holds it, runs the logon task at once rather than waiting for the task's own one-minute restart
6. Quit: main sends `daemon.flush` and leaves the service, every run and every terminal shell running, and the app reconnects to them when it opens again. Only Settings › Runtime's `Stop` and `Restart` (`daemon.stop`, `daemon.restart`) end work in flight, and no surface explains that the work outlives the window. A service started from the command line is untouched either way
7. Stop and restart: after the flush, a service that has not exited gets SIGTERM and, 2 seconds later, SIGKILL
8. Restore: Runtime's `Restore…` (`daemon.requestRestore(backupId)`) goes through main, because the service cannot replace its own store while it runs. Main stops the service, its confirm naming the work that stops as `Stop`'s does, replaces the database, the settings file, the workspaces, the checkpoint copies, the conversation files and the agent memory folder with the backup's, and starts the service again. A backup is a plain copy that holds no credential: a restore on another computer reads it directly, and the person then signs in to providers and links devices again; a restore on the machine that wrote it needs nothing more. `sidekicks db restore <backup>` does the same on a machine with no app, and is refused while the service holds the data folder

The Runtime settings page is the one console surface that drives this lifecycle by hand, and the one that shows what the supervisor above reports. What its stop, its restart and its separate daemon update do, what each of them refuses, and how the daemon's machine-wide values are set — one place per value and no second one — are [Spec-006 §Required Behavior](./006-local-ipc-and-daemon-control.md#required-behavior)'s and are not restated here; the page reads those facts and renders them, and edits none of them anywhere else.

## Default Behavior

- The desktop app connects to the background service at app start (starting it if none answers, per [Spec-006 §Default Behavior](./006-local-ipc-and-daemon-control.md#default-behavior)) and exposes renderer-accessible capabilities via the preload bridge per §Trust Stance; the renderer is not a direct daemon client
- The window draws one quiet cover until the service has answered — the working indicator and one line saying what is happening, `Starting the background service…` when the app started it and `Connecting to the background service…` when it found one running, with nothing behind it drawn half-painted — then fades it away for the rest of the run and never draws it again
- Update checks are **on** by default (`Check for updates automatically`), and the person may turn them off on General; a download starts only on the person's press, and an update installs only at `Restart to finish`
- Crash reports are **kept** by default (`Keep crash reports`, on): a report is built on the machine that crashed, stripped of personal data there and kept there, and nothing sends it anywhere; the person's linked devices read it through Remote Control (`daemon.crashList`), and `sidekicks crash list` reads it from the command line; off, no report is kept
- Notifications are **on** by default: `Show a count on the app icon` and `Notify me outside the app`, and under the second its four kinds — `Waiting on you`, `Finished`, `Failed` and `Notify steps` — each on, and grayed with `Turned off above.` while `Notify me outside the app` is off; all of them are kept per device in the machine's settings file ([Spec-017 §Default Behavior](./017-notifications-and-attention-model.md#default-behavior)). A session's own `Mute notifications` (`session.mute`, undone by `Unmute notifications`, `session.unmute`) silences that session's `Finished` and `Failed` on every channel and every device, and never `Waiting on you` or a Notify step
- Deep-link protocol handler registration is performed on first launch

## Fallback Behavior

- If OS-local transport to the daemon is unavailable: fall back to loopback per [Spec-006 §Fallback Behavior](./006-local-ipc-and-daemon-control.md#fallback-behavior); surface the fallback clearly in the UI
- If the background service cannot be reached after five backoff attempts: at boot, one quiet card in the middle of the window in place of an empty console — `The background service is not answering.` and `Retry`, which asks main to start the service (`daemon.requestStart()`), and nothing else. Once the console has drawn, the same fact belongs to the working line — `Connection lost`, then `Reconnecting…` while the connection is being retried and `Not connected.` once it has stopped, with `Retry` at the line's right — so the card never appears over a session a person was reading. Everything under it stays as it was: the draft keeps its text, the panes stay open, no control is disabled, and a message sent meanwhile waits on its own row reading `Waiting to be delivered`. On Windows, where the service has written down why it cannot start, the card and Settings › Runtime say why instead
- If the service is neither the app's own service version nor the one before it: the console is read-only, and the working line names the side that is behind with a press that opens its fix (§Daemon Supervision Lifecycle)
- If the auto-updater cannot reach the feed: skip this cycle; retry on schedule; do not block normal operation
- If a write from a settings page is refused: the control rolls back to the value it had, and the page renders the daemon's own refusal in place as an inline strip carrying `Try again` as a faint clickable word at the right end of its text line — no box, no icon, and no toast anywhere on that screen. Nothing ever draws as saved
- If the machine's settings file is malformed: the background service, its one writer, reads the defaults, repairs the file, and the page that reads it says both happened. A malformed keyboard map or appearance record reads as the defaults and is repaired by main, which writes both, and the Keyboard page says both happened of the map
- If this install cannot update itself: the General settings page draws no update button at all, says so in place, and names the command that gets the newest version instead
- If a chord a rebind records is already held by another act: the Keyboard page refuses it on the row and names the act that holds it; the row keeps the chord it had
- If the daemon is unreachable while Settings is open: every page keeps its entry in the list, each page says in place what it could not read and that nothing on it can be saved right now, and every control keeps the value it had rather than emptying or disappearing

## Interfaces And Contracts

### Renderer → Main Process → Daemon (via Preload Bridge)

- Wire format between renderer and main process: Electron IPC over `contextBridge`-exposed functions; serialization via structured clone
- Wire format between main process and daemon: JSON-RPC 2.0 with Content-Length framing per Spec-006 and ADR-009, over the service's socket — on Windows the per-user named pipe `\\.\pipe\ai-sidekicks-<user SID>`
- The main process is a transparent forwarder for the daemon contract — no method rewriting, only auth-header attachment, response-payload sanitization and the file-token swap below
- Main's own calls on the service: `daemon.hello`, the handshake at each connect; `daemon.ping`, sent only after 5 seconds with no frame from the service, the link counting as dead after 20 seconds with no frame; and `daemon.flush` before every quit. The link's state reaches the renderer as the `daemon.status` topic on `daemon.subscribe`, which carries the version range too: each app accepts its own service version and the one before it, and outside that range the console is read-only and the working line names the side that is behind. On Windows the topic also carries `cannotStart {reason: wslNotResponding | restartsExhausted {error} | placeGone | connectionNameTaken}`, which main reads, when the pipe is absent, from the status file the service's Windows half writes whenever its state changes (`%LOCALAPPDATA%\ai-sidekicks\run\service-status.json`), trusting it only while the Windows half holds its per-user mutex; and `whileSignedOut: on | off | passwordOutOfDate | notOffered`, for the service that keeps running while the person is signed out of Windows
- The machine-settings members carry the service's own verbs, because the service is the settings file's one writer: `machineSettings.read()` is `daemon.machineSettingsRead`, `machineSettings.write(change)` hands the change to `daemon.machineSettingsUpdate {change}`, answered with the file as written, and `machineSettings.subscribe(handler)` is `daemon.machineSettingsSubscribe`, whose first delivery is the current file (§State And Data Implications)
- Files cross the bridge as tokens, never as paths: `native.showOpenDialog` and `native.showSaveDialog` return tokens; a file dropped on the composer has its path read in the preload with `webUtils.getPathForFile`, and main mints its token; a pasted picture is written by main to a temporary file and handed back as a token; main's relay turns a token into the path the daemon needs, and a path a daemon reply offers reaches the renderer as a token main minted for it. No path string acts as a capability on the bridge
- The daemon calls main for what only main can do on the desktop, through the Preview page verbs (`preview.page*`): among them `preview.pageCookiesRead`; `preview.pageCookiesClear`, which answers `browser.siteCookiesClear {origin}` with `ses.clearData({dataTypes: ['cookies'], …})`; and `preview.pageSiteDataClear {origins?}`, which answers `Forget` (`browser.siteDataForget`) for one registrable domain, and `browser.siteDataClear` with no origins, through `ses.clearData`, because main owns the Preview partition and a per-origin clear leaves a parent-domain cookie. `browser.siteDataList` marks each site `hasCookies`, which Settings › Browser reads as `Saved cookies` where at least one unexpired cookie is kept and `Saved site data` otherwise
- Members main answers itself, with no daemon behind them: `window.setAppearance({theme, scheme, textSize, transcriptWidth}, grounds: {light, dark})` writes main's appearance record, which main stamps on every console document it serves, and `window.subscribeAppearance` carries the record back to every open console window (§Persistence on the renderer scheme); `keyboardMap.read()` and `keyboardMap.write()`, over the keyboard map's own file; and `native.getNotificationPermission()`, which on macOS reads the notification permission through `node-mac-permissions` (MIT), called off the main thread because it blocks — `authorized` and `provisional` read as allowed, `denied` as refused, `not determined` as not asked yet, and no reader as unsupported; the library reports no `limited` or `restricted` state, and every other state it returns, ephemeral included, reads `not determined`. That library is used because Electron 44 has no read of the macOS notification permission — its `Notification` class offers `isSupported()` alone, and `systemPreferences.getMediaAccessStatus` covers only the microphone, the camera and the screen — and a native module of our own would be code to maintain forever

### Renderer → Main Process → Control Plane (via Preload Bridge)

- Path: main forwards each renderer control-plane call, unchanged, to the background service's own `controlPlane.call` over its own connection to the daemon, and the service sends it to the control plane — tRPC v11 over HTTPS for request/response (incl. relay negotiation), and the relay WSS connection speaking the relay's binary wire frames — per ADR-013 and [Spec-027](./027-remote-control.md). The service takes the call as `controlPlane.call {procedure, input}` on its local socket; `procedure` is one of `device.list`, `device.linkStart`, `device.linkRedeem`, `device.link`, `device.linkCancel`, `device.rename`, `device.revoke`, `device.forget`, `device.statementList`, `device.pushAddressSet`, `runtimenode.rename` and `runtimenode.remove`, and relay negotiation or any other procedure is refused.
- Main attaches no control-plane credential and holds none: the service presents this machine's access token under `Authorization: DPoP` with a fresh DPoP proof on each call, and the renderer never sees either. Relay negotiation (`negotiateRelay` → `RelayNegotiationResponse.connectionToken`) and the relay `connectionToken` are the service's too, so no control-plane token crosses the preload bridge to the untrusted renderer (§Preload Bridge Contract forbidden-surface list; renderer-untrusted §Trust Stance). The desktop app reaches the person's other machines as its machine, never as a linked device: the service, which holds this machine's keys and tokens, negotiates and authenticates each channel to another machine, and main forwards the renderer's calls to it and holds none of them. A procedure the control plane defines as anonymous is sent with no credential while the service holds none. Anonymous means authentication is not _required_, not that a held credential must be stripped: the relay counts its sign-in routes per source address, so a present credential changes neither admission nor throttling

### Main Process ↔ Auto-Update Feed

- Transport: HTTPS from the project's GitHub Releases, read through `electron-updater` 6.8.9 with its stock GitHub provider (§Auto-Update Flow; [§Auto-Updater](#auto-updater))
- Artifact format: macOS `.dmg` to install and `.zip` for updates, arm64 and x64; NSIS per user on Windows x64 and arm64; AppImage on Linux x64 and arm64; and `.deb` and `.rpm`, which on install add the project's signed apt or dnf repository, hosted on Cloudsmith, and which the package manager updates and the app never does. There is no Snap, no Flatpak, no MSI and no staged rollout. Each release carries `electron-builder`'s update files, which list each update file's SHA-512, and a manifest listing every file's SHA-256
- Signature: `electron-updater`'s SHA-512 check of the download, and the operating system's code signature: Squirrel.Mac's designated requirement on macOS, and the installer's publisher on Windows (`verifyUpdateCodeSignature`); a release whose version is not above the last one seen is refused
- What the General settings page reads is this channel and nothing else: one subscription and one read over main's own update state, beside the facts the app reports about itself — its version, its platform, its architecture and its locale. The preferences on that page that carry no wire of their own — the editor that opens files, the default checkout for a new project session, the new-session switch, the keep-awake switch, `Keep crash reports` and the auto-update preference — live in the machine's settings file, which the background service writes (§State And Data Implications)

## State And Data Implications

- Where each window was, how big it was, which display it was on and whether it was maximized or fullscreen are remembered across restarts in main's own file under `userData`, one entry per window keyed by the window id the renderer's kept layout shares, and a pane's own window per pane kind, read before the window is built (§Console Design (Meridian) §Persistence on the renderer scheme); not sensitive
- The machine's own settings file, `<home>/.ai-sidekicks/machine-settings.json`, has the background service as its one writer: main and every other device write through the service's `daemon.machineSettingsUpdate`, and every console window reads through the service's live read. Main alone may read the file directly, and only before the service first answers, for the two values it needs at start, the auto-update preference and `Keep crash reports`; the renderer never reads it. It holds the auto-update preference, `Keep crash reports`, the editor preference, the default checkout, the new-session switch, the keep-awake switch, the notification preferences (the switches, the kinds, and the web address's and the email digest's settings other than their secrets), the browser's switches, the screen-reader switch, the environment rows for every project, the backup switch and the backup folder, the folder new clones go into, the `Branch names` pattern, and voice's two settings, the mode and the call voice. The folders this machine can reach are not in it; they are the service's. One schema in `packages/contracts` describes it for every reader. The service reads it each time it starts something a value governs — the headless browser, a provider process, a project session, a project's process, a Terminal pane's shell, a clone, a Codex voice call — and keeps no copy. Every write is an atomic rename over a schema-checked parse with fail-closed defaults, so a half-written file is never read back and a malformed one reads as the defaults, is repaired by the service, and is reported on the page that reads it. Everything else a settings page shows — the accounts, the tool servers, the projects, the browser's site data and the daemon's own facts — is the daemon's, read and written over its wire and never kept as a second copy in main or the renderer. Credential material is never in it
- The screen-reader switch is in that file because it reaches beyond the window: the service reads it when it starts a Terminal pane's shell and sets `CLAUDE_AX_SCREEN_READER=1` there alone
- The keyboard map is its own file, `userData/keyboard-map.json`, owned by main, written whole and atomically over a schema-checked parse through `keyboardMap.write()`, and readable and writable by this person alone; it holds only the rows that differ from the chords the app ships with, keyed by act, and is never written to a wire
- The appearance record is its own file, `userData/appearance.json`, under the same contract as the keyboard map: the theme, the color scheme, the text size, the transcript width and the current theme's light and dark grounds, every value an enum or a number, written by main through `window.setAppearance` and stamped on every console document main serves; a missing or broken file reads as the defaults and main repairs it
- The updater's record is its own file, `userData/updater-state.json`, owned by main: the last release version seen, read and written whether or not the service is running, application state and never a setting, written by no wire; a missing or broken file reads as the installed version
- Console layout, scroll position, selection, pins, and expansion sets — UI state only, never user-authored content: a draft, its staged files and a review's held notes are daemon-held and session-scoped and never enter this store, so they are covered by the daemon's own [Spec-020](./020-data-retention-and-gdpr.md) selectors rather than by a renderer one — persisted **per install** in IndexedDB on the privileged renderer scheme (§Console Design (Meridian) §Persistence on the renderer scheme) — never a projection, which is re-derived on reconnect; this is the one renderer-storage tier the app has, it holds no auth material, and it exists because the scheme is registered `standard: true` before `app.ready` (Plan-020 I-020-6)
- Main keeps no credential material and no keystore entry: this machine's DPoP key, the hosted account's tokens and every other secret are the daemon's. No credential is in the machine's settings file, main's own files or the renderer's storage
- The daemon session token is read from its file at every connect and held in main-process memory only; it is rotated on daemon restart per [Security Architecture §Local Daemon Authentication](../architecture/security-architecture.md#local-daemon-authentication)
- The service watch's record (the service's process id, the time of its last `daemon.ping` reply, the restart attempt count) is kept in main's own `supervisor-state.json` for diagnostics only, and nothing reads it as the service's state ([Plan-025](../plans/025-remote-control.md) Phase 1)
- Crash reports are built and kept on the machine that crashed, stripped of personal data there, read through `daemon.crashList`, and deleted with the diagnostic logs; with `Keep crash reports` off, none is kept
- Update artifact cache: main-owned; cleared after a successful apply or after a configurable retention window

## Example Flows

- `Example: First run.` Main finds no background service answering and starts one detached, drawing `Starting the background service…` over the window until `daemon.hello` completes. There is no welcome wizard and no sign-in gate: the cover fades onto the sessions list, which reads `No sessions yet. Press New session to start with a chat or a project.`, and the only sign-in the console offers is a provider account's, on Settings › Providers.

- `Example: Auto-update applied.` Main checks the feed on schedule and finds a version above the last one seen; General reads `Update available — <version>, released <date>.` The person presses `Download`; the file lands and passes its hash check, and the block reads `Ready to install — <version>. Restart to finish.` The person presses `Restart to finish`; the app restarts at once while the background service and every session, run and terminal shell keep running; the new app's handshake with the service succeeds, and General shows the new version.

- `Example: Background service crash mid-session.` The service exits abnormally; its socket closes and main notices at once. The working line reads `Connection lost` and `Reconnecting…`, and a message the person sends meanwhile waits on its own row reading `Waiting to be delivered`. Main starts the service again with backoff; on recovery the service replays its event log, the renderer resubscribes and re-derives its projections, the working line goes back to what it showed before, the waiting message is delivered, and the transcript catches up.

## Signature Feature Composition Sketches

Each V1 Signature Feature view must compose daemon and control-plane state via the preload bridge. The owning plan listed in parentheses holds the feature's behavior; the renderer is a read-and-steer projection of that behavior, not the source of truth. The section also carries settings pages that compose under the identical rule without themselves being Signature Features; each such view says so in its own `Note`.

### Transcript View — the "everything happens here" surface (→ Plan-010 Live Timeline Visibility And Reasoning Surfaces)

- Data sources: the daemon's transcript reads through the bridge, `timeline.read` for history and the session's one stream, `session.subscribe`, replaying from the last position the window kept and then tailing in the daemon's batched frames (per Spec-011). The transcript names no connected device: which devices are connected shows on the Devices page in Settings and nowhere in a session.
- Renders: chronological event stream (messages, tool calls, approvals, diffs, agent reasoning, interventions, state transitions) per Spec-011
- Interactions: scroll-to-tail, the ask rail at the transcript's right edge (§Non-Goals), and the actions on the person's own messages — Copy, Fork, Edit and `Undo to here` (bullet below); no jump by event id, no filter by actor or type, and no replay from a row, since the transcript is the replay and a person scrolls it
- Edit and undo on the person's own message: every message the person sent carries, on hover or on focus inside it, Copy, Fork, Edit and `Undo to here`, on both providers, through a running turn, and never absent. The pencil puts the message, with everything it carried, back into the draft, and its row holds its place reading `Editing… · 4 turns after this will be superseded`, followed, when the undo's dry run finds files to put back, by its count (`Put back 3 files · 41 lines · 1 skipped`); files another session also changed since that point are listed and left as they are unless the person presses the include line; Enter puts the conversation and the files back to the moment before that message exactly as `Undo to here` does, then sends the edited message, and Escape closes the editor, asking first where the words were changed. The composer holds one edit at a time: a second pencil press puts the first row back and takes the second. While a resend is in flight the turns it will supersede dim together and stay readable and pressable, and once it lands they stay in the flow marked superseded. A resend that fails puts the edited message back in the composer with everything it carried, leaves the conversation as it was, and lands one system message naming the cause, `Resend failed · Connection lost`; where the cut had already landed, that row says the conversation went back and the resend did not. `Undo to here` rolls the conversation and the files back to the moment before that message, together, through the daemon's own checkpointer: it shows the dry run's count first — `Put back 3 files · 41 lines · 1 skipped`, the skipped file and its reason in the count's hover title — and asks in place, showing no count when the dry run finds no file to put back, and afterwards the flow carries `Restored to before <the message's first words>` with the turns after that point marked superseded; an undo that applied only in part names the part that did in those words and the part that did not with its cause, `Restored to before <the message's first words> · files not restored · <cause>`. The undo and the pencil's resend ask first when agents or commands started after that point are still running, naming each count from the daemon's own tree: `Undo to here? The 2 sidekicks and 1 command started after this will stop.` Esc Esc with an empty draft and no turn running opens the same undo as a rewind menu in place above the composer, one row per message the person sent, offering the conversation and the files together, the conversation alone, or the files alone. The dry run is `session.restorePreview` and the undo is `session.restore`, both the daemon's ([Spec-003](./003-queue-steer-pause-resume.md)); this view renders their result and never re-derives it
- Question card: an agent's question — Claude Code's held user-question request or Codex's user-input request, recorded as `question.asked` — is not a row of this surface. It is the question card in the composer, in the place the approval card takes, so exactly one card ever waits on a person at a time: the eyebrow reads question with the agent's short header beside it, the ask is the title, its structured options are rows carrying a mark, a label, a description and a number key on the first nine, the row the agent marked recommended stays where the agent put it and carries a recommended mark with the provider's own suffix stripped from its label, and a dashed something-else row with a text field stands last, so the free-text answer arm is **unconditional** ([Spec-011 §Timeline Entry Types](011-live-timeline-visibility-and-reasoning-surfaces.md#timeline-entry-types)). Several asks page one at a time with the count beside the eyebrow, picks and typed text surviving the paging both ways and every answer going back together on the last page. A permission request is not drawn as a question at all — it is an approval and takes the same place under §Approvals View; a question and an approval are different records, so exactly one card renders any request. Answers go back through `question.resolve`, carrying per question the picked labels, the typed text, the secret (never stored) or a skip, and the card closes when its attention entry on `attention.projectionRead` resolves. The daemon holds the request open with no timer, so the card carries no countdown, no expiry and no expired state: it waits until it is answered, is rebuilt on reload and on every linked device with the first answer settling it everywhere, and is canceled with an interrupted turn. Answering lands one turn of the person's own carrying, per ask, the question as a faint quoted line with the answer under it, and that turn is the record — it cannot be answered again. Plan-010 owns the card and its states; this view renders that projection
- The surface the rows sit on is the `transcript` pane kind (§Pane kinds), which fixes the flow's single width, its derived row gap — one token, `--row-gap`, half the reply's own reading line (`--sm` over 1.65), between every two consecutive rows whatever they are, with no row kind keeping a margin of its own and the stamp under a reply sitting at half the gap — its history paging, its drawn window, and the one path every jump to a row takes. Four surfaces may cover it: the plan reader, the command palette and the quick look over a picture or a document, each on a light scrim with the transcript and the composer still running underneath, and the find box, drawn on the surface's own corner with no scrim and nothing beneath it moving. Nothing else is drawn over the transcript.
- Copying out: the palette's copy-conversation act puts the whole open session on the clipboard in the form the screen reads — the same markdown a reply copies and the same plain text a message copies, in the order they are drawn — and the per-message and per-reply copy controls stay. Copy on a reply writes two flavors in one clipboard write, the markdown and a formatted flavor beside it, taken from the words the agent wrote and nothing else: the authored text blocks joined by a blank line, with the tool rows, their output, the thinking and every transport detail left out. Copying part of a reply puts the markdown that made it on the clipboard, rebuilt from what was selected, with a formatted flavor beside it; Copy on the person's own message writes plain text and nothing else. Nothing exports, prints, publishes or reads a transcript aloud; those two are the only ways a transcript leaves the screen.
- State handling: one surface that tails live and pages history through the `transcript` pane kind, with no second mode; local projection cache invalidation on daemon reconnect
- Owning plan: Plan-010

### Session Composer — the chrome every session view carries (→ Plan-020 Desktop App And Renderer)

- Data sources: the driver capability read and the provider command-and-skill enumeration through `bridge.daemon.call(…)`, both node-local and both scoped to the session's own provider, account and model binding ([Spec-004 §The provider command and skill surface](004-provider-driver-contract-and-capabilities.md#the-provider-command-and-skill-surface)); the context figure and the rate-limit reading the Plan-010 surfaces this spec mounts already carry; and the draft, its staged files and the review notes being held, all three daemon-held and session-scoped rather than kept in window storage.
- **One agent, one composer — and one way to write to another session.** The card addresses the session's own agent, and the only other thing it can address is another session, which the person names with an `@` at the head of the draft (§Commands below). There is no target picker, no machine picker and no posture control anywhere on it, and nothing on it attaches an agent, a provider or a model to a running session: another agent takes part only when the session's own agent delegates to it, or when the person names one from the Sidekicks group of the command list, which inserts the provider's own form for addressing it. A change to the provider, the model, the reasoning effort or the output speed is one configuration mutation on that agent (`agent.configUpdate`); a spawn-bound one applies when the run ends, so it is acknowledged as pending rather than drawn as already in effect, and its `pending` / `applied` / `degraded` / `failed` disposition is rendered from the daemon's answer ([Spec-014 §Same-Agent Provider Switch](014-multi-agent-orchestration.md#same-agent-provider-switch)). The account a session runs on is not on this card and not in the inspector: it moves on the Providers settings page, which moves every unpinned session on that provider ([Spec-025 §Moving a session to another account](./025-provider-accounts-and-credential-homes.md#moving-a-session-to-another-account)). What a run may do on its own is the permission level on the toolbar below, which applies from the next turn, and the composer carries no second reading of it.
- **Every capability gate is the daemon's own report.** The composer derives none of them. A control the session's provider, account or model cannot run is absent rather than grayed — the levels the level control lists, the rungs the effort control offers, the compaction popover behind the context ring — on the fail-closed-projection discipline `I-002-19` states, since a disabled control would assert the capability exists and is momentarily unavailable, which would be false. The one named exception is the output-speed control, which is drawn on every session and grays in place where the chosen model has no fast output (§Rules every console surface obeys), its reason both in its own hover title and in one small faint line beside the composer's chips naming that model — the line following the model control, appearing and going as the chip grays and lights, and folding away with the chips on a card too narrow to hold them. Where the provider refuses a speed a turn asked for, the refusal is one system message in the transcript in words and the control reads its value alone: no suffix, bracket or mark is ever added to a chip.
- State handling: the command-and-skill enumeration is a **live read held for the session's current binding and nothing longer** — not persisted, not cached across sessions, and re-read rather than patched when the provider announces that its own set changed, which is what keeps the list from offering a definition the running provider no longer has. The compaction popover's completed state renders only on the daemon's own compaction row rather than on the call returning.
- **The card.** A floating card at the transcript's own content width, read from the same root token the transcript column reads so the two move together at every width and every text size, docked one gutter's gap off the bottom with the gutters beside it still clickable. Its children run top to bottom: a hairline that takes the strong edge color while the session is stopped; the worktree setup card while one runs; the working line; the approvals card while a request waits; the composer proper — the command list, the attachment row, the draft line with send at its end, and the toolbar; and the session strip, which carries the project chip and the worktree switcher on a project session — on a chat session the worktree chip is absent rather than disabled and the strip reads `Session workspace` beside its `Attach a repo` control — and, right-aligned, the agents badge with the robot glyph and its count. A press on the card's own empty space puts the caret in the message box, unless one of the card's menus is open, in which case the press closes that menu and the closing hands the caret back. Entering a session puts the caret in the message box, once for that session: never taken from a shell that already has it, never from a session that is not the one in view, and not again on a session the person comes back to. On a settled session a bare printable key that nothing else has claimed puts the caret at the end of the draft and lands there, so typing is enough to start a message; it does nothing while a modifier is held, while a menu, list or overlay is open, while the shell has focus, and while the question card is waiting on its number keys. Focusing the draft strengthens the card's border; the message box has no focus ring of its own. The draft grows from one line to the smaller of about eight lines of the current text size and about a third of the transcript's visible height, and scrolls inside itself past that; the transcript's bottom padding is republished to the card's measured height plus its gap whenever the card resizes, so the newest row stays readable above it, and a growing approvals strip, command list or setup card pushes the transcript up rather than covering it.
- **The working line** reads, left to right: the activity indicator; the action words; then, from the moment the turn starts, the elapsed clock and the token figure, each behind its own separator; then, each with its own separator and only while it has something to say, the state slot, the tasks word and the count of unanswered questions; then the pause word and the interrupt word. There is no "working" word — the indicator and the action words carry it. The line sits at the head of the card above the draft and is drawn whenever it has something to say, absent on an idle session and while a plan waits; the card grows upward for it, so the draft never moves when a run starts or ends, and it is never an empty reserved strip. Nothing in the row wraps, and the action words are the only part that truncates, with their full text in their own hover title. The action words say what the agent is doing now, hold the last step's words while reasoning streams between steps, read as thinking before the turn's first step has landed, stay faint after a pause or an interrupt, and read as sending from the press of send until the turn starts, with no clock and no token figure beside them then. They read as compacting while a compaction runs, in place of the verb, and on a session with no live turn the line appears for that duration and hides again; and they read as retrying while the provider is retrying a call of its own, the clock still running and the token figure holding where it stands. While any command runs they are pressable and open the running-commands list in place above the line: one entry per command in the order they started, each with a live timer, its own stop word — the same word that command's transcript row carries — and a live tail of its output that follows the bottom and pauses while it is scrolled up, with a last row that stops every running command in the session and touches no agent. The tail is a window onto the command's own transcript row and never a second copy of its output, and the line itself never counts commands. The token figure reads a down arrow and the tokens received this turn, starting at zero when the turn starts, stepping up once per model round rather than per word, holding across a pause, muting with the clock after a stop, and hidden while the line is the connection line; no other live token figure stands anywhere on the screen. The state slot holds one reading at a time and is absent with its separator when there is none — thinking while reasoning streams, pausing, paused, or interrupted with what is still running after it. Because the slot sits after the clock, nothing before the clock changes on a pause or an interrupt: the indicator freezes, the clock and the token figure mute where they stand, the action words fade, and the clock never moves again. The tasks word is drawn only while the turn has a task list and opens that list in place above the line, one list open at a time with the running-commands list. A lost connection turns the indicator amber, swaps the action words for the connection state, puts the reconnecting or not-connected wording after the clock, hides the token figure, the state slot, the tasks word and the controls, and puts the retry control at the right — all at the row's own height and type, so nothing grows.
- **Commands.** A leading slash or currency character opens the command list, which assistive technology reads as a list of choices and which stays open only while the draft holds no newline and a row still matches what is typed; a word no row matches closes the list rather than refusing the word, because that word is going to the provider as typed. Its order is the console's own words first, each with one line of help and its caught spellings matched but never drawn as rows, then on a Claude Code session every other word that provider's frame offers, marked as the provider's and sent as typed, then the skills both providers carry with their origin marks, then a Sidekicks group under its own heading whose row inserts the provider's own form for addressing one, then one group per working tool server under the server's own name holding the prompts it publishes. `/agents` is a caught spelling of `/sidekicks`: it is never drawn as a row, it presses the Sidekicks pane, and the typed word is replaced, not sent. The console's own words are intercepted in the client and never forwarded as text: most press a console control, and the five bridges do their work through the daemon on the provider's own verb; among them, `/output-style` changes Claude Code's output style for this one session alone and leaves the Providers page's default untouched, and `/advisor` does the same for the session's advisor. Two cases are sent as typed instead, pressing nothing, and the provider's own answer is what shows, with no line of the console's: a console word whose control has nothing to act on in this session — `/effort` on a model with no effort levels, `/fast` on a model with no fast output, `/fork` before anything is said, `/diff` with nothing changed, `/background` with no command waiting — and a console word given an argument its control cannot take — `/effort turbo`, a model not on the chip, `/workflow` with an unknown verb. A word on no list is sent as typed and the provider answers it as it would in its own terminal; there is no escape for a literal slash and no unknown-command row. A word that does work of its own lands one small quiet system message in the transcript; a word that presses a control lands exactly what that control's own press lands, which for most is no row at all. On a Codex session, which never asks a tool server for its prompts, the daemon lists and reads each working server's prompts through its own client, a server behind a sign-in on the service's own sign-in, so the tool-server groups in the list work the same on both providers. The arrows move and wrap, tab completes, Enter runs or completes, and Escape closes the list for the word it was open on and leaves the draft as it is. The draft draws its text plainly — no word in it is tinted, underlined or marked — because the open list is where a recognized word is confirmed. A file mention opens a ranked search of the working folder's files, run on the machine that holds them rather than in this window, waiting a beat after the last keystroke instead of searching per keystroke, retrying once when that machine could not be reached, dropping an answer a newer search has overtaken, and keeping the rows already shown while the next answer lands; it says which state it is in rather than letting an empty list stand in for a failure. An accepted mention is plain text in the draft and renders as a mono chip on the sent turn where it matched a real file; where two files in one message share a name each is drawn with the shortest tail of its path that tells them apart, in the search list and on the sent turn alike, and a row in that list inserts its mention and does nothing else — it opens no file and carries no second control. **The same `@` offers the live sessions beside the files**, each row the session's name and what it is doing, and picking one puts that session's `@name` in the draft: a draft that **begins** with a session's `@name` is addressed to that session, so sending it delivers the message there and it arrives as that session's own row rather than starting a turn on this one, while a session's `@name` anywhere later in a message to this session's own agent stays a mention and draws its chip on the code wash exactly as a file path does. Agent targeting stays a row in the command list and never an `@`.
- **Interrupt and pause.** Interrupt is the working line's last word and Escape is its key once nothing else is open to close; the composer carries no stop control, send never becomes anything else, and there is no cancel-turn anywhere. It interrupts the lead and nothing under it: the turn ends, the command it was running is ended, its running agents are spared, every unfinished live timer in the transcript settles, and one system message lands. The one thing it reaches beyond this session is the session on the other end of a live exchange, whose turn ends with this one and whose queue is emptied, because a press on one side would otherwise leave the other side free to wake it again; that system message names what it reached, in both transcripts ([Spec-014 §Sessions Talking To Each Other](./014-multi-agent-orchestration.md#sessions-talking-to-each-other)). Anything pending goes at once as the next turn, and with nothing pending the state slot says the lead is interrupted and waiting. While agents the session dispatched are still live the word gives way to interrupt-everything, which stops every one of them at every depth from the daemon's own tree, one stop per identity with partial failure carried on the row it failed for and nothing atomic, and returns any still-pending text to the draft one line per message in send order. Pause sits before interrupt while a turn runs, on the session and in every child's view, and is a toggle with no key: the word turns amber, the state slot reads pausing while the step in flight finishes and paused once nothing runs, the clock freezes where it stands, and pressing the word again or sending anything continues from exactly there with nothing repeated. There is no pause-now, no resume word standing in pause's place and no pause row in the transcript; pending rows stay pending while paused and go as the continue; and interrupt stays available throughout.
- **The attachment row and send.** The attachment row is drawn only while something is staged, and holds one chip per staged thing with its own removal control: one chip per file the attach control picked, one chip for the marks a `browser` pane sent, and one chip for the review notes the person is holding. Attach opens the platform's own picker through `native.showOpenDialog` — files only, several at once, no type filter, never a folder — and staging writes nothing into the draft: the chip is the whole record of what will go with the message, and a folder reaches an agent only as a path typed or dropped in. Staging is bounded by what the daemon and the provider accept, and those figures are read from them rather than written on the screen: a file that does not fit is refused in the row's own one-line refusal naming that file and the limit it hit, and where several were picked at once the line says how many were taken — a file is never dropped in silence. A file still being copied says so on its chip and send waits for the copy, so a drop followed straight by Enter never loses it: the wait is invisible on a small file, past a short delay the chip's size slot reads `Copying…`, and a copy that fails takes its chip away and the row's one-line refusal reads `<name> was not attached · <cause>`, the cause the daemon's own, with `Try again` at its right, which copies that file again. Send is the one filled control on the card, at the right end of the draft line, sharing one diameter and one center line with the context ring below it; it is dimmed only while the message carries no text, no staged file and no held notes, and it carries no other state — a message that is only a file goes as it is, with no sentence written for it. Enter sends and shift-Enter inserts a newline. A message lands in the transcript the moment send is pressed, whether or not a turn is running, drawn as an outline with the time it was sent and settling in place the moment the daemon has it, so no row re-mounts under the reader; several stack in send order as the transcript's last rows while the agent's new rows are inserted above them. A send that fails takes its row back out of the transcript and puts everything it carried back into the composer — the words, the staged files and the held notes — with the caret where it was, and lands one system message naming the cause. From an empty draft the up and down arrows walk back through every message this session has sent, which the transcript already holds, and forward again, returning whatever was typed before the walk; they recall only from an empty draft, so they never collide with moving the caret through text, and they yield to a question card while one is open. `⌃R` in the message box searches the same messages: what is typed after it narrows to the newest sent message containing it, `⌃R` again steps to the next older match, Enter or `→` puts the match in the draft, and Escape gives the draft back as it was; recall and search fill the draft and send nothing. `⌃R` is the `Search sent messages` row on Settings › Keyboard, whose line reads `Searches the messages sent in the open session, from the message box.`, and in the terminal the key stays the shell's.
- **The toolbar's controls.** Six value controls, drawn on every session: the model control, reading the model with its context window on its own rows, and opening **both providers' models under two headings**, `Claude Code` and `Codex`, so picking a model under the other provider's heading is how a session changes provider — the control keeps reading the model, the working line's state slot reads `switching to Codex` or `switching to Claude` and nothing longer, picking again replaces the pick and picking the current provider's model back cancels it, and the switch itself applies when the run ends, landing two faint collapsed rows in the transcript at that point, `Hand-over brief · Claude Code` and `Switched to Codex · continued from a brief · 3 things did not carry over` — and the plain transcript file the daemon writes beside the brief for the new provider to read on demand is not drawn at all ([Spec-014 §Same-Agent Provider Switch](014-multi-agent-orchestration.md#same-agent-provider-switch)); the effort control, reading the reasoning effort as one word and opening the one ladder — `Low`, `Medium`, `High`, `Extra high`, `Max`, `Ultra` — with the rungs the model lacks absent, so a model offers exactly the rungs it publishes, each rung mapped one to one onto the provider's own name (`low`, `medium`, `high`, `xhigh`, `max`, `ultra`) and applied from the next turn; the output-speed control, reading fast or standard; the mode control, which swaps its glyph with its word between build and plan; the permission-level control, reading the session's level and opening the five levels in one fixed order from the most careful to the least — read-only, which reads on its own and asks before any change; ask, which edits files on its own and asks before running commands; reviewed, where the provider's own reviewer approves routine work, blocks the rest and tells the agent why, and a block can be allowed once; sandboxed, which never asks and writes only inside the worktree; and YOLO, which never asks and has no limits at all — with a one-line note on each; and the tool-servers control, reading how many of the session's tool servers are switched on and opening them as a grouped list with a session-level switch on every row under one link into the MCP servers settings page; this list is the only place in a session that lists its tool servers and their states, and the command list names a server only as the heading over the prompts it publishes — grouped rather than marked, the working servers first under their names alone, then a faint rule, then the ones that are not working, dimmed, each carrying one short faint word — `Needs sign-in` or `Failed`, from the same inventory that settings page reads — with its whole reason in that word's hover title and the failed ones last, a server still starting sitting with the working ones until it says otherwise. A row's one other act, `Attach a resource…`, lists what that server publishes and stages the chosen one in the attachment row; no row carries a reconnect or a sign-in control. On Codex a resource read rides Codex's own connection, and the service fronts each server for Codex's long calls, so prompts, resources and long calls work the same on both providers. A switch turned off stops the session offering that server's tools at all, so none of its definitions reach the model, it applies at the next turn and one flipped mid-turn says so in one faint line on its own row rather than changing anything under the run, and settings, every other session and every session started later are untouched. A session with no tool servers still draws the control, its list holding one line saying it has none above that same link. Every one of them reads its value as a word and never carries a pressed tint, no value chip ever looks selected beside its neighbors, and each opens its list in place above the chip, hung off the dock rather than inside the card, which clips its overflow — a list that is present but half clipped is a defect. A control whose value differs from what the session started on carries one small mark saying so and loses it the moment the value matches again; that mark is the only sign of a departure. A new session opens the model and effort controls on the last model and effort the person used, and the level control at `YOLO` however the last session was left; with remembering off, or on the first session ever, it starts on the first usable signed-in account's provider and that provider's default model. Nothing else carries over — not the mode, the speed or the compaction bound. The level list never shows a provider's own mode name and never shows plan, which is the mode control's, and a level the session's provider, account or model cannot run is absent rather than grayed. Beside the six, the right-hand cluster holds the attach control and then the context ring at the far right: one figure and one meter, gray on gray, never colored, with a faint notch on its arc at the compaction bound, opening the compaction popover in place above it: `Compact now`, then the slider reading `Compact automatically at <B>% · of <window>`, its range derived for this session from the provider's live figures — the top stop at the provider's own compaction point on this window (Codex's at 90 %), the bottom stop at the session's fixed start plus a tenth of the window, rounded up — and a Settings default outside that range reading at the nearer stop, unmarked. The figures no request reports are read from the installed provider, never typed in, and read again when the installed version changes; none is called provider-reported. Before a Codex session's first usage report the slider is inactive under `The range opens after the first reply, once Codex reports what this session loads.`, its bottom stop then counted from that turn's input, and `/autocompact` refuses a value outside the stops. The `Context compacted` row opens to the summary the provider kept, in the provider's own words, in a capped panel that scrolls inside itself; a compaction whose provider kept no summary stays a plain row with nothing to open. A compaction the provider attempts and fails lands no row, and one the person asked for that did not run says why on the working line; a turn too long even after compaction comes back as a failed send reading `Send failed · Too long for this session's context window`. Nothing on this screen estimates what a draft will cost — the ring's figure is the one the provider reported, and no local count of the text being typed is drawn. Once the card is measured too narrow to hold them — the chips' own width at the current text size against what is left beside the ring and send, measured and never a figure — every value chip gives way to one overflow chip whose menu carries a row per control reading that control's name and its current value, and picking a row opens that control's own list exactly as its chip would; the right-hand cluster never folds, nothing else in the card reflows, and the chips are back the moment they fit again.
