The composer carries no standing attachment tray, no emoji picker, no formatting toolbar, no clear control, no colored context ring, no abort control, no second send variant, no send-later or schedule, and no count of the draft; it proposes no text of its own, and the draft is the platform's own text field, so the operating system's dictation, spelling, substitutions and input methods work in it. The console's own voice carries no button on the card: it is the `/voice` command and the Space key (the voice bullet below).
- **The ask rail** rides the transcript's own right edge inside its scroller and is pointer-only: invisible at rest, fading in once the pointer has rested inside the rail's own edge zone and out again a moment after it leaves, hidden from assistive technology, carrying no focusable control, not drawn while the session holds fewer than two asks, and not drawn at all while the transcript column is at its floor, so a narrow window or a pane taking the width leaves the transcript its whole measure. It carries one hairline tick per message the person sent and none for an agent turn or a tool row; the tick of the ask nearest the middle of the visible band is filled in the accent; hovering a tick shows a one-line label with the first words of that ask, and pressing it scrolls the transcript there. There is no preview card, no widening and no jump arrow, and two chords give the keyboard the same two jumps without adding anything to the screen.
- **The jump control** sits above the card inside the dock, riding the card's height, and is drawn only while the transcript's follow is released; nothing is drawn over the transcript for it and nothing else moves. One press lands on the newest row and pins the follow again, scrolling to the tail pins it too, and a session opens pinned with nothing drawn.
- **Voice.** `/voice` turns voice on in the session it is typed in, and `/voice` again or `/voice off` turns it off; `/voice hold` and `/voice tap` pick the mode, one mode for both providers kept in the machine's settings file as `voice.mode` (`hold` or `tap`, default `hold`), beside the call voice, `voice.callVoice` (the voice a spoken call answers in; only Codex's call speaks today, and `voice.voiceList` gives the choices; missing reads as Codex's `defaultV1`), both read and written through `machineSettings.read()` and `machineSettings.write(change)`, and voice is on in one session at a time on this computer, the service holding which, so every window and device showing that session shows the same. While it is on the session strip reads `Voice on`, and `Listening…`, beside a mark that swells with the microphone's level, takes its place while the microphone is open; the empty draft's placeholder shows the mode, `Hold Space to talk` or `Tap Space to talk`, and reads `Tap Space to stop` while a tap has the microphone open. Only that session's Space talks, and nothing is recorded until Space is held or tapped. Voice waits for the person's answer to the system microphone prompt however long it takes. On Claude Code a recording is dictation: the window captures the microphone with echo cancellation, noise suppression and gain control, cuts it to 16 kHz 16-bit mono, draws its level and hands the frames to the daemon, whose own socket to the speech service returns the words at the caret, dimmed until they settle (`voice.dictationStart`, `voice.dictationWrite`, `voice.dictationStop`, `voice.dictationSubscribe`). On Codex it is Codex's own spoken call with the window as its WebRTC peer: the capture runs through an AudioWorklet into a media-stream destination whose track the call carries, which holds what is said while the call connects, and the call's offer carries the `oai-events` data channel the window reads `turn.created` from, so a new answer silences the rest of an old one (`voice.callStart`, `voice.callStop`, `voice.callSubscribe`, `voice.voiceList`). A hold's request plays into the call at 1.5× with its pitch kept (§Console Libraries, the voice playback row). Escape while recording drops the recording and interrupts nothing, and voice's refusals are the strip's own one-line refusal.
- Owning plan: Plan-020
- Note: chrome, not a V1 Signature Feature — it is the composition point the Signature Feature views mount their composer-area affordances into.

### Approvals View (→ Plan-009 Approvals, Permissions, Trust Boundaries)

- Data sources: the daemon's approval subscription and its approval projection, both read through the bridge; the rules in force on the session, read from where the providers keep them. What an approval is, when one is raised, and how a rule is scoped and kept are [Spec-010 §Required Behavior](./010-approvals-permissions-and-trust-boundaries.md#required-behavior)'s and [Spec-010 §Default Behavior](./010-approvals-permissions-and-trust-boundaries.md#default-behavior)'s; this view is the one surface that draws them, and it re-derives no rule of its own.
- **One card, in the composer.** A waiting request renders inside the composer above the draft, so a person answers without leaving the transcript. It is never a modal, a toast, a free-standing transcript card or a pane of its own: there is no approvals pane and no approval history. The one list of approval state is the inspector's `Rules` section, one row per approval rule in force on the session, the session's own and the project's alike, each reading what it does, its subject and its scope — `Allow pnpm test · this session`, `Block api.example.com · this project` — with `Revoke` confirming in place (`approval.ruleList`, `approval.ruleRevoke`); a session with none reads `No rules yet`. Every standing rule on the machine, in every account home and attached project, is Settings › Providers' list (`provider.standingRuleList`, `provider.standingRuleRevoke`), read from the same files. Exactly one is shown at a time and a queue is worked one item at a time: with more than one waiting the card reads its place in the queue beside its eyebrow — `1 of 3` — and with one waiting it carries no count at all.
- **What the card holds.** A two-pixel accent inset top rule rather than a fill; an eyebrow reading approval; the title over its summary line; one reason line under the summary in the provider's own words saying why this particular act stopped, stripped of control characters and terminal escapes before it is drawn and absent rather than empty where the provider sent none; and, in a fixed-width block, the exact thing it is asking about, whole — the command for a command, the path for a read or a write, the text for an input — wrapping rather than cutting and scrolling inside itself past a height derived from the card, which pushes the transcript up rather than covering it and keeps its actions in reach. That one line is editable in place before the card is answered, in its own type, and the changed text is what goes back with the answer and what the row then records as having run; there is no raw form of the call, and the card offers only the three actions below.
- **Three actions, in one fixed order:** decline, then a standing allow for this session on the subject the daemon derived from the ask — a command's program and first subcommand, a network request's host, a written file's name — then approve once, the hero last. The middle action reads `Always allow <subject> this session` and, on a project session, carries an arrow offering `Always in this project` (a chat has no project and no arrow); either scope hands the rule to the provider, which keeps it — a project rule in the provider's own project file, Claude Code's `.claude/settings.local.json` or Codex's `.codex/rules/sidekicks.rules` kept out of commits by `.git/info/exclude` — and the daemon keeps no rule store. A later ask on that subject runs with no card at every level that asks. The middle action is absent rather than grayed on an ask the provider marks as one that must not carry a standing allow, and nothing takes its place. An ask to send input to a command that is already running reads `Send input to <the running command>`, naming the command as its row does, with `Terminal input in the <worktree> worktree` under it and the input whole in the fixed-width block, labeled `Terminal input`; it takes no standing allow, so the card draws `Decline` and `Approve once` alone, a decline telling the agent so while the command keeps running, and on Codex that input is not editable, because Codex takes no changed input back. The person's own typing into a terminal never raises this card. On a network ask decline carries the same arrow offering a block on that host, so the rule set is allow and block rather than allow alone, and an allow and a block on one subject cannot both stand. Pressing decline opens one optional line under the actions for why not, which goes back to the agent through the provider's own refusal field and sends a bare decline when it is left empty.
- **The card exists only at the three levels that ask.** The two that answer for themselves never raise one: moving the level to one of them answers an open card and its blocked row runs, a later ask raises nothing, and moving back to a level that asks asks again.
- **It waits until it is answered.** The daemon holds the request open with no timer, so the card carries no countdown, no expiry and no expired state. It is rebuilt on reload and on every device the person has linked, the first answer settles it everywhere, and a device that was showing it closes it with a line saying it was answered elsewhere rather than with anything that reads as an error. The closers are the answer itself, an interrupt of the run — which cancels the open request and records the cancellation — a move to a level that never asks, and the provider process ending. An agent's own question and a plan waiting for a verdict take the same place on the same terms.
- **Answering.** Approving resolves the call in place: the card hides, the blocked timer stops waiting and keeps running, the working line becomes the running verb, the result lands when it settles, and the session's review data is marked stale — there is no approved banner and no extra row. Declining resolves in place the same way, the blocked call's row reading declined where its result would sit, and the session leaves the attention list. An approval a child raised is the parent's approval, answered in the parent's composer through one approval pipeline and one trust boundary; the child's own inline card answers by pressing the composer's matching action by index, so there is still exactly one answer surface, and walking to an approval from a child scrolls it into view and tints it faintly in the accent for a moment while the pane renders no second approval control. The card takes focus when it arrives while the draft has focus, landing on approve once, and that focus move never scrolls the transcript; its actions are walked with the left and right arrows and pressed with Enter, the walk stopping at each end. No single key answers it: there is no per-action keyboard accelerator, no approve-all, no per-approval notification and no cancel-turn action.
- Owning plan: Plan-009

### Runs View (→ Plan-002 Queue, Steer, Pause, Resume)

- Data sources: the daemon's run-state and queue subscriptions ([Spec-003 §Interfaces And Contracts](./003-queue-steer-pause-resume.md#interfaces-and-contracts)). A run's state, its queue and its intervention history are the daemon's facts; the renderer derives none of them.
- **There is no runs pane.** A run is controlled where it is watched. Pause and interrupt are the last two pressable words of the working line at the head of the composer card — pause a toggle with no key, interrupt answering Escape once nothing else is open to close — and while agents the session dispatched are still live interrupt gives way to interrupt-everything. The composer carries no stop control, send never becomes anything else, and there is no cancel-turn anywhere in the console.
- **What is waiting is the transcript's own last rows.** A message sent while a turn runs lands at once as the person's own row, drawn as an outline with the time it was sent, and stays there until the agent takes it at its next step, when it settles in place without re-mounting; several stack in send order as the transcript's last rows while the agent's new rows are inserted above them. On hover or keyboard focus such a row offers four acts, the same on both providers: a grip that drags it, or the arrows with the row focused, to move it within that stack (`run.queueReorder`, the full new order, refused unless it names exactly the messages still waiting); `Edit`, which puts its text back in the draft and leaves `Editing…` in the row so its place is held, sends back into that place on Enter as one call that replaces the waiting message, its old entry becoming superseded (`run.queueCreate` with `replacesQueueItemId`, refused once the agent has taken the message), and restores the old text on Escape; `Remove`, after which nothing reaches the agent (`run.queueCancel`); and `Send now`, which interrupts the turn, delivers that row first and lets the rest follow in order as the next turn's messages. Moving a row here changes the send order of messages the person has already sent; it is not a priority override on the daemon's admission queue, whose only removal path stays cancel-before-admission.
- Interactions: pause and its continue on an active run (`run.pause`, `run.resume`) and the interrupt (`run.intervene`); a steer is a message sent while the turn runs; a child's own controls are `run.childSteer`, `run.childInterrupt` and `run.childPauseSet {paused}`, and `run.childrenStop` stops every child at every depth; enqueue and cancel-before-admission on the queue. All are forwarded to the daemon's run engine per the run-state domain model ([Spec-003 §Interfaces And Contracts](./003-queue-steer-pause-resume.md#interfaces-and-contracts)), and the pencil's resend and `Undo to here` on a sent message are the daemon's undo (`session.restore`), not a run control of their own (§Transcript View).
- Owning plan: Plan-002

### Agents Pane View (→ Plan-013 Multi-Agent Orchestration)

- Data sources: the session's agents and their child-run links through `bridge.daemon.call(…)` — `agent.list`, `orchestration.childRunLinkRead`, and `orchestration.budgetRead` per agent, drawn only for a budget the person has set, so with none set no budget row is drawn — and each child's own flow from the same transcript reads the Transcript View uses, per the Plan-013 method-string registry (api-payload-contracts.md §Plan-013). A child run is started by the daemon's own paths (`orchestration.runCreate`) and never from a control here; run-level stop-condition outcomes render in the transcript, not here
- Controls: a child's pause and interrupt are `run.childPauseSet {paused}` and `run.childInterrupt`, its steer box sends through `run.childSteer`, and the list's stop-all is `run.childrenStop`, which stops every child at every depth from the daemon's own tree, one stop per identity; each is one wire mutation per explicit act
- **The agents pane in the session's view.** The `agents` pane kind draws the session's dispatched agents, as wide as its widest row reads at the current text size and not resizable, with a header carrying the title, its sub-line, an all-control, a divider, previous, next, `Open in new window` and close; like every side pane it can move into a window of its own (§Detached panes). One list view and one child view switch in place. **The list view** carries a bar with a stop-all control while anything is live and a hide-finished toggle, off by default, over one tree of every agent in the order the work happened, finished rows dimmed; a row is a button in the tab order carrying the agent's swatch — a saved definition's own icon, or the robot glyph where it sets none, on the agent's hue, never an initial letter or a photo — its name and model on one line, `via <agent>` naming the agent that was asked where it was a peer call, its act line in its own state color, its result once finished, an amber waiting control while it waits on a person, and a foot with tools, tokens, spend and duration. Depth indents by one step of the current text size per level and draws a hairline connector to the row's left. **The child view** holds the child's head — its swatch, its name, the one-line ask clamped to one line with the whole ask in its hover title, and its meta line carrying model, reasoning effort as one word, `via <agent>` where it was a peer call, tokens, spend, start, and the chain walking every ancestor as pressable links — then that child's own flow in the transcript's own rendering at pane scale, with the pane's foot holding the child's own working line, any approval card inline, and the steer box with its send. A live child view streams only while it is open. Its controls are words at the end of that view's own working line rather than buttons on a card: pause and interrupt, on the same terms as the session's, neither drawn once the child is done while the steer box stays, so a send starts the child again; a child stopped for good draws that word and no box. Interrupt is the last word in the line, so every interrupt on the screen sits in one place. The pane carries no pause or stop control on a card, no resume word, no kind label, no relay note, no mechanism tag, no overflow menu, no open control, no steer button, no second approval control and no dependency graph.
- Owning plan: Plan-013
- Note: this view binds to the Spec-014 surface as registered in api-payload-contracts.md §Plan-013.

### Provider Accounts And Cost View (→ Plan-023 Provider Accounts And Credential Homes)

- Data sources: the node-local `providerAccount` wire namespace through `bridge.daemon.call(…)` for the registry read, current-account selection, and per-account authentication probe result, and the session cost receipt read surface for spend — concrete method names for both are registered in api-payload-contracts.md per [Spec-025 §Interfaces And Contracts](025-provider-accounts-and-credential-homes.md#interfaces-and-contracts) and [Spec-014 §Session Cost Receipt](014-multi-agent-orchestration.md#session-cost-receipt)
- Renders: a **provider page of one section per provider**, each section in two halves — that provider's own settings above the accounts registered against it. **The provider's half** carries: the provider's own line, reading as installed and signed in with its version, as not installed on this machine, or as undecided where the check could not settle, and never as a guess — a not-installed reading carrying `Install` beside it on every platform, which runs that provider's own documented installer once, as the person, where the service runs — with no administrator step, no terminal and no input, `Installing Claude Code…` and `Cancel` while it runs, no time limit of the app's own, the installer's own timeouts bounding it, and the provider checked again afterwards — while a failure reads `Claude Code did not install: <the installer's own reason>` with the command under it as text to run by hand, and a provider command older than the build this console works with reading as too old and naming the version that is needed; the path to that provider's command in one field, reading as auto-detected while it is empty, re-checked where it now points whenever it changes and re-reading that provider's model catalog with it so the model control never offers a model the new command does not have, and refusing in place where nothing runnable sits there; a switch that makes the provider available for new sessions, which every provider can turn off, and which is grayed with its reason where that provider's command is not installed; a bound on how many helper processes the provider may run at one time, empty by default, which is no limit, the daemon lifting the provider's own built-in limit; `0` running none, the daemon withholding the provider's helper tool; and any other number passed to the provider as its limit, with no ceiling of the console's own — how deep an agent's own helpers may go is bounded neither by this control nor anywhere else in the console; the standing rules that provider holds on this machine, read from the provider's own files in every account home and every attached project, one row per rule reading what it allows or refuses with its scope — an account home or a project — and the file it came from, and a revoke beside it that removes the rule where the provider keeps it and confirms in place, the provider following the change as it does on its own, so a loaded Codex conversation keeps the rules it loaded with (`provider.standingRuleList`, `provider.standingRuleRevoke`), a provider holding none saying so rather than drawing an empty list; the protected paths, a read-only list of the paths an agent is refused on this provider — the built-in credential files together with the deny rules the person already keeps in that provider's own configuration, global and project — each entry reading where it came from, `~/.aws/credentials · built in` or `secrets/** · your Claude Code project settings`, with nothing on it to edit, the provider's own precedence deciding where two entries overlap, and each entry enforced by the provider's own rule at the levels that provider enforces it, which [Spec-010 §Required Behavior](./010-approvals-permissions-and-trust-boundaries.md#required-behavior) states for each provider; the provider-level automatic-compaction bound, which moves in steps of five between the lowest bottom stop and the highest top stop that provider's models report, so each provider's range is its own, and is mapped onto that provider's own control, drawn in both providers' sections because both expose it — a different control from a session's own bound, which sits behind that session's context ring ([Spec-011 §Context Window and Usage Meters](011-live-timeline-visibility-and-reasoning-surfaces.md#context-window-and-usage-meters)), so the row's own line says the bound is mapped onto the provider's and that the session's stays where it is; Claude Code's output style, one choice reading the style the installed build is on with that build's other styles beside it, one line describing the style it is on and one saying the choice applies to sessions started later, no style typed and no such row under Codex; `Reach Codex sessions started in a terminal`, one switch that is **on by default** and is drawn under Codex only, never under Claude Code, because the mechanism is that provider's alone — under the one line `Runs one Codex service on this machine while it is on, so your sidekicks can message a Codex session you typed in a terminal.` — such a session has no row in the sessions list, and the agents reach it through the session-messaging tools — and grayed where Codex is not installed, saying `Codex is not installed on this machine.` on the row and in its own title the way the availability switch does ([Spec-014 §Sessions Talking To Each Other](./014-multi-agent-orchestration.md#sessions-talking-to-each-other)); and one action that imports that provider's own existing conversations, reporting into one progress row on the page — no pop-up, no wizard, no screen of its own — carrying a stop while it runs, skipping every session this console already holds and saying how many it skipped, counting the files it could not read and unfolding their paths on a press, saying which reason it is where there was nothing to bring in, and confirming in place before it runs where it would attach projects this console does not have — reading that provider's own folders at the press and keeping nothing between presses, so a session finished a moment ago is in the read and there is no list to go stale and no rescan to offer, and what it brings in is a copy taken when it ran, nothing re-reading a source afterwards and nothing merging what the provider added since, so importing again is the way to catch up. **The accounts half** opens with one line saying that new sessions run on the account marked `Default`, that pressing an account gives it that mark and moves every running session on that provider that is not pinned to one, and that a saved agent or a workflow step can be pinned to a specific account instead; then it draws one row per account. **An account is named only by the identity its provider reports at sign-in** — the email address the provider returns, then the plan in the provider's own word, then the organization where the plan carries one — with no invented name and no typed label, because one address can hold two accounts on two plans. The one exception is an account added by a pasted token or an API key, which the provider names nowhere: it reads the name the person gave it in the paste form beside the credential's kind, `Work · Codex API key`, a name that is required, differs from that provider's other account names, and is renamed in place. Beside the identity a row carries its billing-mode chip, the `Default` mark on the one account per provider that holds it, its state in words with when it was last checked, an account whose login is gone reading `Login expired · Sign in again`, never collapsed into `Cannot tell right now`, the one remedy that applies to that state and no other, the ages the background observation writes — last checked, last refreshed, and the re-login horizon rendered as an approximation, a value the daemon reports as unknown **reading as not known rather than being fabricated** or read as though the account were fine — a freshness switch that is on by default and turns that observation off for this account alone without signing it out, and its quota windows per `(account, limit)`, each labeled with its own limit rather than merged and named from the length the provider reported rather than from its place in the reply, each a bar with its figure and its reset time in words beside it, **each carrying the time it was read** as `read 7:24 PM` — a figure that moves at the end of every turn on that account, so an account being worked on reads current and the background cadence only carries the ones nothing is running on — a window that has reset reading as reset, a reading taken before that account's credential changed reading as being from before the change, an account with no reading yet reading `not read yet` with no bar at all, and a reading the daemon could not take keeping its last figure with `could not read · 7:24 PM` beside it rather than moving a bar. Under the windows sits that account's tokens and spend — the day's figures, and on a press by day and by model — carried with its billing mode, so a plan seat's usage never reads as billed dollars and an account whose billing mode nobody has declared reads as not set rather than being folded unlabeled into a currency total; then the same two figures summed across that provider's accounts, which state when they span more than one billing mode and when they span more than one cost provenance ([Spec-025 §Billing mode](025-provider-accounts-and-credential-homes.md#billing-mode)) — and which read **tokens only** on a provider that reports no cost of its own, `All accounts · today 18M tokens · tokens only`, rather than showing a dollar figure no provider supplied. Beside that line stands the tightest window any of that provider's accounts is against, naming the account, its plan and that one limit's figure, `Tightest · <email> · Max · 5-hour limit 67%`; a percentage is never summed across accounts, because a limit belongs to one account. Where a provider publishes its own usage history it is drawn beside these with the provider named, never folded into them. **Never rendered here:** credential-home contents, which the renderer neither reads nor receives, and a token value, which no reply carries. The list draws its loading, empty and failure states rather than an empty list — ghost rows in the shape of the real rows while it is being read, a provider with no accounts naming the way to get one, a failure carrying try-again as one faint word, a provider whose accounts carry no `Default` mark saying that new work on it will not start until one is marked, an account whose usage cannot be read drawing one quiet line in place of its meters rather than an empty bar, and a warning the daemon reports about one provider drawn as its own strip in that provider's section. Spend and tokens by day and by model are drawn here and nowhere else: no usage screen stands beside this page, and no session surface carries an account's windows or an account-wide figure.
- Interactions: register an account, remove one, make one the provider's current account, correct its billing mode, start a brokered sign-in, cancel an in-flight sign-in, supply a non-interactive token under the name the person gives it, rename such an account in place, turn the background observation off for one account or back on, turn that account's window starting off or back on under it, check one account now, reset a credential home, and run the one-time copy of the person's own provider memories into an account's own home — each forwarded to the daemon registry as one wire mutation per explicit user action, and each act on the provider's half the same: `Install` and its `Cancel`, the command path, the availability switch, the helper bound, a standing rule's revoke, the compaction bound, the output style and the conversation import — with the protected-paths list the one thing on that half carrying no act at all, since nothing on it is added, edited or removed here. **Signing out and removing are two acts and never one:** signing out ends that account's login and leaves the row exactly where it is, so the same account signs in again without being registered twice, while removing forgets the registry row itself, **runs no sign-out of the provider's own**, leaves the credential folder on the machine untouched because that folder is the person's own material, confirms first because the account's spend and history go with the row, and is refused in place while a run bound to that account is live, naming the sessions. Checking one account checks that account alone and settles in place with one line saying what it did and did not do, since the observation reads the provider's own limits inside that account's own folder and spends no model turn: the daemon signs nothing in, reads and writes no credential, speaks no token endpoint, and lets the provider keep its own login current. There is no credential-refresh control at all: a refresh the provider asks the daemon for is refused, and the renewal the provider performs for itself shows on screen only as the last-refreshed age moving. What the windows carry beside them is a read again, which takes a fresh reading and moves no login of its own. **Keeping an account fresh carries one switch indented under it, and that one does spend a turn.** The nested switch reads `Start each five-hour window as soon as it opens` under Claude Code and `Start each usage window as soon as it opens` under Codex, is on by default, is off whenever its parent is off, and carries one line saying what it costs: under Claude Code `When the five-hour window resets, one short message on the smallest model starts the next one right away, so its clock runs from now. The weekly window resets at a fixed time of its own. About one small turn per window, on this account.`, and under Codex `When a window resets, one short message on the smallest model starts the new window right away, so its clock runs from now. About one small turn per window, on this account.` It draws nothing else — no row, no count and no list of the turns it spent; the turn it spends appears only in that account's tokens for the day and the week and, where the provider reports a cost, in its spend, and the limit row it belongs to carries the new window's reset time at the next read ([Spec-025 §Credential-home health observation](025-provider-accounts-and-credential-homes.md#credential-home-health-observation)). A brokered sign-in renders the provider's verification URL and, on a device-code arm, its user code with how long it has left and then that it has expired, the URL always drawn and selectable so a browser that did not open is not a dead end, with cancel available while the attempt is in flight; **completion arrives on the registry subscription and is never inferred from a timer**, a completion reported successful updates the row only after the daemon's own observation lands ([Spec-025 §Brokered interactive sign-in](025-provider-accounts-and-credential-homes.md#brokered-interactive-sign-in)), and a sign-in that does not finish says so where it was started, leaves the account as it was, and keeps the control that starts it again. The token field is **write-only in the renderer as well as on the wire**: masked on entry, never read back, never held in serializable renderer state a devtools inspection or crash report would capture, and cleared on submit — the wire rule protects the transport, this protects the screen. A token the provider does not accept is refused under the field it was pasted into with the field left empty and the controls still there to try another; re-supplying one targets the account already there and keeps its identity, its spend and its history; and a token account's own remedy is to mint a fresh token and paste it rather than to retry, because it carries no refresh. **Pressing an account is the account switch, and this page is the only place it happens.** It makes that account the provider's current one, moves which account new sessions run on, and moves every running session on that provider that is not pinned to one — each at its next request, a running tool finishing and never repeated, nothing interrupted and no waiting words on its working line — and it rewrites no credential, each account keeping its own home. Each moved session's transcript carries one faint collapsed row at that point, `Switched to account <name>` (`<name>` being the account as the Providers page lists it), expanding to the account it came from and the time ([Spec-025 §Moving a session to another account](025-provider-accounts-and-credential-homes.md#moving-a-session-to-another-account)). A key cycles a provider's accounts while it is held, a small strip showing them with the mark moving on each repeat, and **the account under the mark when the key is released** becomes current, so one gesture is one switch. The session inspector's account fact reads which account a session is on and offers no switch, and this page sends no per-run account override. A press naming an account that cannot be realized is refused in place with that account's own remedy, and nothing moves. One reading on this page is its own rather than the daemon's: a provider that exposes no provider-level setting at all reads that the provider decides it itself — the form that section takes, drawn only where that is true, which is neither provider today because both expose the compaction bound
- State handling: every rendered figure is wire-verbatim — no client-side derivation, re-summing, or parallel per-account tally. An account's lifetime spend and the total across accounts are each read as the daemon serves them from its committed-spend fold, one figure per account and one for the set, and the renderer assembles neither of them from the other and neither from per-session receipts, which is the client-side re-aggregation [Spec-014 §Cost Figure Display Consistency](014-multi-agent-orchestration.md#cost-figure-display-consistency) forbids. Spend and tokens by day and by model are served the same way, as group-bys the daemon performs over its own per-turn usage rows ([Spec-025 §State And Data Implications](025-provider-accounts-and-credential-homes.md#state-and-data-implications)); the renderer slices nothing itself. Account-scoped state (authentication probe result, provider quota) is held per account rather than once per provider — and quota per `(account, limit)` rather than per account, since one account may stand against several limits at once — and a reading observed under a superseded credential generation is re-read rather than rendered as current. **The page derives no eligibility of its own**: health, readiness, expiry, and which controls are available are all rendered from what the daemon reports and disabled where it reports nothing, the fail-closed-projection discipline `I-002-19` states for the edit affordance. A page that recomputed any of them would be the surface nothing enforces
- Owning plan: Plan-023
- Note: a settings page, not a V1 Signature Feature — it composes through the same preload bridge under the same read-and-steer rule.

### MCP Servers View — the settings-level governance page (→ Plan-022 MCP Server Configuration And Governance)

- Data sources: the node-local `mcp.*` wire namespace through `bridge.daemon.call(…)` for the server inventory and per-server detail, and `mcp.subscribe` — the live governance stream — as the update channel, so the page refreshes on the daemon's own transitions rather than by polling, which [Spec-024 §Status Observation and Events](024-mcp-server-configuration-and-governance.md#status-observation-and-events) forbids the daemon to do on the providers and which this page must not reintroduce from above. The page subscribes to that stream **before** it reads the inventory, so nothing that changes between the two is lost, and status arriving on it is applied to the row it names rather than triggering a whole-inventory re-read. Plan-022 already registers every operation this page calls; this view mints none — no operation, no event kind and no error — so a control that would need a new one is not a control this page draws
- Renders: a settings-level page in two panes — the server list on the left, the selected server's detail on the right. Each list row carries the server name, its provider and configuration scope, the normalized connection status, and an enabled marker. **The connection status is one of five words** — connected, starting, needs sign-in, failed, and not known yet — and this page is where they are stated: every other surface in the console that says what a tool server is doing, the composer's tool-servers chip among them, draws one of those same five from this one inventory. A server name held in two places is shown as held in two places rather than resolved away. With nothing selected the detail pane says so in one line and asks for a pick. The list draws its first read rather than an empty list: `Reading the server list…` while it is read, `The server list could not be read.` with `Try again` as one faint word at the end of its line, and a provider with no tool servers set up saying so. The detail pane adds the server's tool inventory — per tool, whether it is on, whether it asks before running, and what happens if a call is interrupted, each carrying its effective value beside whether that value is the server's own or set here — the effective idempotency class among them. Configuration content splits three ways on this page rather than being uniformly withheld, because a page that could not show or collect any would have no way to register a server at all. **(1) Input the person authors in the declaration form**: transport, command, arguments, URL, timeouts, the required-server marker, and environment-variable and header **names**. **(2) Read-back**, which is exactly the daemon's redacted config view and nothing beyond it — the same non-secret fields, with secret **values** structurally absent — so the page can support an edit workflow without the daemon ever serving credential material ([Spec-024 §Unified Inventory](024-mcp-server-configuration-and-governance.md#unified-inventory)). **(3) Never rendered**: any value the daemon does not serve — environment-variable values, header values, tokens, URL query values — and the page reads no provider config file, which remains the source of truth and the daemon's to read. Credential-bearing values the form does collect are **write-only in the renderer as well as on the wire**, on the Provider Accounts token-field discipline above: masked on entry, never read back, never held in serializable renderer state a devtools inspection or crash report would capture, and cleared on submit. The two governance **event** payloads remain credential-free and content-free by construction, and the page adds nothing to them
- Interactions: add or update a server declaration, remove one, enable or disable one, set or clear a per-tool idempotency-class override, sign in to a server, and reconnect — each forwarded to the daemon as **one wire mutation per explicit user action**, the same discipline the Provider Accounts view states: nothing batches, nothing retries on the person's behalf, and nothing hides an act the daemon would refuse. Reconnect is offered on the selected server whatever its state and drawn first on one reading failed; it asks the provider to open the connection again, writes nothing and changes no setting, says so, and where it moves no state it says that nothing changed rather than reading as success. `Sign in to this server` is one control whatever the server admits, and gives the background service one OAuth sign-in for that server, used by both providers: by default the service signs in as its own client; a server whose entry names a client its owner issued is signed in as that client; a server whose authorization server admits only Claude Code's or Codex's own client is signed in through the provider it admits, the service then holding that sign-in as its own; and a server that demands proof-of-possession (DPoP) tokens, which neither provider can sign in to, is signed in by the service with a signing key made for that one sign-in and fronted by the service for both providers, which signs a fresh proof for every request. The control stays pressable while a sign-in waits, a press ending that wait and starting the next attempt, and the page renders the daemon-returned authorization URL and settles on the daemon's own completion event rather than on a timer or on the browser returning — the same shape a brokered provider sign-in already takes on the Provider Accounts page. The service keeps each refresh token in the operating system's credential store under its own item and renews it itself, and each provider process it launches gets a header helper that asks the service for a current access token, so no token is logged, relayed, shown or given to the renderer ([Spec-024 §OAuth Orchestration](024-mcp-server-configuration-and-governance.md#oauth-orchestration)). That URL is **transient render state and nothing else**: it arrives on the login reply alone, is held only for the life of the attempt, is written to no renderer storage and no log, and is dropped at settlement — the write-only discipline this page already applies to a credential-bearing form value, applied to a value that is a one-shot launch handle rather than a credential. It is admitted to the renderer as the daemon's own reply value rather than as renderer-composed input, which is the distinction the external-open path exists to police. The prohibition it does not touch is the one that matters: tokens, authorization codes, and PKCE material reach this page on no surface at all. A refusal renders the daemon's typed reason verbatim — a refused action is refused before any write, and the page shows that refusal rather than pre-filtering the control, so the person learns the rule from the system that enforces it
- State handling: every rendered value is wire-verbatim, and **the page derives no state of its own** — connection status, enablement, tool overrides, and effective idempotency class are all rendered from what the daemon reports, the fail-closed-projection discipline `I-002-19` states for the edit affordance. A page that recomputed effective class would be a second source of truth for exactly the decisions [Spec-013](013-persistence-recovery-and-replay.md) recovery depends on. **Eligibility is a separate question and this page does not project it at all**: no inventory field reports whether an operation would be permitted, so every control is offered, the daemon adjudicates, and its typed refusal renders — which is the same rule as the no-pre-filtering interaction above rather than a second one. The one non-offer is a control whose required input is **structurally absent** from the entry the daemon served — on the degraded arm the daemon serves when the binding store is unreachable, the per-tool overrides are absent outright and the enabled marker survives only where the provider itself declares it, so the per-tool controls have nothing to act on while every control whose input did arrive is offered as usual — and that is absence of data, not a judgment the renderer formed. Status arriving on the subscription is applied to the row it names rather than triggering a whole-inventory re-read
- Owning plan: Plan-022
- Note: a settings page, not a V1 Signature Feature — it composes through the same preload bridge under the same read-and-steer rule.

## Implementation Notes

_This section captures architecture-relevant, non-normative implementation guidance; its primary-source citations are in §References §Research Conducted._

### Electron Version And Support Window

Electron 41 reached end-of-support on 2026-08-25 with the release of Electron 44.0.0 (Chromium 152.0.7977.54, Node 24.18.1); the supported majors are 44 / 43 / 42. V1 builds target **Electron 44.x**, pinned at 44.5.1, and [ADR-015 §Decision](../decisions/015-electron-desktop-app.md#decision) carries the 42 / 43 / 44 supported-branch subset of the GHSA-3c8v-cfp5-9885 floors with **44.x the only admissible V1 runtime**. Two facts of that line the console depends on: the detached-`WebContentsView` bounds fix (electron/electron PR #53031, closing #43257) landed on 44 and was backported to 42 / 43 late in their lives, so earlier point releases on those branches clear the CVE floor and still lack the fix — rather than carry a per-branch fix floor the release feed cannot verify, V1 admits 44.x alone, and the `browser` pane kind is buildable on nothing below it; and the Electron 40 renderer-clipboard deprecation stays enforced through §Renderer Bundle. One dependency rides the floor: Electron 44 is Node ABI 149, which no `better-sqlite3` 12.x release ships a prebuild for, so the daemon's binding moves to the 13.x Node-API line before or with the pin ([ADR-021 §Decision](../decisions/021-v1-toolchain-selection.md#decision)).

Electron has **no LTS lane**. The project team supports the latest three stable majors on an 8-week release cadence aligned with Chromium's 4-week stable channel, so a major goes end-of-support roughly every eight weeks.

**Consequence:** Plan-020 must budget two forced major-version upgrades in V1's first year after ship. Same-week patch adoption is required on security-advisory drops. This is the single largest recurring operational cost of choosing Electron and is accepted per ADR-015; Plan-020 must include release-engineering capacity for the cadence.

Sources:

- [Electron release timeline](https://www.electronjs.org/docs/latest/tutorial/electron-timelines)
- [Release schedule](https://releases.electronjs.org/schedule)
- [GitHub Security Advisories, electron/electron](https://github.com/electron/electron/security/advisories)

### Electron Fuses

Electron Fuses are build-time toggles on the shipped binary that harden the binary against misuse as a generic Node runtime or against injection vectors. The posture declared above (RunAsNode disabled, NodeOptions disabled, CLI inspect disabled, asar integrity enabled, OnlyLoadAppFromAsar enabled, cookie encryption enabled) matches the current Electron-documented production hardening recommendation and the posture VS Code, Slack, and 1Password ship with.

### Detached Service Launch

The daemon is the person's own background service, never a child of the desktop app. Main starts it with Node's `child_process.spawn` and `detached: true`, its standard streams not connected to the app and the child handle released with `unref()`, as Node's documentation requires for a detached child to keep running after its parent exits, and reaches it through its socket — on Windows, the per-user named pipe `\\.\pipe\ai-sidekicks-<user SID>` — so nothing between main and the service rides a process channel of the app's own. The service is not started with Electron's `utilityProcess.fork`: a utility-process child dies when the app quits, and a quit must leave the service running.

Measured on Electron 44.5.1 on macOS and Electron 44.1.0 on Linux: a detached child survives the app's quit, SIGTERM and SIGKILL. On Linux a logout ends the detached child unless lingering is on, as it does the per-user service. Where a detached child does not survive, the service runs through the per-user service `sidekicks daemon install` sets up. On a Windows computer whose providers live in WSL 2, a per-user logon task starts the Windows half (`sidekicks-windows-half.exe`), which starts the daemon inside the distribution with `wsl.exe --exec` and serves the same per-user named pipe. On native Windows the service outlives the app as it does everywhere else: a detached child where a measurement on a Windows machine shows one survives a quit, and the per-user service otherwise.

`daemon.hello` is the handshake. `daemon.ping` is sent after 5 s of quiet on the link, which counts as dead after 20 s of silence, and the connection state is the `daemon.status` topic on `daemon.subscribe`; no heartbeat is polled on a timer. A crash restarts the service with the backoff §Daemon Supervision Lifecycle declares.

A quit flushes the service (`daemon.flush`) and leaves the service, every run and every terminal shell running, and no surface explains it. Only Settings › Runtime's `Stop` and `Restart` end work: after the flush, a daemon that has not exited gets SIGTERM and, 2 s later, SIGKILL, because a workflow step's thread that never started keeps the daemon's process from exiting.

Sources:

- [Node.js `child_process` — `options.detached`](https://nodejs.org/api/child_process.html#optionsdetached)
- [Electron utilityProcess API](https://www.electronjs.org/docs/latest/api/utility-process) — the child-process API the service is not started with

### Native Keystore

`node-keytar` is archived by its maintainers (last release v7.9.0) and is **not** a supported dependency for new projects. The replacement this project adopts is **`@napi-rs/keyring`** 2.1.0 (MIT, 47 kB plus one native package per platform — a napi-rs Rust binding to the `keyring-rs` crate, self-described as a "100% compatible node-keytar alternative"). It supports macOS Keychain, Windows Credential Manager, and Linux Secret Service. It does **not** require libsecret on Linux (it uses `secret-service-rs`), which matters in headless CI, WSL, and Codespaces environments. It is also the replacement path used by the Microsoft Authentication Library for JS and the Azure Identity SDK.

Main keeps no entry in the operating system's keystore and holds no credential: this machine's DPoP key, the hosted account's tokens and every other secret are the background service's, each its own item in the operating system's credential store — the login keychain on macOS, Credential Manager on Windows, the Secret Service on Linux. On macOS and Linux the service opens each item through `@napi-rs/keyring`, on Linux with `{ linux: { store: "secret-service" } }` so the kernel keyring, which empties at reboot, is never used. On Windows, where that binding writes every entry at a roaming persistence, the service writes each Credential Manager entry through its Windows half at `CRED_PERSIST_LOCAL_MACHINE`, whether the service runs on Windows or in a WSL 2 distribution, so a Windows computer has one place for secrets on either side.

On Linux with no Secret Service answering, the service keeps its items in one file, `secrets.json` in its own data folder, readable only by the person (mode `0600`), as `gh` and Codex do. It never falls back silently: Settings › Runtime then shows one line under the service's own, `Secrets are kept in <path>, readable only by you, because no Secret Service is running.`, `<path>` being that file, and the line is absent on every other machine.

Sources:

- [`node-keytar` archive notice](https://github.com/atom/node-keytar) — "archived by the owner on Dec 15, 2022"
- [`@napi-rs/keyring` 2.1.0](https://www.npmjs.com/package/@napi-rs/keyring)
- [MSAL JS keytar migration issue](https://github.com/AzureAD/microsoft-authentication-library-for-js/issues/7170) — corroborating MSAL's move off keytar

### Build And Packaging

`electron-builder` **26.15.x** is the packaging tool for V1 (26.15.3, published 2026-06-09, and 26.15.7, published 2026-07-18 under the `v26` dist-tag). The channels it builds are exactly these: on macOS 13 and later (Electron 44's floor), arm64 and x64, a `.dmg` to install and a `.zip` for updates; on Windows 10 and later, x64 and arm64, an NSIS installer per user; on Linux x64 and arm64, an AppImage, and `.deb` and `.rpm` packages, which on install add the project's signed apt or dnf repository (`signed-by=/usr/share/keyrings/…`). There is no MSI, no Snap and no Flatpak: the MSI exists for an organization's deployment tools and the updater does not update it; AppImage, `.deb` and `.rpm` with the signed repository already reach every mainstream distribution; and Flathub requires building from source with no network, which `electron-builder`'s Flatpak target, built from prebuilt binaries, does not do. Electron 44 publishes no 32-bit build, so none ships, and Windows on ARM ships, its terminal sidecar included. The repository is hosted on Cloudsmith's open-source plan ("at least 50GB of artifact data + 200GB of package delivery for free", with an attribution link), because each release adds four packages of about 120 MB and GitHub Pages' 1 GB cap would fill within three releases; it keeps the last ten versions, and older packages stay on GitHub Releases. The app knows its install kind from the package type `electron-builder` records (`resources/package-type`), from `APPIMAGE`, and from the platform's own marks. Reasoning: cross-platform installer-format coverage is broader than Electron Forge's, production-proven at the scale of VS Code / 1Password / Slack, and integrates cleanly with `electron-updater` for the update path on macOS, Linux and Windows.

**Alternative considered:** Electron Forge. Forge v7.11.1 (2026-01-12) is the Electron team's officially-maintained packaging tool, and the electronjs.org docs recommend it as the default. The Forge README directs production users to v7.x. Forge wins on "closer to Electron's official cadence and less third-party surface." It loses on auto-update: Forge requires wiring `update.electronjs.org` or a custom updater, neither of which covers Linux at all.

Decision: `electron-builder` wins for this spec because cross-platform auto-update on macOS + Windows + Linux is a V1 requirement (see §Auto-Updater below). Plan-020 must pin to `electron-builder` v26.15.x minor versions and review the changelog on every bump (electron-builder is outside "official Electron support," per the Electron docs). **Electron binary materialization.** The 44.x line publishes no install script, and the change it inherits is **Electron 42.0's**, not 44's: `docs/breaking-changes.md` §Breaking API Changes (42.0) records both that `electron` "no longer downloads itself via `postinstall` script" and that `ELECTRON_SKIP_BINARY_DOWNLOAD` "is no longer supported, as its primary purpose was to prevent the `postinstall` script from running" — 41.6.1 published `postinstall: node install.js` and every manifest from 42 through 45 carries no `scripts` field at all — binary acquisition having moved into a lazy download at module scope in `npm/index.js`, which every `require('electron')` reaches. A plain `pnpm install` therefore plants the launcher shim and no `dist/`, and the first process to require Electron pays a **120–160 MB** platform download wherever it happens to run (130 MB for `darwin-arm64` at 44.5.1; 158 MB for `win32-x64`), including inside a test's own timeout. The binary is materialized at INSTALL time instead: `apps/desktop` carries an idempotent `postinstall` that runs Electron's own install entry point when the binary is absent, skips when it is present, fails closed if it is still absent afterwards, and honors `ELECTRON_SKIP_BINARY_DOWNLOAD` — the escape 42.0 dropped, reinstated by this wrapper on its own terms rather than by Electron's — so a CI leg that never launches Electron stays fast. Every consumer calls that one script rather than a second acquisition path, and the smoke harness refuses before spawning, naming the remedy, rather than timing out.

**Release files and reproducible builds:** A release publishes its files and a checksum list. Neither `electron-builder` nor Electron Forge guarantees bit-reproducible binaries out of the box, and `electron-builder` does not read `SOURCE_DATE_EPOCH` itself; V1 claims no reproducible build on any platform, and CI runs no reproducible-build job.

Sources:

- [electron-builder releases — v26.15.x line](https://github.com/electron-userland/electron-builder/releases)
- [Electron Forge v7.11.1 / v8.0.0-alpha](https://github.com/electron/forge)
- [Electron Forge overview](https://www.electronjs.org/docs/latest/tutorial/forge-overview)
- [electron.build](https://www.electron.build)
- [Electron 44.0.0 release notes](https://www.electronjs.org/blog/electron-44-0) — 32-bit builds removed, macOS 13 floor
- [Cloudsmith open-source hosting policy](https://docs.cloudsmith.com/resources/open-source-hosting-policy)
- [Debian — using third-party repositories](https://wiki.debian.org/DebianRepository/UseThirdParty) — `signed-by` pointing at a key file
- [Flathub requirements](https://docs.flathub.org/docs/for-app-authors/requirements) — built from source, no network during the build

### Auto-Updater

The app updates through `electron-updater` **6.8.9** (published 2026-06-05, part of the `electron-builder` project) as shipped, with its stock GitHub provider and nothing of the project's on top. The provider reads the update files `electron-builder` publishes to the project's GitHub Releases, and `electron-updater` enforces the SHA-512 they list on the download. The operating system's code signature decides the rest on macOS and Windows. Squirrel.Windows is explicitly unsupported and must not be used. The update block's steps:

1. **`Checked`.** The provider reads the latest release's update file. Main refuses a release whose version is not above the last one seen, and `allowDowngrade` stays off; the last version seen is kept for the life of the install in main's own updater record, `userData/updater-state.json`, which main reads and writes while the service is down and no wire writes; it is application state, never a setting, and a missing or broken record reads as the installed version.
2. **`Downloading`.** `autoDownload` is off, so the download starts on the person's press. It is a blockmap delta where the platform has one — NSIS, the macOS zip once a previous update is cached, and AppImage — and the full artifact otherwise.
3. **`Checking the signature…`.** `electron-updater` checks the downloaded file's SHA-512 against the release's update file before `update-downloaded`, and on Windows it also checks the installer's publisher (`verifyUpdateCodeSignature`).
4. **`Ready to install`.** `autoInstallOnAppQuit` is off, so on macOS Squirrel.Mac is handed the update only when the person presses `Restart to finish` (`quitAndInstall()`) and never before the check has passed; left on, `electron-updater` would feed Squirrel.Mac before any listener could refuse the file.
5. **`Restart to finish`.** The restart asks nothing and stops no session, run or terminal shell, because the service keeps them running through it. After relaunch the daemon handshake runs. Neither `electron-updater` nor Electron's built-in `autoUpdater` rolls back, and the app adds no rollback of its own: a bad release is fixed by the next one.

Any failed check discards the file and draws `The update could not be verified and was not installed.` with `Try again`. A check is two HTTPS requests of a few kilobytes each, every four hours and at startup; the first release measures a check's bytes and a delta's size against the full artifact. The update file never sets a staged rollout (`stagingPercentage`), which exists to protect a population of users. An install a package manager put there is that package manager's to update: a `.deb` or `.rpm` install never runs `electron-updater`'s own Debian or RPM updater, which installs with `--allow-unauthenticated` or `--nogpgcheck` and so bypasses the repository's signature. The service updates on its own path, from Settings › Runtime and `sidekicks self-update`, checking the downloaded program against the SHA-256 the release's manifest lists for it, and nothing more.

**Alternative (not chosen):** Electron's built-in `autoUpdater` module uses Squirrel.Mac on macOS and Squirrel.Windows (or MSIX updater, auto-detected) on Windows; it has **no built-in Linux support**. The hosted `update.electronjs.org` service is restricted to public GitHub repos, macOS (DMG) + Windows (NSIS) only, and requires signed macOS builds. Neither covers V1's Linux requirement.

Sources:

- [electron-updater v6.8.9 release](https://github.com/electron-userland/electron-builder/releases)
- [electron-updater documentation](https://www.electron.build/auto-update)
- [Electron built-in autoUpdater API](https://www.electronjs.org/docs/latest/api/auto-updater)
- [update.electronjs.org](https://github.com/electron/update.electronjs.org)
- [electron-updater source at `electron-updater@6.8.9`](https://github.com/electron-userland/electron-builder/tree/electron-updater%406.8.9/packages/electron-updater/src) — the GitHub provider, the hash check on the download, the publisher check on Windows, the blockmap deltas per platform, the Debian and RPM updaters

### Code Signing And Notarization

**CA/Browser Forum Ballot CSC-31 (effective 2026-03-01, per [CSCBR v3.10.0 §6.3.2](https://cabforum.org/uploads/CA-Browser-Forum-CSCBR-3.10.0.pdf)):** All publicly-trusted code-signing certificates issued on or after 2026-03-01 have a maximum validity of **460 days** (≈14 months). EV and non-EV certs are both affected uniformly. Hardware-token holders must physically rekey every ≤14 months (≤460 days) (literal "15 months" can equal 456–465 days depending on month lengths and may exceed the cap).

#### macOS

- **Identity:** until the Apple Developer ID Application certificate exists ([BL-108](../backlog.md#bl-108-plan-021-windows--macos-signing-procurement-evidence)), every macOS build is signed with the project's own self-signed code-signing identity, held as a release secret, with the same bundle identifier on every build. Squirrel.Mac requires an update to satisfy the running app's designated requirement: a self-signed identity's requirement names its certificate, so every later build signed with it meets it and one-press update works, where an ad-hoc signature's requirement is one build's `cdhash` and an unsigned app cannot update at all. The privacy permissions, which follow the designated requirement, stay granted across updates. The first launch still meets Gatekeeper's prompt for an app that is not notarized, which the person clears once; that prompt, and nothing else in the update path, waits on the certificate. Once it exists, the identity is the Developer ID Application certificate, issued under the $99/year Apple Developer Program membership.
- **Process:** Hardened runtime enabled on every build; once the Apple certificate exists, Apple notarization via `xcrun notarytool` (the deprecated `altool` command must not be used) + `xcrun stapler` to attach the notarization ticket to the artifact so it works offline.
- **Entitlements:** The hardened runtime must declare only what the app needs. V8/JIT commonly requires `com.apple.security.cs.allow-unsigned-executable-memory` and `com.apple.security.cs.allow-jit`; dynamic loading of native modules may require `com.apple.security.cs.allow-dyld-environment-variables`.
- **Operational risk:** Apple's notarization queue can take 24–120+ hours, per developer-forum reports. Plan-020's release pipeline must include **timeout + retry** logic rather than synchronous blocking on notarization.

#### Windows

- **Path:** [SignPath Foundation](https://signpath.org)'s free code signing for open-source projects signs the Windows installer and `sidekicks-windows-half.exe`. The certificate is issued to SignPath Foundation, so Windows names `SignPath Foundation` as the publisher rather than AI Sidekicks. Eligibility, per SignPath's terms: an OSI license without commercial dual-licensing for any component, no proprietary components, active maintenance, an existing release, MFA on the maintainers' GitHub accounts, and a published code-signing policy on the project site; Microsoft's code-signing options page names it as the route for qualifying open-source projects. A finding that the project is not eligible goes back to the project's owner for a decision, and the signing path never changes silently. The signature removes SmartScreen's hard block on an unsigned file and keeps the one-press NSIS update path, and the updater checks each downloaded installer's publisher (§Auto-Updater). There is no Azure Artifact Signing account and no bought OV or EV certificate. Until that signing is in place ([BL-108](../backlog.md#bl-108-plan-021-windows--macos-signing-procurement-evidence)), Windows builds publish unsigned: SmartScreen's prompt is cleared once with `Run anyway`, and the updater's publisher check starts with the first signed build.
- **Smart App Control reality:** Valid code signing is **not sufficient** for Smart App Control to allow a binary. SAC evaluates cert trust chain _and_ cloud reputation (Intelligent Security Graph). New binaries with low distribution get blocked until reputation builds, even with a valid signature. First-launch UX on Windows 11 will be rough until reputation accumulates; the mitigation is `Run anyway` guidance in the release notes and the README's install section, because the prompt shows before the app can run.
- **Windows 10 EOL:** Windows 10 reached end-of-support on 2025-10-14. V1 supports Windows 10 and 11, x64 and arm64. Because Windows 10 is EOL, this spec accepts the security-posture implication of shipping to an EOL OS for V1; whether a later release drops Windows 10 is §Open Questions'.

#### Linux

There is no universal code-signing model for Linux; each format the project ships carries its own:

- **`.AppImage`** — optional embedded GPG signature (rarely verified by users); this project additionally publishes the artifact hash alongside the download in the release's checksum list (§Build And Packaging).
- **`.deb` / `.rpm`** — the project signs neither package itself: Cloudsmith's per-repository key signs each RPM on upload and the apt and dnf repository metadata, and a `.deb` is checked through apt's signed Release file. On install each package adds the project's signed apt or dnf repository on Cloudsmith, its key file under `/usr/share/keyrings/` and named by `signed-by`, as Debian's guidance for third-party repositories asks, so every later version arrives through the package manager with the repository's signature checked.
- **Snap and Flatpak** — none ships (§Build And Packaging).

`electron-builder` supports all of the above. No automatic signing beyond what each format specifies.

Sources:

- [CA/Browser Forum CSC-31 ballot](https://cabforum.org/working-groups/code-signing/requirements/)
- [SignPath Foundation terms](https://signpath.org/terms) — eligibility; the certificate is issued to SignPath Foundation
- [Microsoft Learn — code-signing options](https://learn.microsoft.com/en-us/windows/apps/package-and-deploy/code-signing-options) — SmartScreen on an unsigned file; SignPath Foundation for open-source projects
- [Squirrel.Mac `SQRLCodeSignature.m`](https://github.com/Squirrel/Squirrel.Mac/blob/8d808803bc89ec0e2aa1450474856dfee3b00c6b/Squirrel/SQRLCodeSignature.m) — an update must satisfy the running app's designated requirement
- [Electron code-signing tutorial](https://www.electronjs.org/docs/latest/tutorial/code-signing) — `autoUpdater` without a consistent signature
- [Apple Developer ID](https://developer.apple.com/developer-id/)
- [Apple Developer forum — notarization queue delays](https://developer.apple.com/forums/thread/813441)
- [Windows 10 lifecycle](https://learn.microsoft.com/en-us/lifecycle/products/windows-10-home-and-pro)

### Crash Reporting

Electron's built-in `crashReporter` module uses **Crashpad** (not Breakpad as older docs suggest) and covers the main process, the renderers and every other process the app starts.

Crash reports stay on the machine that crashed. Crashpad writes each crash to that machine only (`crashReporter.start({ uploadToServer: false })`, which keeps reports on disk in the app's own data folder), and the service turns each one into a report there: the process type, the app and service versions, the platform, and the crashing thread's stack as module names and offsets, symbolicated where symbols are at hand — Electron's own from `symbols.electronjs.org`, the product's from its release — with file paths cut to their file name and session ids replaced by stable hashes. A native minidump holds process heap memory, which for main holds the daemon session token, and a pre-upload scrubber cannot reach a minidump's bytes, so the raw minidump never leaves the machine; it is deleted with the diagnostic logs past `Keep diagnostic logs for`.

`Keep crash reports`, under the update block on Settings › General, is the one control for it: on by default, under `Reports stay on this machine, with personal data stripped.`, and off, no report is kept. Nothing sends a report anywhere. Any of the person's linked devices reads a machine's reports through Remote Control (`daemon.crashList`), the relay carrying the read and storing nothing, and `sidekicks crash list` reads them from the command line.

Symbolication runs once per crash, with symbol files fetched only for the modules in the stack, cached in the service's folder and aged out with the diagnostic logs. The parser is `minidump-stackwalk` 0.27.0 (MIT, from rust-minidump), measured before it is adopted. Declined: a crash service on an account the person holds, which adds a third party; a project-run sink that collects every install's reports, which serves other people; Sentry self-hosted, which needs 16 GB of memory and is FSL-licensed; and `mini-breakpad-server`, which is archived.

Sources:

- [Electron `crashReporter` API (Crashpad)](https://www.electronjs.org/docs/latest/api/crash-reporter) — `uploadToServer: false` keeps reports on disk
- [Electron symbol server](https://www.electronjs.org/docs/latest/development/debugging-with-symbol-server)
- [rust-minidump `minidump-stackwalk`](https://github.com/rust-minidump/rust-minidump/tree/main/minidump-stackwalk)
- [Sentry self-hosted requirements](https://develop.sentry.dev/self-hosted/)

### Console Libraries

_Every console axis names the libraries considered, the evidence, and the verdict — ADOPT · ADOPT-with-constraints · OWN-BUILD · AVOID — under one rule: a well-maintained library that cuts code we would otherwise write is adopted only where it leaves the visuals, the product requirements, speed, and memory untouched. Citations per axis are in §References §Console Design Sources. Versions are exact-pinned in `apps/desktop/package.json`; a bump re-reads the row._

| Axis | Verdict | Constraints and reason |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Timeline virtualization | ADOPT-with-constraints `@tanstack/react-virtual` 3.14.13 | Headless, 7.6 kB gzip, the only candidate whose scroller we own (`getScrollElement`, `scrollToFn`, `observeElementOffset`); no smooth `scrollToIndex`, stable keys, direct DOM updates. **The end anchor and the follow-on-append are the library's, not ours.** The pinned adapter resolves `@tanstack/virtual-core` 3.17.11, which carries `anchorTo`, `followOnAppend`, `scrollEndThreshold` and `scrollToEnd` (read in the installed package), so the list takes them as they stand and sets `overflow-anchor: none` while it is following, turning the browser's own scroll anchoring off, a virtualizer's `transform` and `position` writes suppressing it anyway. The sub-row reading anchor and the window cap stay own-build (no library has a sub-row reading anchor). Chromium caps element height at 33,554,431 px, so the window cap is a ceiling, not a nicety. **What arrives, and how much of it is drawn again.** The stream reaches the renderer in the daemon's frames ([Spec-006 §Required Behavior](006-local-ipc-and-daemon-control.md#required-behavior) sets their size and cadence), each change carrying its own cursor, and the daemon never waits for a screen that has fallen behind: it drops, marks the gap on the next frame, and the renderer repairs from the record by cursor, taking a snapshot read past the gap ([Spec-006 §Required Behavior](./006-local-ipc-and-daemon-control.md#required-behavior)). A row grows at its tail, so the tail block is the unit of change while the list follows — settled text is never rendered again and one row is re-measured. The reveal runs at the rate the words arrive, with two gears for a backlog, both under the frame budget, and reduced motion reaches the reveal engine itself rather than stopping at the stylesheet (§Meridian, the design language). AVOID virtua (internal scroll writes), react-virtuoso (not headless), react-window, react-virtualized. |
| Streaming markdown | ADOPT micromark + `mdast-util-from-markdown` with GFM; ADOPT-with-constraints remend (tail only); OWN-BUILD the delta-fed block segmenter, the mdast-to-React mapper, the reveal router, and the footnote registry | Whole-message re-parse per token is quadratic; settled blocks parse once with a two-block settle lag; `html` nodes render as literal text, so no sanitizer is on the path. Bold, italic, strikethrough, subscript and superscript render in a reply; a backslash escape stays the literal character it escapes; a single tilde is never read as strikethrough, so `~240MB` or a path under `~` stays as written; neither a colon-wrapped shortcode nor a bare email address is expanded; a footnote mark is a bracketed number that presses, its definition rendered at the foot of that reply and the press moving the page to it, with no card and no hover preview. Markup in a reply is shown as the text it is: a tag the agent wrote reads as its angle brackets on the screen. An address written plainly in a reply, with or without angle brackets, is a link; an address inside inline code or a code fence is not, so a URL in a command stays part of the command. A reply that opens with a brace or a bracket and nothing before it is drawn as an indented code fence from its first characters, the decision never changing under the reader, and prose after it renders as prose. Lists render with their markers and their nesting ladders — disc, circle, square for bullets; numbers, then letters, then roman for ordered lists — and with the numbers the agent actually wrote; definition lists render too, and every marker takes the text's own color, never the accent. A quote a provider marked as a note, a tip, an important point, a warning or a caution is drawn as the plain hairline-ruled quote with the marker's word as its bold title and nothing else, the marker itself never showing as literal text. A code block names its language in one quiet word at its corner beside its own `Copy`, the word absent on a fence that names none, with no line numbers; that `Copy`, on every block of code in a reply, a plan and a note, copies that block alone and reads `Copied` for a moment. A task-list item keeps its marker as the text the agent wrote, `[x]` or `[ ]`, under an ordinary bullet, and never ticks, because nothing on this screen writes to a file. A reply carries none of the four document controls — no switch between the rendered reply and its source, no link on a heading, no contents list, and no way to sort a table — and markdown is rendered only where it is read, in the conversation, an agent's reply, a note and a plan, never a file as a document. AVOID react-markdown, the rehype stack, markdown-it, markdown-to-jsx, and every Tailwind-styled streaming renderer (styling identity leakage, raw HTML rendering, whole-message re-lex). |
| Syntax highlighting | ADOPT `shiki` 4.4.3 **in the daemon**, as the one colorer for the whole product ([ADR-033](../decisions/033-one-syntax-colorer-in-the-daemon.md)); the renderer holds no instance and no Worker colors anything | The daemon colors a file once with the JavaScript engine and lazy grammars, caches the spans against what the file holds, and hands them out through `highlight.read`; every surface paints the spans it is handed as classes that read the theme's own code tokens, never as a color written on the element, so a theme or scheme switch repaints the same spans in place and colors nothing twice. A language nobody recognizes stays plain with no round trip. A block still arriving keeps the colors it already has, and only the line still arriving is drawn plain until it lands; a block redrawn after a switch starts from the colors it had, so it never flashes back to plain. Never the preset bundles, and never the WebAssembly engine, whose linear memory grows to about 29 MB and is never reclaimed. The package moves from the desktop app to the daemon in the same change, at the same pin. AVOID react-shiki, highlight.js, the prism family, sugar-high, starry-night. |
| Math and diagrams | ADOPT-with-constraints KaTeX (lazy, settled blocks only, `trust: false`, MathML output first); ADOPT-with-constraints `mermaid` 11.17.2 (MIT), lazy and `securityLevel: 'strict'` | No direct DOMPurify dependency. A dollar sign that is a price or a shell variable is left as text; the source shows in the block's own box until the formula typesets; and each block's height is kept so scrolling back never collapses and regrows the page. A diagram block draws itself with no press asked for: the picture is made as soon as the block has settled, its source shows in its place while the picture is being made and stays one press away afterwards, and a block that cannot be drawn says so and why — the parser's own reason — above that source rather than showing the bare source. The library is loaded on its own chunk at the first diagram, which is why §Budgets lists diagrams among the lazy chunks: it is 84.0 MB unpacked. It is initialized with the base theme and its theme variables resolved from the Meridian tokens, with its own error rendering suppressed so the block's line is the one a person reads; because its color maths cannot parse the perceptual and custom-property color forms the tokens are written in, each token is resolved to concrete 8-bit color first.
